Ethics

God, mind and freedom proved like geometry: definitions, axioms, and two hundred and fifty-nine propositions

by Baruch Spinoza

1677 2026

Part One: Concerning God

Definitions, Axioms, Propositions 1 to 17

Definitions

1. By self-caused I mean that whose essence involves existence: that whose nature cannot be conceived except as existing.

2. A thing is called finite after its kind when it can be limited by another thing of the same nature. A body, for instance, is called finite because we can always conceive a greater body. In the same way a thought is limited by another thought. But a body is not limited by a thought, nor a thought by a body.

3. By substance I mean that which is in itself and is conceived through itself: that whose conception can be formed without needing the conception of anything else.

4. By attribute I mean that which the intellect perceives as constituting the essence of substance.

5. By mode I mean the modifications of substance: that which exists in, and is conceived through, something other than itself.

Footnote: "Affectiones"

6. By God I mean a being absolutely infinite—that is, a substance consisting of infinite attributes, each of which expresses eternal and infinite essence.

Explanation. I say absolutely infinite, not infinite after its kind. Of a thing infinite only after its kind, infinite attributes may be denied; but that which is absolutely infinite contains in its essence everything that expresses reality, and involves no negation.

7. A thing is called free when it exists solely by the necessity of its own nature and its action is determined by itself alone. A thing is called necessary, or rather constrained, when something outside it determines it to exist and act in a fixed and definite way.

8. By eternity I mean existence itself, insofar as it is conceived to follow necessarily from the definition of the eternal thing alone.

Explanation. Existence of this kind is conceived as an eternal truth, like the essence of a thing, and so cannot be explained by duration or time—even though duration may be conceived as having no beginning and no end.

Axioms

1. Everything that exists, exists either in itself or in something else.

2. That which cannot be conceived through anything else must be conceived through itself.

3. From a given definite cause an effect necessarily follows; and conversely, if there is no definite cause, no effect can follow.

4. Knowledge of an effect depends on, and involves, knowledge of the cause.

5. Things that have nothing in common cannot be understood one through the other: the conception of one does not involve the conception of the other.

6. A true idea must agree with the thing it is an idea of.

7. If a thing can be conceived as not existing, its essence does not involve existence.

Proposition 1

Substance is by nature prior to its modifications.

Proof. This is clear from Definition 3 of this Part and Definition 5 of this Part.

Proposition 2

Two substances with different attributes have nothing in common.

Proof. Also evident from Definition 3 of this Part. Each must exist in itself and be conceived through itself; in other words, the conception of one does not imply the conception of the other.

Proposition 3

Things that have nothing in common cannot be one the cause of the other.

Proof. If they have nothing in common, then one cannot be understood by means of the other (Axiom 5 of this Part), and therefore one cannot be the cause of the other (Axiom 4 of this Part). Q.E.D.

Proposition 4

Two or more distinct things are distinguished from one another either by a difference in the attributes of their substances or by a difference in their modifications.

Proof. Everything that exists, exists either in itself or in something else (Axiom 1 of this Part)—that is (by Definition 3 of this Part; Definition 5 of this Part), there is nothing outside the understanding except substance and its modifications. So there is nothing besides the understanding by which several things could be distinguished from one another except substances, or, what comes to the same thing (see Axiom 4 of this Part), their attributes and modifications. Q.E.D.

Proposition 5

There cannot exist in the universe two or more substances having the same nature or attribute.

Proof. If there were several distinct substances, they would have to be distinguished from one another either by a difference in their attributes or by a difference in their modifications (Proposition 4 of this Part). If only by their attributes, then it must be granted that there cannot be more than one substance with any given attribute. If by their modifications—since substance is by nature prior to its modifications (Proposition 1 of this Part)—then, setting the modifications aside and considering substance in itself, that is, truly (Definition 3 of this Part; Definition 6 of this Part), one substance cannot be conceived as different from another—that is (by Proposition 4 of this Part), there cannot be several substances, but one substance only. Q.E.D.

Proposition 6

One substance cannot be produced by another substance.

Proof. There cannot be in the universe two substances with the same attribute, that is, two substances with anything in common (Proposition 2 of this Part), and therefore (Proposition 3 of this Part) one cannot be the cause of the other, nor can one be produced by the other. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows that a substance cannot be produced by anything outside itself. For in the universe there is nothing but substances and their modifications (as appears from Axiom 1 of this Part; Definition 3 of this Part; Definition 5 of this Part). Now (by Proposition 6 of this Part) substance cannot be produced by another substance; therefore it cannot be produced by anything outside itself. Q.E.D. This is shown still more readily by the absurdity of the opposite. For if substance were produced by an external cause, knowledge of it would depend on knowledge of that cause (Axiom 4 of this Part), and (by Definition 3 of this Part) it would not then be substance at all.

Proposition 7

Existence belongs to the nature of substance.

Proof. Substance cannot be produced by anything outside itself (Proposition 6 of this Part, Corollary), so it must be its own cause—that is, its essence necessarily involves existence, or existence belongs to its nature.

Proposition 8

Every substance is necessarily infinite.

Proof. There can be only one substance with any given attribute, and existence follows from its nature (Proposition 7 of this Part); its nature therefore involves existence, either as finite or as infinite. It does not exist as finite, for (by Definition 2 of this Part) it would then be limited by something else of the same kind, which would also necessarily exist (Proposition 7 of this Part), and there would be two substances with the same attribute, which is absurd (Proposition 5 of this Part). It therefore exists as infinite. Q.E.D.

Note 1. Since finite existence involves a partial negation, and infinite existence is the absolute affirmation of a thing's nature, it follows (solely from Proposition 7 of this Part) that every substance is necessarily infinite.

Note 2. No doubt those who think loosely about things, and are not used to knowing them through their first causes, will find the demonstration of Proposition 7 of this Part hard to follow. Such people make no distinction between the modifications of substances and the substances themselves, and do not know how things are produced; so they attribute to substances the beginning they observe in natural objects. People ignorant of true causes confuse everything—they think trees might talk as well as men, that men might be formed from stones as easily as from seed, and imagine that any form might change into any other. In the same way, those who confuse the two natures, divine and human, readily attribute human passions to God, especially while they do not know how passions arise in the mind. But if people would only consider the nature of substance, they would have no doubt about the truth of Proposition 7 of this Part. In fact this proposition would be a universal axiom and counted a truism. For by substance they would understand that which is in itself and is conceived through itself—that is, something whose conception does not require the conception of anything else—whereas modifications exist in something outside themselves, and are conceived by means of a conception of the thing they exist in. This is why we can have true ideas of modifications that do not exist: although they may have no actual existence apart from the intellect conceiving them, their essence is so bound up in something outside themselves that they can be conceived through it. Substances, on the other hand, can have no truth outside the intellect except their existence, because they are conceived through themselves. So for a person to say that he has a clear and distinct—that is, a true—idea of a substance, but is not sure whether that substance exists, would be the same as saying that he had a true idea but was not sure whether it was false (a little thought will make this plain); and for anyone to claim that substance is created would be to say that a false idea is true—the height of absurdity. It must therefore be admitted that the existence of substance, like its essence, is an eternal truth. And from this we can conclude by another line of reasoning that there is only one such substance. I think it worth doing at once; and to proceed in order, we must first lay down:

1. The true definition of a thing neither involves nor expresses anything beyond the nature of the thing defined. From which it follows:

2. No definition implies or expresses any particular number of individuals, since it expresses nothing beyond the nature of the thing defined. The definition of a triangle, for instance, expresses nothing beyond the nature of a triangle: it does not imply any fixed number of triangles.

3. For each existing thing there must necessarily be a cause why it exists.

4. This cause of existence must either be contained in the nature and definition of the thing, or lie outside it.

It follows that if a given number of individual things exist in nature, there must be a cause why exactly that number exists, neither more nor fewer. For example, if twenty men exist in the universe (for simplicity I will suppose them existing at the same time, with no predecessors), and we want to account for the existence of these twenty, it will not be enough to give the cause of human existence in general; we must also show why there are exactly twenty, neither more nor fewer, since a cause must be assigned for the existence of each one. Now this cause cannot lie in the nature of man, for the true definition of man involves no consideration of the number twenty. So the cause of the existence of these twenty men, and therefore of each of them, must be sought outside each individual. Hence we may lay down the absolute rule that everything which can consist of several individuals must have an external cause. And since it has already been shown that existence belongs to the nature of substance, existence must be included in its definition, and must be deducible from that definition alone. But from its definition (Proposition 2 of this Part, Note; Proposition 3 of this Part, Note) we cannot infer the existence of several substances; therefore there is only one substance of any given nature. Q.E.D.

Proposition 9

The more reality or being a thing has, the more attributes it has (Definition 4 of this Part).

Proposition 10

Each attribute of the one substance must be conceived through itself.

Proof. An attribute is what the intellect perceives of substance as constituting its essence (Definition 4 of this Part), and therefore must be conceived through itself (Definition 3 of this Part). Q.E.D.

Note. It is clear from this that although two attributes are in fact conceived as distinct—that is, one without the help of the other—we cannot conclude from that that they constitute two entities, or two different substances. For it is the nature of substance that each of its attributes is conceived through itself, since all the attributes it has have always existed in it together, and none could have been produced by any other; each simply expresses the reality or being of substance. So it is far from absurd to ascribe several attributes to one substance. Nothing in nature is clearer than that every entity must be conceived under some attribute, and that its reality or being is in proportion to the number of its attributes expressing necessity, or eternity and infinity. It is therefore perfectly clear that an absolutely infinite being must be defined as consisting of infinite attributes, each of which expresses a certain eternal and infinite essence.

If anyone now asks by what sign he can tell one substance from another, let him read the following propositions, which show that there is only one substance in the universe, and that it is absolutely infinite. He would be looking for such a sign in vain.

Proposition 11

God, or substance consisting of infinite attributes, each of which expresses eternal and infinite essence, necessarily exists.

Proof. Deny it if you can, and conceive that God does not exist. Then his essence does not involve existence. But that (Proposition 7 of this Part) is absurd. Therefore God necessarily exists.

Another proof. For everything whatever a cause or reason must be assigned, either for its existence or for its non-existence. If a triangle exists, a reason or cause must be given for its existence; if it does not exist, a cause must also be given, one that prevents it from existing or annuls its existence. This reason or cause must either be contained in the nature of the thing or lie outside it. The reason for the non-existence of a square circle, for instance, is indicated in its own nature: it would involve a contradiction. And the existence of substance likewise follows from its nature alone, since its nature involves existence (see Proposition 7 of this Part).

But the reason why a triangle or a circle exists does not follow from the nature of those figures; it follows from the order of nature as a whole, in extension. From that order it must follow either that a triangle necessarily exists or that it is impossible for it to exist. So much is self-evident. And it follows from all this that a thing necessarily exists if there is no cause or reason to prevent it.

If, then, no cause or reason can be given that prevents God from existing, or that destroys his existence, we must conclude that he necessarily does exist. And if such a reason or cause could be given, it would have to be drawn either from God's own nature or from outside it—that is, from another substance of another nature. For if it were of the same nature, that would be to admit that God exists. But a substance of another nature could have nothing in common with God (by Proposition 2 of this Part), and so could neither cause nor destroy his existence.

Since, then, a reason or cause that would annul the divine existence cannot be drawn from anything outside the divine nature, it must, if God does not exist, be drawn from God's own nature, which would involve a contradiction. To make such a claim about a being absolutely infinite and supremely perfect is absurd. Therefore neither within God's nature nor outside it can any cause or reason be assigned that would annul his existence. Therefore God necessarily exists. Q.E.D.

Another proof. The power of not existing is a lack of power, and conversely the power of existing is a power, as is obvious. If, then, the only things that necessarily exist are finite beings, finite beings are more powerful than a being absolutely infinite, which is plainly absurd. So either nothing exists, or a being absolutely infinite necessarily exists as well. Now we do exist, either in ourselves or in something else that necessarily exists (see Axiom 1 of this Part; Proposition 7 of this Part). Therefore a being absolutely infinite—that is, God (Definition 6 of this Part)—necessarily exists. Q.E.D.

Note. In this last proof I deliberately showed God's existence a posteriori, so that the proof would be easier to follow, not because God's existence does not follow a priori from the same premises. For since the power of existing is a power, it follows that the more reality there is in a thing's nature, the more strength it has for existing. So a being absolutely infinite, such as God, has from himself an absolutely infinite power of existing, and therefore absolutely exists. Perhaps many will not see the force of this proof, being accustomed to consider only things that arise from external causes. Of such things they observe that those which come quickly into existence also quickly disappear, whereas they regard as harder to bring about—that is, not so easily brought into existence—those they conceive as more complicated.

To clear up this misconception I need not discuss how much truth there is in the proverb "what comes quickly, goes quickly," nor whether, from the point of view of nature as a whole, all things are equally easy. I need only point out that I am not speaking here of things that come about through causes outside themselves, but of substances, which (by Proposition 6 of this Part) cannot be produced by any external cause. Things produced by external causes, whether they have many parts or few, owe whatever perfection or reality they possess entirely to the power of that external cause; their existence arises from the perfection of their cause, not from their own. But whatever perfection substance possesses is due to no external cause, so the existence of substance must arise from its own nature alone, which is nothing but its essence. The perfection of a thing, then, does not annul its existence but asserts it; imperfection does annul it. So we can be more certain of the existence of a being absolutely infinite or perfect—that is, of God—than of the existence of anything else. For since his essence excludes all imperfection and involves absolute perfection, every ground for doubting his existence is removed, and we are given the utmost certainty on the question. This, I think, will be clear to any reasonably attentive reader.

Proposition 12

No attribute of substance can be truly conceived from which it would follow that substance can be divided.

Proof. The parts into which substance so conceived would be divided will either keep the nature of substance or they will not. If the former, then (by Proposition 8 of this Part) each part will be infinite, and (by Proposition 6 of this Part) self-caused, and (by Proposition 5 of this Part) must consist of a different attribute—so that several substances could be formed out of one, which (by Proposition 6 of this Part) is absurd. Moreover, the parts (by Proposition 2 of this Part) would have nothing in common with their whole, and the whole (by Definition 4 of this Part; Proposition 10 of this Part) could exist and be conceived without its parts, which everyone will admit is absurd. If we take the second alternative—that the parts will not keep the nature of substance—then, if the whole substance were divided into equal parts, it would lose the nature of substance and cease to exist, which (by Proposition 7 of this Part) is absurd.

Proposition 13

Substance absolutely infinite is indivisible.

Proof. If it could be divided, the parts into which it was divided would either keep the nature of absolutely infinite substance or not. If the former, we should have several substances of the same nature, which (by Proposition 5 of this Part) is absurd. If the latter, then (by Proposition 7 of this Part) substance absolutely infinite could cease to exist, which (by Proposition 11 of this Part) is also absurd.

Corollary. It follows that no substance, and consequently no extended substance, insofar as it is substance, is divisible.

Note. The indivisibility of substance can be understood more easily like this. The nature of substance can only be conceived as infinite, and by a part of substance nothing can be understood but finite substance, which (by Proposition 8 of this Part) involves an obvious contradiction.

Proposition 14

Besides God no substance can exist or be conceived.

Proof. God is a being absolutely infinite, of whom no attribute expressing the essence of substance can be denied (by Definition 6 of this Part), and he necessarily exists (by Proposition 11 of this Part). If any substance besides God existed, it would have to be explained by some attribute of God, and then two substances with the same attribute would exist, which (by Proposition 5 of this Part) is absurd. Therefore no substance besides God can exist, or consequently be conceived. And if it could be conceived, it would have to be conceived as existing; but that (by the first part of this proof) is absurd. Therefore besides God no substance can exist or be conceived. Q.E.D.

Corollary 1. It follows clearly, first, that God is one: that is (by Definition 6 of this Part), there is only one substance in the universe, and it is absolutely infinite, as we have already indicated (in Proposition 10 of this Part, Note).

Corollary 2. It follows, second, that extension and thought are either attributes of God or (by Axiom 1 of this Part) modifications of the attributes of God.

Proposition 15

Whatsoever is, is in God, and without God nothing can be, or be conceived.

Proof. Besides God no substance exists or can be conceived (by Proposition 14 of this Part), that is (by Definition 3 of this Part), nothing that is in itself and is conceived through itself. But modes (by Definition 5 of this Part) can neither be nor be conceived without substance, so they can only be in the divine nature and can only be conceived through it. And substances and modes are the whole of what exists (by Axiom 1 of this Part). Therefore without God nothing can be, or be conceived. Q.E.D.

Note. Some assert that God, like a man, consists of body and mind and is subject to passions. How far such people have strayed from the truth is clear enough from what has been said. But I pass them over. For everyone who has reflected at all on the divine nature denies that God has a body, and finds excellent proof of it in the fact that by body we understand a definite quantity, so long, so broad, so deep, bounded by a certain shape—and it is the height of absurdity to say such a thing of God, a being absolutely infinite. But meanwhile, in the other arguments they use to prove their point, they show that they think of corporeal or extended substance as wholly apart from the divine nature, and say it was created by God. By what power it could have been created they have no idea; which shows plainly that they do not know the meaning of their own words. I have proved clearly enough, at least in my own judgment (Proposition 6 of this Part, Corollary, Note 2; Proposition 8 of this Part, Corollary), that no substance can be produced or created by anything other than itself. And I showed (in Proposition 14 of this Part) that besides God no substance can exist or be conceived. From which we concluded that extended substance is one of the infinite attributes of God. But to explain this more fully, I will refute my opponents' arguments, which all start from the following points.

Extended substance, insofar as it is substance, consists (they think) of parts; and so they deny that it can be infinite, or consequently that it can belong to God. They illustrate this with many examples, of which I will take one or two. If extended substance is infinite, they say, conceive it divided into two parts. Each part will be either finite or infinite. If finite, then an infinite is composed of two finite parts, which is absurd. If infinite, then one infinite is twice as large as another infinite, which is also absurd.

Again, if an infinite line is measured out in feet, it will consist of an infinite number of such parts; and it would equally consist of an infinite number of parts if each part measured only an inch—so one infinity would be twelve times as great as another.

Lastly, if from a single point two diverging lines are drawn, at first a definite distance apart but produced to infinity, it is certain that the distance between them will keep increasing until at length it changes from definite to indefinable. Since these absurdities follow, they say, from considering quantity as infinite, the conclusion is drawn that extended substance must be finite and cannot belong to the nature of God.

The second argument is also drawn from God's supreme perfection. God, they say, being a supremely perfect being, cannot be passive; but extended substance, insofar as it is divisible, is passive. It follows that extended substance does not belong to the essence of God.

Such are the arguments I find on the subject in writers who use them to prove that extended substance is unworthy of the divine nature and cannot possibly belong to it. But an attentive reader will see that I have already answered them, since all their arguments rest on the assumption that extended substance is composed of parts, and I have shown that assumption to be absurd (Proposition 12 of this Part, Corollary; Proposition 13 of this Part). Moreover, anyone who reflects will see that all these absurdities—if absurdities they are, which I am not now discussing—do not follow at all from the notion of an infinite quantity, but only from the notion that an infinite quantity is measurable and composed of finite parts. The only fair conclusion to draw, therefore, is that infinite quantity is not measurable and cannot be composed of finite parts. Which is exactly what we have already proved (in Proposition 12 of this Part). So the weapon they aimed at us has in fact recoiled on themselves. If they still insist on concluding from their own absurdity that extended substance must be finite, they are behaving like a man who asserts that circles have the properties of squares, finds himself landed in absurdities, and then denies that circles have a centre from which all lines drawn to the circumference are equal. For they take extended substance, which can only be conceived as infinite, one and indivisible (Proposition 8 of this Part; Proposition 5 of this Part; Proposition 12 of this Part), and then assert, in order to prove it finite, that it is composed of finite parts and can be multiplied and divided.

Others, likewise, after asserting that a line is composed of points, can produce plenty of arguments to prove that a line cannot be infinitely divided. It is no less absurd to assert that extended substance is made up of bodies or parts than it would be to assert that a solid is made up of surfaces, a surface of lines, and a line of points. This must be admitted by everyone who knows clear reason to be infallible, and most of all by those who deny that a vacuum is possible. For if extended substance could be divided so that its parts were really separate, why should one part not be destroyed while the others stayed joined together as before? And why should they all be fitted into one another so as to leave no vacuum? Surely, of things really distinct from one another, one can exist without the other and remain in its original condition. Since, then, there is no vacuum in nature (of which more later), and all the parts must come together to prevent one, it follows that the parts cannot really be distinguished, and that extended substance, insofar as it is substance, cannot be divided.

If anyone asks me the further question why we are naturally so prone to divide quantity, I answer that we conceive quantity in two ways: abstractly and superficially, as we imagine it, or as substance, as we conceive it by the intellect alone. If we regard quantity as it is represented in our imagination, which we do often and more easily, we shall find it finite, divisible and made up of parts. But if we regard it as it is represented in our intellect, and conceive it as substance—which is very difficult to do—we shall find, as I have sufficiently proved, that it is infinite, one and indivisible. This will be plain enough to anyone who distinguishes between the intellect and the imagination, especially if he remembers that matter is everywhere the same, and that its parts are distinguishable only insofar as we conceive matter as modified in different ways—so that its parts are distinguished not really but modally. Water, for instance, insofar as it is water, we conceive to be divided, and its parts to be separated one from another; but not insofar as it is extended substance, for from that point of view it is neither separated nor divisible. Again, water, insofar as it is water, is produced and destroyed; but insofar as it is substance, it is neither produced nor destroyed.

I think I have now answered the second argument as well; it rests on the same assumption as the first, that matter, insofar as it is substance, is divisible and composed of parts. And even if it were, I do not see why it should be thought unworthy of the divine nature, since besides God (by Proposition 14 of this Part) there is no substance from which it could receive its modifications. All things, I repeat, are in God; and everything that comes to pass comes to pass solely through the laws of God's infinite nature, and follows (as I shall shortly show) from the necessity of his essence. So it can in no way be said that God is passive with respect to anything other than himself, or that extended substance is unworthy of the divine nature, even supposing it divisible, so long as it is granted to be infinite and eternal. But enough of this for the present.

Proposition 16

From the necessity of the divine nature there must follow an infinite number of things in infinite ways—that is, everything that can fall within the sphere of an infinite intellect.

Proof. This proposition will be clear to anyone who remembers that from the given definition of any thing the intellect infers several properties which really do follow necessarily from it (that is, from the actual essence of the thing defined), and that it infers more properties the more reality the definition of the thing expresses—that is, the more reality the essence of the thing defined involves. Now since the divine nature has absolutely infinite attributes (by Definition 6 of this Part), each of which expresses infinite essence after its kind, it follows that from the necessity of its nature an infinite number of things—that is, everything that can fall within the sphere of an infinite intellect—must necessarily follow. Q.E.D.

Corollary 1. It follows that God is the efficient cause of everything that can fall within the sphere of an infinite intellect.

Corollary 2. It also follows that God is a cause in himself, and not through an accident of his nature.

Corollary 3. It follows, thirdly, that God is the absolutely first cause.

Proposition 17

God acts solely by the laws of his own nature, and is constrained by no one.

Proof. We have just shown (in Proposition 16 of this Part) that from the necessity of the divine nature alone—or, what comes to the same thing, from the laws of his nature alone—an infinite number of things follow absolutely, in an infinite number of ways. And we proved (in Proposition 15 of this Part) that without God nothing can be or be conceived, but that all things are in God. So there can be nothing outside him by which he could be conditioned or constrained to act. Therefore God acts solely by the laws of his own nature, and is constrained by no one. Q.E.D.

Corollary 1. It follows, first, that there is no cause, either outside God or within him, besides the perfection of his own nature, that moves him to act.

Corollary 2. It follows, second, that God is the sole free cause. For God alone exists by the sole necessity of his nature (by Proposition 11 of this Part; Proposition 14 of this Part, Corollary 1) and acts by the sole necessity of his own nature; therefore God is (by Definition 7 of this Part) the sole free cause. Q.E.D.

Note. Others think God is a free cause because he can, as they suppose, bring it about that the things we have said follow from his nature—the things in his power—should not come to pass, or should not be produced by him. But this is the same as saying that God could bring it about that it should follow from the nature of a triangle that its three interior angles are not equal to two right angles; or that from a given cause no effect should follow, which is absurd.

I shall also show below, without the help of this proposition, that neither intellect nor will belongs to God's nature. I know there are many who think they can show that supreme intellect and free will do belong to God's nature, on the ground that they know of nothing more perfect to attribute to God than what is the highest perfection in ourselves. And although they conceive God as actually supremely intelligent, they do not believe he can bring into existence everything he actually understands, since they think that would destroy his power. If God had created everything in his intellect, they argue, he could not create anything more, and this they think would clash with his omnipotence. So they prefer to assert that God is indifferent to all things, and creates nothing except what he has decided, by some absolute act of will, to create. But I think I have shown clearly enough (by Proposition 16 of this Part) that from God's supreme power, or infinite nature, an infinite number of things—that is, all things—have necessarily flowed forth in an infinite number of ways, and always flow from the same necessity, in the same way as it follows from the nature of a triangle, from eternity and for eternity, that its three interior angles are equal to two right angles. So the omnipotence of God has been in act from all eternity, and will remain in act for all eternity. This way of treating the question attributes to God, in my opinion, a far more perfect omnipotence. For on the other view we are forced to confess that God understands an infinite number of creatable things which he will never be able to create; for if he created everything he understands, he would, on their showing, exhaust his omnipotence and make himself imperfect. So to establish that God is perfect they would be reduced to establishing at the same time that he cannot bring about everything his power extends to—a hypothesis that seems to me utterly absurd, and utterly at odds with God's omnipotence.

Further, to say a word here about the intellect and the will that we attribute to God: if intellect and will do belong to God's eternal essence, we must take these words in a sense quite different from the one they usually bear. For the intellect and will that would constitute God's essence would have to be as far apart as the poles from the human intellect and will, and would in fact have nothing in common with them but the name—about as much correspondence as there is between the Dog, the constellation in the sky, and a dog, the animal that barks. I prove it as follows. If intellect belongs to the divine nature, it cannot, as ours is generally thought to be, come after the things understood or exist alongside them, since God is prior to all things by reason of his causality (Proposition 16 of this Part, Corollary 1). On the contrary, the truth and formal essence of things is what it is because it exists as such, by representation, in the intellect of God. So the intellect of God, insofar as it is conceived to constitute God's essence, is in reality the cause of things, both of their essence and of their existence. This seems to have been recognised by those who have asserted that God's intellect, God's will and God's power are one and the same. Since, then, God's intellect is the sole cause of things, both of their essence and of their existence, it must differ from them in respect of its essence and in respect of its existence. For a cause differs from the thing it causes precisely in what the latter gains from the former.

For example, a man is the cause of another man's existence but not of his essence (for the essence is an eternal truth), and so the two men may be entirely alike in essence but must differ in existence; and hence if one of them ceases to exist, the other does not necessarily cease with him. But if the essence of one could be destroyed and made false, the essence of the other would be destroyed too. So a thing which is the cause both of the essence and of the existence of a given effect must differ from that effect both in essence and in existence. Now the intellect of God is the cause both of the essence and of the existence of our intellect; therefore God's intellect, insofar as it is conceived to constitute the divine essence, differs from our intellect both in essence and in existence, and can agree with it in nothing but the name, as we said before. The reasoning would be exactly the same for the will, as anyone can easily see.

Propositions 18 to 36

Proposition 18

God is the indwelling cause of all things, and not a cause standing outside them.

Proof. All things that are, are in God and must be conceived through God (by Proposition 15 of this Part); therefore (by Proposition 16 of this Part, Corollary 1) God is the cause of the things that are in him. That is our first point. Further, besides God there can be no substance (by Proposition 14 of this Part), that is, nothing existing in itself outside God. That is our second point. God, therefore, is the indwelling cause of all things, and not a cause standing outside them. Q.E.D.

Proposition 19

God, and all the attributes of God, are eternal.

Proof. God (by Definition 6 of this Part) is substance, which (by Proposition 11 of this Part) necessarily exists, that is (by Proposition 7 of this Part) existence belongs to its nature, or, what comes to the same thing, follows from its definition. Therefore God is eternal (by Definition 8 of this Part). Further, by the attributes of God we must understand what (by Definition 4 of this Part) expresses the essence of the divine substance—in other words, what belongs to substance; that, I say, must be involved in the attributes of substance. Now eternity belongs to the nature of substance (as I have already shown in Proposition 7 of this Part); therefore eternity must belong to each of the attributes, and so all are eternal. Q.E.D.

Note. This proposition is also evident from the way in which (in Proposition 11 of this Part) I demonstrated the existence of God. It is evident, I repeat, from that proof, that the existence of God, like his essence, is an eternal truth. Further (in Proposition 19 of my Principles of the Cartesian Philosophy) I have proved the eternity of God in another way, which I need not repeat here.

Proposition 20

The existence of God and his essence are one and the same.

Proof. God (by Proposition 19 of this Part) and all his attributes are eternal, that is (by Definition 8 of this Part), each of his attributes expresses existence. So the same attributes of God that explain his eternal essence explain at the same time his eternal existence—in other words, what constitutes God's essence constitutes at the same time his existence. Therefore God's existence and God's essence are one and the same. Q.E.D.

Corollary 1. It follows that God's existence, like his essence, is an eternal truth.

Corollary 2. It follows, second, that God and all the attributes of God are unchangeable. For if they could change in respect of existence, they would also have to be able to change in respect of essence—that is, plainly, to change from true to false, which is absurd.

Proposition 21

All things that follow from the absolute nature of any attribute of God must always exist and be infinite; in other words, they are eternal and infinite through that attribute.

Proof. Conceive, if you can (supposing the proposition to be denied), that something in some attribute of God can follow from the absolute nature of that attribute and yet be finite, with an existence or duration that is determined: for instance, the idea of God expressed in the attribute of thought. Now thought, insofar as it is supposed to be an attribute of God, is necessarily (by Proposition 11 of this Part) infinite in its nature. But insofar as it possesses the idea of God, it is supposed finite. It cannot, however, be conceived as finite unless it is limited by thought (by Definition 2 of this Part); and it is not limited by thought insofar as thought has constituted the idea of God, for so far it is supposed to be finite. Therefore it is limited by thought insofar as thought has not constituted the idea of God—which nevertheless (by Proposition 11 of this Part) must necessarily exist.

We have therefore granted a thought that does not constitute the idea of God; and so the idea of God does not follow naturally from thought's nature insofar as it is absolute thought, since thought is conceived both as constituting and as not constituting the idea of God. This is against our hypothesis. Therefore, if the idea of God expressed in the attribute of thought—or indeed anything else in any attribute of God, for we may take any example, since the proof holds universally—follows from the necessity of the absolute nature of that attribute, the thing in question must be infinite. That was our first point.

Furthermore, a thing that follows in this way from the necessity of the nature of any attribute cannot have a limited duration. For suppose it can. Suppose something that follows from the necessity of the nature of some attribute exists in some attribute of God—the idea of God expressed in the attribute of thought, for instance—and suppose that at some time it did not exist, or is about to cease to exist.

Now thought, being an attribute of God, must necessarily exist unchanged (by Proposition 11 of this Part; Proposition 20 of this Part, Corollary 2); and beyond the limits of the duration of the idea of God—supposing it at some time not to have existed, or not to be going to exist—thought would have had to exist without the idea of God, which is contrary to our hypothesis, since we supposed that, thought being given, the idea of God necessarily flowed from it. Therefore the idea of God expressed in thought, or anything that necessarily follows from the absolute nature of some attribute of God, cannot have a limited duration, but is eternal through that attribute. That is our second point. And note that the same proposition may be affirmed of anything that in any attribute follows necessarily from God's absolute nature.

Proposition 22

Whatever follows from any attribute of God, insofar as that attribute is modified by a modification which exists necessarily and as infinite through it, must also exist necessarily and as infinite.

Proof. The proof of this proposition is like that of the one before it.

Proposition 23

Every mode that exists both necessarily and as infinite must follow either from the absolute nature of some attribute of God, or from an attribute modified by a modification which exists necessarily and as infinite.

Proof. A mode exists in something else, through which it must be conceived (Definition 5 of this Part), that is (Proposition 15 of this Part), it exists solely in God and can be conceived only through God. If, therefore, a mode is conceived as existing necessarily and as infinite, it must be inferred or perceived through some attribute of God, insofar as that attribute is conceived as expressing infinity and necessity of existence—in other words (Definition 8 of this Part), eternity; that is, insofar as it is considered absolutely. A mode, then, which exists necessarily and as infinite, must follow from the absolute nature of some attribute of God, either directly (Proposition 21 of this Part) or through some modification which follows from the absolute nature of that attribute; that is (by Proposition 22 of this Part), one which exists necessarily and as infinite.

Proposition 24

The essence of things produced by God does not involve existence.

Proof. This proposition is evident from Definition 1 of this Part. For that whose nature, considered in itself, involves existence is self-caused, and exists by the sole necessity of its own nature.

Corollary. It follows that God is the cause not only of things coming into existence but of their continuing in existence—or, in the language of the schools, God is the cause of the being of things (essendi rerum). For whether things exist or do not exist, whenever we consider their essence we see that it involves neither existence nor duration; so their essence cannot be the cause of either. God must be the sole cause, since to him alone does existence belong (Proposition 14 of this Part, Corollary 1). Q.E.D.

Proposition 25

God is the efficient cause not only of the existence of things but also of their essence.

Proof. Deny it, and God is not the cause of the essence of things; and then the essence of things can (by Axiom 4 of this Part) be conceived without God. That (by Proposition 15 of this Part) is absurd. Therefore God is the cause of the essence of things. Q.E.D.

Note. This proposition follows more clearly from Proposition 16 of this Part. For it is evident from that proposition that, given the divine nature, the essence of things must be inferred from it no less than their existence. In a word, God must be called the cause of all things in the same sense in which he is called the cause of himself. The following corollary will make this clearer still.

Corollary. Individual things are nothing but modifications of the attributes of God, or modes by which the attributes of God are expressed in a fixed and definite way. The proof appears from Proposition 15 of this Part and Definition 5 of this Part.

Proposition 26

A thing determined to act in a particular way has necessarily been so determined by God; and a thing that has not been so determined by God cannot determine itself to act.

Proof. That by which things are said to be determined to act in a particular way is necessarily something positive; this is obvious. Therefore God, by the necessity of his nature, is the efficient cause both of its essence and of its existence (Proposition 25 of this Part; Proposition 16 of this Part). That is our first point. Our second point plainly follows from it. For if a thing that had not been determined by God could determine itself, the first part of our proof would be false, and that, as we have shown, is absurd.

Proposition 27

A thing that has been determined by God to act in a particular way cannot make itself undetermined.

Proof. This proposition is evident from the third axiom.

Proposition 28

No individual thing—nothing finite, that is, and having a determined existence—can exist or be determined to act unless it is determined for existence and action by another cause, which is also finite and has a determined existence; and this cause in its turn cannot exist or be determined to act unless it is determined for existence and action by another cause, which is also finite and has a determined existence; and so on to infinity.

Proof. Whatever is determined to exist and act has been so determined by God (by Proposition 26 of this Part; Proposition 24 of this Part, Corollary).

But what is finite and has a determined existence cannot be produced by the absolute nature of any attribute of God, for whatever follows from the absolute nature of any attribute of God is infinite and eternal (by Proposition 21 of this Part). It must therefore follow from some attribute of God insofar as that attribute is considered as in some way modified; for substance and modes are the whole of what exists (by Axiom 1 of this Part; Definition 3 of this Part; Definition 5 of this Part), and modes are simply modifications of the attributes of God. But a determined thing cannot follow from God, or from any of his attributes, insofar as that attribute is modified by a modification which is infinite and eternal. Therefore it must follow from, or be determined for existence and action by, God or one of his attributes insofar as they are modified by some modification which is finite and has a determined existence. That is our first point. And this cause, or this modification, must in its turn be determined by another cause, also finite and with a determined existence, for the reason by which we established the first part of this proof; and that last by another, for the same reason; and so on, for the same reason, to infinity. Q.E.D.

Note. Since certain things must be produced immediately by God—namely those which follow necessarily from his absolute nature—and the rest through these primary things, which nevertheless can neither exist nor be conceived without God, it follows: first, that God is absolutely the proximate cause of the things immediately produced by him. I say absolutely, not "after his kind," as is usually stated. For the effects of God can neither exist nor be conceived without their cause (Proposition 15 of this Part; Proposition 24 of this Part, Corollary). Second, that God cannot properly be called the remote cause of individual things, except to distinguish them from what he produces immediately, or rather from what follows from his absolute nature. For by a remote cause we understand a cause in no way joined to its effect; and all things that are, are in God, and depend on God so entirely that without him they can neither be nor be conceived.

Proposition 29

Nothing in the universe is contingent; all things are determined by the necessity of the divine nature to exist and to act in a particular way.

Proof. Whatever is, is in God (Proposition 15 of this Part). But God cannot be called contingent, for (by Proposition 11 of this Part) he exists necessarily and not contingently. Further, the modes of the divine nature follow from it necessarily and not contingently (Proposition 16 of this Part); and they follow so whether we consider the divine nature absolutely or as in some way determined to act (Proposition 27 of this Part). Further, God is the cause of these modes not only insofar as they simply exist (by Proposition 24 of this Part, Corollary) but also insofar as they are considered as determined to act in a particular way (Proposition 26 of this Part). If they are not determined by God (Proposition 26 of this Part), it is impossible, and not contingent, that they should determine themselves; and if they are determined by God, it is impossible, and not contingent, that they should make themselves undetermined. Therefore all things are determined by the necessity of the divine nature, not only to exist but to exist and act in a particular way, and there is nothing contingent. Q.E.D.

Note. Before going further, I want to explain here what we should understand by nature viewed as active (natura naturans) and nature viewed as passive (natura naturata). I say explain, or rather call attention to it, for I think it is already clear enough from what has been said. By nature viewed as active we should understand what is in itself and is conceived through itself, or those attributes of substance which express eternal and infinite essence—in other words (Proposition 14 of this Part, Corollary 1; Proposition 17 of this Part, Corollary 2), God, insofar as he is considered as a free cause.

By nature viewed as passive I understand everything that follows from the necessity of God's nature, or of any of God's attributes: that is, all the modes of the attributes of God, insofar as they are considered as things which are in God and which without God can neither exist nor be conceived.

Proposition 30

The intellect in act, whether finite or infinite, must comprehend the attributes of God and the modifications of God, and nothing else.

Proof. A true idea must agree with its object (Axiom 6 of this Part); in other words, obviously, whatever is contained in the intellect by representation must exist in nature. But in nature (by Proposition 14 of this Part, Corollary 1) there is no substance but God, nor any modifications but those (Proposition 15 of this Part) which are in God and can neither be nor be conceived without God. Therefore the intellect in act, whether finite or infinite, must comprehend the attributes of God and the modifications of God, and nothing else. Q.E.D.

Proposition 31

The intellect in act, whether finite or infinite, along with will, desire, love and the rest, must be referred to nature viewed as passive, not to nature viewed as active.

Proof. By the intellect we do not, obviously, mean absolute thought, but only a particular mode of thinking, different from other modes such as love and desire, and therefore (Definition 5 of this Part) requiring to be conceived through absolute thought. It must (by Proposition 15 of this Part; Definition 6 of this Part) be conceived through some attribute of God which expresses the eternal and infinite essence of thought, in such a way that without that attribute it could neither be nor be conceived. It must therefore be referred to nature viewed as passive rather than to nature viewed as active, as must the other modes of thinking. Q.E.D.

Note. In speaking of the intellect in act I am not admitting that there is such a thing as intellect in potentiality. Wishing to avoid all confusion, I want to speak only of what we perceive most clearly, namely the very act of understanding, than which nothing is perceived more clearly. For we cannot perceive anything without at the same time adding to our knowledge of the act of understanding.

Proposition 32

Will cannot be called a free cause, but only a necessary one.

Proof. Will is only a particular mode of thinking, like intellect; therefore (by Proposition 28 of this Part) no act of will can exist or be determined to act unless it is determined by some cause other than itself, and that cause by a third, and so on to infinity. And if will is supposed infinite, it must still be determined to exist and act by God—not by virtue of his being substance absolutely infinite, but by virtue of his possessing an attribute which expresses the infinite and eternal essence of thought (by Proposition 23 of this Part). So however it is conceived, whether as finite or as infinite, it requires a cause by which it is determined to exist and act. Therefore (Definition 7 of this Part) it cannot be called a free cause, but only a necessary or constrained one. Q.E.D.

Corollary 1. It follows, first, that God does not act from freedom of the will.

Corollary 2. It follows, second, that will and intellect stand in the same relation to the nature of God as motion and rest do, and as absolutely all natural phenomena do, which must be determined by God (Proposition 29 of this Part) to exist and act in a particular way. For will, like the rest, needs a cause by which it is determined to exist and act in a particular way. And although, once will or intellect is granted, an infinite number of results may follow, God cannot on that account be said to act from freedom of the will, any more than the infinite number of results that follow from motion and rest would justify us in saying that motion and rest act by free will. So will belongs to God no more than anything else in nature does; it stands in the same relation to him as motion, rest and the like, which we have shown to follow from the necessity of the divine nature and to be determined by it to exist and act in a particular way.

Proposition 33

Things could not have been produced by God in any other way, or in any other order, than the way and order in which they have in fact been produced.

Proof. All things follow necessarily from the nature of God (Proposition 16 of this Part), and are determined by the nature of God to exist and act in a particular way (Proposition 29 of this Part). If, then, things could have had a different nature, or could have been determined to act in a different way, so that the order of nature would have been different, God's nature would also have been able to be different from what it now is; and therefore (by Proposition 11 of this Part) that different nature would also have had to exist, and consequently there could have been two or more Gods. That (by Proposition 14 of this Part, Corollary 1) is absurd. Therefore things could not have been produced by God in any other way, and so on. Q.E.D.

Note 1. Having thus shown, more clearly than the noonday sun, that there is nothing to justify our calling things contingent, I want to explain briefly what we should mean by the word contingent. But first I will explain the words necessary and impossible.

A thing is called necessary either in respect of its essence or in respect of its cause; for the existence of a thing follows necessarily either from its essence and definition or from a given efficient cause. And a thing is called impossible for the same sorts of reason: either because its essence or definition involves a contradiction, or because there is no external cause determined to produce such an effect. But a thing can be called contingent in no respect except in relation to a defect in our knowledge.

A thing whose essence we do not know to involve a contradiction or not, or which we know involves no contradiction but whose existence we are still in doubt about because the order of causes escapes us—such a thing, I say, can appear to us neither necessary nor impossible. So we call it contingent, or possible.

Note 2. It clearly follows from what has been said that things have been produced by God in the highest perfection, since they have followed necessarily from a most perfect nature. Nor does this prove any imperfection in God; on the contrary, it is his perfection that has compelled us to affirm it. From the contrary proposition it would clearly follow, as I have just shown, that God is not supremely perfect; for if things had been produced in any other way, we should have to assign God a nature different from the one we are bound to attribute to him when we consider an absolutely perfect being.

I do not doubt that many will dismiss this idea as absurd and refuse to give their minds to it, simply because they are used to assigning God a freedom very different from the one we (Definition 7 of this Part) have deduced. They assign him, in short, absolute free will. But I am also convinced that if such people reflect on the matter and weigh our chain of propositions properly, they will reject the freedom they now attribute to God, not merely as worthless but as a serious obstacle to organised knowledge. There is no need for me to repeat what I said in the note to Proposition 17 of this Part. But for my opponents' sake I will show further that even if it were granted that will belongs to the essence of God, it would still follow from his perfection that things could not have been created by him other than they are, or in a different order. This is easily proved if we reflect on what my opponents themselves concede: that it depends solely on the decree and will of God that each thing is what it is—for otherwise God would not be the cause of all things—and that all God's decrees have been ratified from all eternity by God himself, for otherwise God would be convicted of imperfection or change. But in eternity there is no when, no before and no after; and so it follows solely from the perfection of God that God never can decree, and never could have decreed, anything but what is, and that God did not exist before his decrees and would not exist without them. But it will be said that even supposing God had made a different universe, or had ordained different decrees from all eternity concerning nature and her order, we could not conclude any imperfection in God. Those who say this must admit that God can change his decrees. For if God had ordained decrees about nature and her order different from those he has ordained—in other words, if he had willed and conceived something different about nature—he would have had to have a different intellect from the one he has, and a different will. And if we may assign God a different intellect and a different will without any change in his essence or his perfection, what would prevent him from changing the decrees he has made about created things and remaining perfect all the same? For his intellect and will concerning created things and their order are the same in respect of his essence and perfection, however they are conceived.

Further, all the philosophers I have read admit that in God there is no potential intellect, only actual; and since they also admit that God's intellect, God's will and God's essence are identical, it follows that if God had had a different actual intellect and a different will, his essence too would have been different. And so, as I concluded at the start, if things had been produced by God in a different way from the way they have been, God's intellect and will—that is, as they admit, his essence—would have had to be different, which is absurd.

Since, then, things could not have been produced by God in any but the actual way and order in which they have been, and since the truth of this proposition follows from God's supreme perfection, we can have no sound reason for persuading ourselves that God did not wish to create everything that was in his intellect, and to create it in the same perfection in which he understood it.

But it will be said that there is in things no perfection and no imperfection; that what is in them, and makes them called perfect or imperfect, good or bad, depends solely on the will of God, and that if God had so willed he might have brought it about that what is now perfection should be the extreme of imperfection, and the other way round. But what is such a claim except an open declaration that God, who necessarily understands what he wills, might bring it about by his will that he should understand things differently from the way he does understand them? That, as we have just shown, is the height of absurdity. So I may turn the argument back on those who use it: all things depend on the power of God. For things to be different from what they are, God's will would have to be different. But God's will cannot be different, as we have just demonstrated very clearly, from God's perfection. Therefore neither can things be different. I confess that the theory which subjects all things to the will of an indifferent deity, and makes them all depend on his decree, is less far from the truth than the theory of those who hold that God acts in all things with a view to promoting what is good. For these last seem to set up something outside God, which does not depend on God, and which God looks to in acting as a model, or aims at as a definite goal. This is only another name for subjecting God to the rule of fate—an utter absurdity in the case of God, whom we have shown to be the first and only free cause both of the essence of all things and of their existence. I need spend no more time refuting such wild theories.

Proposition 34

God's power is identical with his essence.

Proof. From the sole necessity of God's essence it follows that God is the cause of himself (Proposition 11 of this Part) and of all things (Proposition 16 of this Part, Corollary). Therefore the power of God, by which he and all things are and act, is identical with his essence. Q.E.D.

Proposition 35

Whatever we conceive to be in God's power necessarily exists.

Proof. Whatever is in God's power must (by Proposition 34 of this Part) be comprehended in his essence in such a way that it necessarily follows from it, and therefore necessarily exists. Q.E.D.

Proposition 36

There is nothing from whose nature some effect does not follow.

Proof. Whatever exists expresses God's nature or essence in a definite and determined way (by Proposition 25 of this Part, Corollary); that is (by Proposition 34 of this Part), whatever exists expresses in a definite and determined way God's power, which is the cause of all things. Therefore an effect must (by Proposition 16 of this Part) necessarily follow. Q.E.D.

Appendix

In what has gone before I have explained the nature and properties of God: that he necessarily exists; that he is one; that he is and acts solely by the necessity of his own nature; that he is the free cause of all things, and in what way; that all things are in God and depend on him so completely that without him they could neither exist nor be conceived; and lastly that all things are predetermined by God, not by his free will or absolute decree, but by the very nature of God, that is, by his infinite power. I have also taken care, where the occasion offered, to clear away the prejudices that might stand in the way of my demonstrations. But a good many misconceptions remain, and they might well prove serious obstacles to understanding the chain of things as I have set it out. I have therefore thought it worth while to bring them before the bar of reason.

All such opinions spring from one notion, commonly held: that everything in nature acts as men do, with an end in view. It is taken for certain that God himself directs all things to a definite goal—for it is said that God made all things for man, and man that he might worship God. So I shall consider this opinion, asking first why it is so widely believed, and why men are naturally so ready to adopt it; second, I shall show that it is false; and last, I shall show how it has given rise to prejudices about good and bad, right and wrong, praise and blame, order and confusion, beauty and ugliness, and the like. This is not the place to derive those misconceptions from the nature of the human mind. It will be enough here if I take as my starting point what everyone ought to admit: that all men are born ignorant of the causes of things; that all have a desire to seek what is useful to them; and that they are conscious of that desire. From this it follows, first, that men think themselves free, because they are conscious of their volitions and desires and never even dream, in their ignorance, of the causes that have disposed them to want and desire as they do. Second, that men do everything for an end, namely for what is useful to them and what they seek. So it happens that they look only for knowledge of the final causes of events, and when they have learned those they are satisfied, having no further ground for doubt. If they cannot learn such causes from outside, they are driven to turn to themselves and ask what end would have induced them personally to bring the event about; and so they necessarily judge other natures by their own. Further, since they find, in themselves and outside themselves, many things that help them considerably in the search for what is useful—eyes for seeing, teeth for chewing, plants and animals for food, the sun for light, the sea for breeding fish, and so on—they come to look on the whole of nature as a means for obtaining such conveniences. And since they are aware that they found these conveniences and did not make them, they think they have grounds for believing that some other being made them for their use. Looking on things as means, they cannot believe them to be self-created; and judging from the means they are used to preparing for themselves, they are bound to believe in some ruler or rulers of the universe, endowed with human freedom, who have arranged and adapted everything for human use. Having no information about such rulers, they are bound to estimate their nature by their own; and so they assert that the gods ordained everything for man's use in order to bind man to themselves and receive the highest honour from him. Hence it also follows that each man devised for himself, according to his abilities, a different way of worshipping God, so that God might love him more than the rest and direct the whole course of nature to satisfy his blind greed and insatiable avarice. Thus the prejudice grew into superstition and took deep root in the human mind; and this is why everyone laboured so hard to understand and explain the final causes of things. But in trying to show that nature does nothing in vain—that is, nothing useless to man—they seem only to have demonstrated that nature, the gods and men are all mad together. Consider, I beg you, the result. Among nature's many helps they were bound to find some hindrances too—storms, earthquakes, diseases and the like—so they declared that these things happen because the gods are angry at some wrong done them by men, or some fault in their worship. Experience protested day after day, and showed by countless examples that good and bad fortune fall alike to the pious and the impious; but they would not give up their inveterate prejudice. It was easier for them to file such contradictions among the other unknown things whose use they were ignorant of, and so keep their present and inborn ignorance intact, than to pull down the whole fabric of their reasoning and start again. So they laid it down as an axiom that God's judgments far surpass human understanding. That doctrine alone might well have kept the truth hidden from the human race for ever, if mathematics had not offered another standard of truth—concerned solely with the essences and properties of figures, and taking no account of final causes. There are other reasons besides mathematics, which I need not go into here, that might have turned men's minds towards these general prejudices and led them to a knowledge of the truth.

I have now said enough on my first point. There is no need to show at length that nature has no particular goal in view, and that final causes are mere human fictions. This is clear enough already, both from the causes and foundations on which I have shown the prejudice to rest, and from Proposition 16 of this Part and the Corollary of Proposition 32 of this Part, and in fact from all the propositions in which I have shown that everything in nature proceeds by a kind of necessity and with the utmost perfection. But I will add a few remarks to overthrow the doctrine of final causes altogether. What is really a cause it takes for an effect, and the other way round; it makes what is by nature first come last, and what is highest and most perfect the most imperfect. Passing over the questions of cause and priority as self-evident, it is plain from Proposition 21 of this Part, Proposition 22 of this Part and Proposition 23 of this Part that the effect produced immediately by God is the most perfect, and that an effect requiring several intermediate causes for its production is to that extent more imperfect. But if the things made immediately by God were made to enable him to reach his end, then the things that come after, for whose sake the first were made, are necessarily the most excellent of all.

Further, this doctrine does away with the perfection of God. For if God acts for an object, he necessarily desires something he lacks. Theologians and metaphysicians do, it is true, draw a distinction between an object of want and an object of assimilation; but they still confess that God made all things for his own sake and not for the sake of what he created. And since they can point to nothing before the creation, other than God himself, as an object for which God should act, they are driven to admit—as they plainly must—that God lacked the things for whose attainment he created the means, and that he desired them.

We should not fail to notice that the followers of this doctrine, eager to display their ingenuity in assigning final causes, have brought in a new method of proof: a reduction not to the impossible but to ignorance—which shows that they have no other way of exhibiting their doctrine. Take an example. If a stone falls from a roof onto someone's head and kills him, they will demonstrate by their new method that the stone fell in order to kill the man; for if it had not fallen with that object by God's will, how could so many circumstances have come together by chance, as they often do? Perhaps you will answer that it happened because the wind was blowing and the man was walking that way. "But why," they will press, "was the wind blowing, and why was the man walking that way at that very moment?" If you answer again that the wind had got up because the sea had begun to be rough the day before, the weather having been calm until then, and that the man had been invited out by a friend, they will press again: "But why was the sea rough, and why was the man invited just then?" And so they will pursue their questions from cause to cause until at last you take refuge in the will of God—the sanctuary of ignorance. In the same way, when they contemplate the structure of the human body they are amazed; and being ignorant of the causes of so great a work of art, they conclude that it was fashioned not mechanically but by divine and supernatural skill, and so put together that no one part injures another.

So anyone who looks for the true causes of miracles, and who tries to understand natural things as an intelligent being rather than gape at them like a fool, is set down and denounced as an impious heretic by those whom the masses worship as the interpreters of nature and of the gods. Such people know that if ignorance were removed, the wonder that is their only means of proving and preserving their authority would vanish with it. But I leave this subject and pass on to my third point.

Once men had persuaded themselves that everything created is created for their sake, they were bound to regard as the chief quality in everything whatever was most useful to themselves, and to reckon those things best that had the most beneficial effect on mankind. They were bound, too, to form abstract notions to explain the nature of things—goodness, badness, order, confusion, warmth, cold, beauty, deformity and so on. And from the belief that they are free agents came the further notions of praise and blame, sin and merit.

I shall speak of the second group later, when I treat of human nature; the first I will explain briefly here.

Whatever conduces to health and to the worship of God they have called good, and whatever hinders these they have called bad. And since people who do not understand the nature of things verify nothing, but merely imagine things after a fashion and mistake their imagination for understanding, they firmly believe that there is an order in things—being really ignorant both of things and of their own nature. When things are such that the impression they make on our senses costs the imagination little effort, and so can easily be remembered, we say they are well ordered; when the opposite, we say they are ill ordered or confused. And since what is easily imagined is more pleasing to us, men prefer order to confusion, as though there were any order in nature except in relation to our imagination; and they say that God has created all things in order, thus attributing imagination to God without knowing it—unless indeed they would have it that God foresaw human imagination and arranged everything so as to be most easily imagined. If that is their theory, they will perhaps not be troubled by the fact that we find countless things far surpassing our imagination, and a great many more that confound its weakness. But enough of this. The other abstract notions are nothing but ways of imagining, in which the imagination is affected differently; and yet the ignorant take them for the chief attributes of things, since they believe everything was created for their own sake, and call a thing good or bad, healthy or rotten and corrupt, according as it affects them. For instance, if the motion that visible objects communicate to our nerves is good for our health, the objects causing it are called beautiful; if the motion excited is the contrary, they are called ugly.

Things perceived by our sense of smell are called fragrant or foul; by taste, sweet or bitter, full-flavoured or insipid; by touch, hard or soft, rough or smooth, and so on.

Whatever affects our ears is said to produce noise, sound or harmony. In this last case there are men mad enough to believe that God himself takes pleasure in harmony, and philosophers are not wanting who have persuaded themselves that the motion of the heavenly bodies produces harmony. All of which sufficiently shows that everyone judges of things according to the state of his brain, or rather mistakes the forms of his imagination for the things themselves. We need no longer wonder that all the controversies we have seen have arisen, and scepticism at the end of them. For although human bodies agree in many respects, they differ in a great many others; so that what seems good to one seems bad to another, what seems well ordered to one seems confused to another, what pleases one displeases another, and so on. I need not go on, both because this is not the place to treat the subject at length and because the fact is well enough known. Everyone says: "So many men, so many minds; everyone is wise in his own way; brains differ as completely as palates." All of which proverbs show that men judge of things according to the disposition of their minds, and imagine rather than understand. For if they understood things, they would be convinced by what I have urged, even if they were not attracted by it—as mathematics bears witness.

We have now seen that all the explanations commonly given of nature are mere ways of imagining, and indicate not the true nature of anything but only the constitution of the imagination. And although they have names, as though they were entities existing outside the imagination, I call them imaginary entities rather than real ones. So all the arguments brought against us from such abstractions are easily rebutted.

Many argue like this. If all things follow from the necessity of God's absolutely perfect nature, why are there so many imperfections in nature—things corrupted to the point of stinking, loathsome deformity, confusion, evil, sin and the rest? But such reasoners are, as I have said, easily refuted. The perfection of things is to be reckoned from their own nature and power alone; things are not more or less perfect according as they please or offend human senses, or according as they serve mankind or go against it. And to those who ask why God did not create all men so that they should be governed by reason alone, I give no answer but this: because he did not lack the material for creating every degree of perfection from the highest to the lowest; or, to speak more strictly, because the laws of his nature are so vast that they suffice for the production of everything an infinite intelligence can conceive, as I have shown in Proposition 16 of this Part.

These are the misconceptions I undertook to note here. If there are any more of the same kind, anyone can easily dispel them for himself with a little reflection.

Part Two: On the Nature and Origin of the Mind

Preface, Definitions, Axioms, the Physics, Propositions 1 to 13

Preface

I now pass on to explaining what must necessarily follow from the essence of God, the eternal and infinite being—not all of it (for we proved in Part 1, Proposition 16, that an infinite number must follow in an infinite number of ways), but only what can lead us, as if by the hand, to knowledge of the human mind and its highest blessedness.

Definitions
