1. By body I mean a mode which expresses in a definite and determined way the essence of God insofar as he is considered as an extended thing (see Part 1, Proposition 25, Corollary).

2. I consider as belonging to the essence of a thing that which, being given, the thing is necessarily given also, and which, being taken away, the thing is necessarily taken away also; in other words, that without which the thing can neither be nor be conceived, and which itself can neither be nor be conceived without the thing.

3. By idea I mean the conception which the mind forms because it is a thinking thing.

Explanation. I say conception rather than perception, because the word perception seems to imply that the mind is passive towards its object, whereas conception seems to express an activity of the mind.

4. By an adequate idea I mean an idea which, considered in itself and without reference to its object, has all the properties or intrinsic marks of a true idea.

Explanation. I say intrinsic in order to exclude the mark that is extrinsic, namely the agreement between the idea and its object (ideatum).

5. Duration is the indefinite continuance of existing.

Explanation. I say indefinite because it cannot be determined by the existence of the existing thing itself, nor by its efficient cause, which necessarily gives the thing its existence but does not take it away.

6. Reality and perfection I use as meaning the same thing.

7. By particular things I mean things that are finite and have a determined existence. But if several individual things work together in one action, so as to be all at once the effect of one cause, I consider them all, so far, as one particular thing.

Axioms

1. The essence of man does not involve necessary existence; that is, in the order of nature it may come about that this or that man exists, or does not exist.

2. Man thinks.

3. Modes of thinking, such as love and desire and the other passions, do not occur unless there is in the same individual an idea of the thing loved, desired, and so on. But an idea can exist without any other mode of thinking being present.

4. We perceive that a certain body is affected in many ways.

5. We feel and perceive no particular things except bodies and modes of thought.

Note. The Postulates are given after the conclusion of Proposition 13 of this Part.

Proposition 1

Thought is an attribute of God, or God is a thinking thing.

Proof. Particular thoughts, this thought and that, are modes which express the nature of God in a definite and determined way (Part 1, Proposition 25, Corollary). God therefore possesses the attribute (Part 1, Definition 5) whose concept is involved in all particular thoughts, and through which they are conceived. Thought, then, is one of the infinite attributes of God which express God's eternal and infinite essence (Part 1, Definition 6). In other words, God is a thinking thing. Q.E.D.

Note. This proposition is also evident from the fact that we can conceive an infinite thinking being. For the more thoughts a thinking being is conceived as thinking, the more reality or perfection it is conceived as containing. Therefore a being which can think an infinite number of things in an infinite number of ways is, in respect of thinking, necessarily infinite. Since, then, from the consideration of thought alone we conceive an infinite being, thought is necessarily (Part 1, Definition 4; Part 1, Definition 6) one of the infinite attributes of God, which is what we wanted to show.

Proposition 2

Extension is an attribute of God, or God is an extended thing.

Proof. The proof of this proposition is like that of the last.

Proposition 3

In God there is necessarily the idea not only of his essence but also of everything that necessarily follows from his essence.

Proof. God (by Proposition 1 of this Part) can think an infinite number of things in infinite ways, or (what is the same thing, by Part 1, Proposition 16) can form the idea of his essence and of everything that necessarily follows from it. Now everything that is in God's power necessarily is (Part 1, Proposition 35). Therefore such an idea as we are considering necessarily is, and is in God alone (Part 1, Proposition 15). Q.E.D.

Note. Most people understand by the power of God the free will of God, and his right over everything that exists—which is why all existing things are generally reckoned contingent. For it is said that God has the power to destroy everything and reduce it to nothing. And the power of God is very often compared to the power of kings. But we have refuted this doctrine (Part 1, Proposition 32, Corollary 1; Part 1, Proposition 2), and we have shown (Part 1, Proposition 16) that God acts by the same necessity by which he understands himself: in other words, just as it follows from the necessity of the divine nature, as everyone admits, that God understands himself, so by the same necessity it follows that God performs infinite acts in infinite ways. We further showed (Part 1, Proposition 34) that God's power is identical with God's essence in action; so it is as impossible for us to conceive God as not acting as to conceive him as not existing. If I were to pursue the subject, I could point out that the power commonly attributed to God is not only human—showing that God is conceived by most people as a man, or in the likeness of a man—but actually involves a negation of power. But I am unwilling to go over the same ground so often. I only beg the reader, again and again, to turn over in his mind what I said in Part One from Proposition 16 of this Part to the end. No one will be able to follow my meaning unless he is scrupulously careful not to confuse the power of God with the human power and right of kings.

Proposition 4

The idea of God, from which an infinite number of things follow in infinite ways, can only be one.

Proof. Infinite intellect comprehends nothing but the attributes of God and his modifications (Part 1, Proposition 30). Now God is one (Part 1, Proposition 14, Corollary). Therefore the idea of God, from which an infinite number of things follow in infinite ways, can only be one. Q.E.D.

Proposition 5

The actual being of ideas has God for its cause only insofar as he is considered as a thinking thing, and not insofar as he is unfolded in any other attribute. That is, the ideas both of the attributes of God and of particular things have for their efficient cause not their objects (ideata), the things perceived, but God himself insofar as he is a thinking thing.

Proof. This proposition is evident from Proposition 3 of this Part. We there drew the conclusion that God can form the idea of his essence, and of everything that necessarily follows from it, solely because he is a thinking thing, and not because he is the object of his own idea. So the actual being of ideas has God for its cause insofar as he is a thinking thing. It may also be proved differently. The actual being of ideas is, obviously, a mode of thought—that is (Part 1, Proposition 25, Corollary), a mode which expresses the nature of God in a certain way insofar as he is a thinking thing—and therefore (Part 1, Proposition 10) involves the conception of no other attribute of God, and consequently (by Part 1, Axiom 4) is not the effect of any attribute but thought. Therefore the actual being of ideas has God for its cause insofar as he is considered as a thinking thing, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 6

The modes of any given attribute are caused by God insofar as he is considered through the attribute of which they are modes, and not insofar as he is considered through any other attribute.

Proof. Each attribute is conceived through itself, without any other (Part 1, Proposition 10), so the modes of each attribute involve the conception of that attribute and of no other. Thus (Part 1, Axiom 4) they are caused by God only insofar as he is considered through the attribute whose modes they are, and not insofar as he is considered through any other. Q.E.D.

Corollary. Hence the actual being of things which are not modes of thought does not follow from the divine nature because that nature has prior knowledge of the things. Things represented in ideas follow, and are derived from, their own attribute in the same way and by the same necessity as ideas follow, on our showing, from the attribute of thought.

Proposition 7

The order and connection of ideas is the same as the order and connection of things.

Proof. This proposition is evident from Part 1, Axiom 4. For the idea of anything caused depends on knowledge of the cause of which it is an effect.

Corollary. Hence God's power of thinking is equal to his realised power of acting: that is, whatever follows from the infinite nature of God in the world of extension (formaliter) follows without exception, in the same order and connection, from the idea of God in the world of thought (objective).

Note. Before going any further, I want to recall what has been pointed out above: that whatever can be perceived by an infinite intellect as constituting the essence of substance belongs altogether to one substance only. Consequently thinking substance and extended substance are one and the same substance, comprehended now through one attribute and now through the other. So too a mode of extension and the idea of that mode are one and the same thing, expressed in two ways. This truth seems to have been dimly recognised by those Jews who held that God, God's intellect, and the things understood by God are identical. A circle existing in nature, for instance, and the idea of that circle, which is also in God, are one and the same thing displayed through different attributes. And so, whether we conceive nature under the attribute of extension, or under the attribute of thought, or under any other attribute, we shall find the same order, one and the same chain of causes—that is, the same things following in either case.

I said that God is the cause of an idea—of the idea of a circle, for instance—insofar as he is a thinking thing, and the cause of the circle insofar as he is an extended thing, simply because the actual being of the idea of a circle can only be perceived as having for its proximate cause another mode of thinking, and that another, and so on to infinity. So as long as we consider things as modes of thinking, we must explain the order of the whole of nature, the whole chain of causes, through the attribute of thought alone; and insofar as we consider things as modes of extension, we must explain the order of the whole of nature through the attribute of extension alone; and so with the other attributes. Therefore of things as they are in themselves God is really the cause, since he consists of infinite attributes. For the present I cannot explain my meaning more clearly.

Proposition 8

The ideas of particular things, or of modes, that do not exist must be comprehended in the infinite idea of God in the same way as the formal essences of particular things or modes are contained in the attributes of God.

Proof. This proposition is evident from the last; it is understood more clearly from the note before it.

Corollary. Hence, so long as particular things do not exist except insofar as they are comprehended in the attributes of God, their representations in thought, that is their ideas, do not exist except insofar as the infinite idea of God exists. And when particular things are said to exist not only insofar as they are involved in the attributes of God but also insofar as they are said to endure, their ideas will also involve the existence through which they are said to endure.

Note. If anyone wants an example to throw more light on this, I am afraid I cannot give one that adequately explains what I am speaking of here, since it is unique; but I will try to illustrate it as far as I can. The nature of a circle is such that if any number of straight lines intersect within it, the rectangles formed by their segments will be equal to one another; so an infinite number of equal rectangles are contained in a circle. Yet none of these rectangles can be said to exist except insofar as the circle exists, nor can the idea of any of them be said to exist except insofar as it is comprehended in the idea of the circle. Now suppose that of this infinite number of rectangles two only exist. The ideas of those two exist not only insofar as they are contained in the idea of the circle, but also as involving the existence of those rectangles; and that is what distinguishes them from the ideas of the remaining rectangles.

Proposition 9

The idea of an individual thing actually existing is caused by God not insofar as he is infinite, but insofar as he is considered as affected by another idea of a thing actually existing, of which he is the cause insofar as he is affected by a third idea, and so on to infinity.

Proof. The idea of an individual thing actually existing is an individual mode of thinking, and is distinct from other modes (by Proposition 8 of this Part, Corollary, Note); so (by Proposition 6 of this Part) it is caused by God only insofar as he is a thinking thing. But not (by Part 1, Proposition 28) insofar as he is a thing thinking absolutely: only insofar as he is considered as affected by another mode of thinking; and he is the cause of this second one as being affected by a third, and so on to infinity. Now the order and connection of ideas is (by Proposition 7 of this Part) the same as the order and connection of causes. Therefore the cause of a given individual idea is another individual idea, or God insofar as he is considered as modified by that idea; and of this second idea God is the cause insofar as he is affected by another idea, and so on to infinity. Q.E.D.

Corollary. Whatever takes place in the individual object of any idea, the knowledge of it is in God only insofar as he has the idea of that object.

Proof. Whatever takes place in the object of any idea, the idea of it is in God (by Proposition 3 of this Part), not insofar as he is infinite but insofar as he is considered as affected by another idea of an individual thing (by Proposition 8 of this Part); and (by Proposition 7 of this Part) the order and connection of ideas is the same as the order and connection of things. Therefore the knowledge of what takes place in any individual object will be in God only insofar as he has the idea of that object. Q.E.D.

Proposition 10

The being of substance does not belong to the essence of man; in other words, substance does not constitute the actual being of man.

Footnote: "Forma"

Proof. The being of substance involves necessary existence (Part 1, Proposition 7). If, therefore, the being of substance belonged to the essence of man, then substance being given, man would necessarily be given also (Definition 2 of this Part), and consequently man would necessarily exist, which is absurd (Axiom 1 of this Part). Therefore, and so on. Q.E.D.

Note. This proposition may also be proved from Part 1, Proposition 5, where it is shown that there cannot be two substances of the same nature; for since there can be many men, the being of substance is not what constitutes the actual being of man. The proposition is evident, too, from the other properties of substance—that substance is by nature infinite, unchangeable, indivisible, and so on—as anyone may see for himself.

Corollary. It follows that the essence of man is constituted by certain modifications of the attributes of God. For (by Proposition 10 of this Part) the being of substance does not belong to the essence of man. That essence, therefore (by Proposition 1 of this Part; Proposition 15 of this Part), is something which is in God, and which without God can neither be nor be conceived, whether it is a modification (Proposition 1 of this Part; Proposition 25 of this Part, Corollary) or a mode which expresses God's nature in a definite and determined way.

Note. Everyone must surely admit that nothing can be or be conceived without God. All agree that God is the one and only cause of all things, both of their essence and of their existence; that is, God is the cause of things not only in respect of their coming to be (secundum fieri) but also in respect of their being (secundum esse).

At the same time many assert that whatever a thing cannot be or be conceived without belongs to the essence of that thing; so they believe either that the nature of God belongs to the essence of created things, or that created things can be or be conceived without God—or, as is more likely, they hold inconsistent doctrines. The cause of such confusion is, I think, mainly that they do not keep to the proper order of philosophical thinking. The nature of God, which should be reflected on first, since it is prior both in the order of knowledge and in the order of nature, they have taken to be last in the order of knowledge, and have put first what they call the objects of sense. So while they are considering natural things they pay no attention at all to the divine nature; and when they afterwards turn their minds to studying the divine nature they cannot keep in view the first hypotheses with which they overlaid their knowledge of natural things, since those hypotheses are no help towards understanding the divine nature. It is hardly surprising, then, that such people contradict themselves freely.

But I pass over this. My only intention here was to give a reason for not saying that whatever a thing cannot be or be conceived without belongs to the essence of that thing. Individual things can neither be nor be conceived without God, and yet God does not belong to their essence. I said that "I consider as belonging to the essence of a thing that which, being given, the thing is necessarily given also, and which, being taken away, the thing is necessarily taken away also; or that without which the thing can neither be nor be conceived, and which itself can neither be nor be conceived without the thing" (Definition 2 of this Part).

Proposition 11

The first element constituting the actual being of the human mind is the idea of some particular thing actually existing.

Proof. The essence of man (by Proposition 10 of this Part) is constituted by certain modes of the attributes of God, namely (by Axiom 2 of this Part) by modes of thinking, among all of which (by Axiom 3 of this Part) the idea is prior in nature; and when the idea is given, the other modes—those the idea is prior to—must be in the same individual (by Part 2, Axiom 3). Therefore an idea is the first element constituting the human mind. But not the idea of a non-existent thing, for then (Proposition 8 of this Part, Corollary) the idea itself could not be said to exist. It must therefore be the idea of something actually existing. But not of an infinite thing, for an infinite thing (Part 1, Proposition 21; Part 1, Proposition 22) must always necessarily exist, and this would (by Axiom 1 of this Part) be absurd. Therefore the first element constituting the actual being of the human mind is the idea of something actually existing. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows that the human mind is part of the infinite intellect of God. So when we say that the human mind perceives this or that, we are saying that God has this or that idea—not insofar as he is infinite, but insofar as he is displayed through the nature of the human mind, or insofar as he constitutes the essence of the human mind. And when we say that God has this or that idea not only insofar as he constitutes the essence of the human mind but also insofar as he has, along with the human mind, the idea of another thing as well, then we are saying that the human mind perceives a thing partly, or inadequately.

Note. Here, I do not doubt, readers will come to a halt, and think of many things that will make them hesitate. I therefore beg them to go with me slowly, step by step, and to pass no judgment on what I say until they have read to the end.

Proposition 12

Whatever takes place in the object of the idea constituting the human mind must be perceived by the human mind; that is, there will necessarily be an idea of it in the human mind. In other words, if the object of the idea constituting the human mind is a body, nothing can take place in that body without being perceived by the mind.

Proof. Whatever takes place in the object of any idea, the knowledge of it is necessarily in God (Proposition 9 of this Part, Corollary) insofar as he is considered as affected by the idea of that object—that is (Proposition 11 of this Part), insofar as he constitutes the mind of something. Therefore whatever takes place in the object constituting the idea of the human mind, the knowledge of it is necessarily in God insofar as he constitutes the essence of the human mind; that is (by Proposition 11 of this Part, Corollary), the knowledge of that thing will necessarily be in the mind—in other words, the mind perceives it.

Note. This proposition is also evident, and is more clearly understood, from Proposition 7 of this Part, which see.

Proposition 13

The object of the idea constituting the human mind is the body—that is, a certain mode of extension actually existing, and nothing else.

Proof. If the body were not the object of the human mind, the ideas of the modifications of the body would not be in God (Proposition 9 of this Part, Corollary) in virtue of his constituting our mind, but in virtue of his constituting the mind of something else; that is (Proposition 11 of this Part, Corollary), the ideas of the modifications of the body would not be in our mind. But (by Axiom 4 of this Part) we do have the idea of the modifications of the body. Therefore the object of the idea constituting the human mind is the body, and the body as it actually exists (Proposition 11 of this Part). Further, if there were any other object of the idea constituting the mind besides the body, then, since nothing can exist from which some effect does not follow (Part 1, Proposition 36), there would necessarily have to be in our mind an idea which was the effect of that other object (Proposition 11 of this Part); but (Part 1, Axiom 5) there is no such idea. Therefore the object of our mind is the body as it exists, and nothing else. Q.E.D.

Note. We thus understand not only that the human mind is united to the body, but also what the union of mind and body consists in. Nobody, however, will be able to grasp it adequately or distinctly without first having adequate knowledge of the nature of our body. The propositions we have advanced so far have been entirely general, applying no more to men than to other individual things, all of which are animated, though in different degrees. For of everything there is necessarily an idea in God, of which God is the cause in just the same way as he is of the idea of the human body; so whatever we have asserted of the idea of the human body must necessarily be asserted of the idea of everything else. Yet on the other hand we cannot deny that ideas differ from one another as their objects do, one being more excellent than another and containing more reality, just as the object of one idea is more excellent than the object of another and contains more reality.

Footnote: "Animata"

So in order to determine what makes the human mind differ from other things, and in what it surpasses them, we need to know the nature of its object, that is, of the human body. What that nature is I cannot explain here, nor is it necessary for the proof of what I am advancing. I will only say in general that the more a given body is fitted, beyond others, for doing many things at once or undergoing many impressions at once, the more its mind is fitted, beyond others, for forming many perceptions at once; and the more the actions of a body depend on itself alone, and the fewer other bodies take part with it in acting, the more its mind is fitted for distinct understanding. We can thus recognise the superiority of one mind over another, and can further see why we have only a very confused knowledge of our own body, along with many related questions which I shall deduce in the following propositions from what has been said. It is therefore worth explaining and proving my present statements more strictly, and to do that I must first lay down a few propositions about the nature of bodies.

1. All bodies are either in motion or at rest.

2. Every body moves sometimes more slowly, sometimes more quickly.

Lemma 1

Bodies are distinguished from one another in respect of motion and rest, quickness and slowness, and not in respect of substance.

Proof. The first part of this I take to be self-evident. That bodies are not distinguished in respect of substance is plain both from Part 1, Proposition 5 and from Part 1, Proposition 8. It comes out still more clearly from Part 1, Proposition 15, Note.

Lemma 2

All bodies agree in certain respects.

Proof. All bodies agree in involving the conception of one and the same attribute (Definition 1 of this Part), and in being capable of moving more slowly or more quickly, and of being absolutely in motion or at rest.

Lemma 3

A body in motion or at rest must be determined to motion or rest by another body, and that other body has been determined to motion or rest by a third, and that third by a fourth, and so on to infinity.

Proof. Bodies are individual things (Definition 1 of this Part) which (Lemma 1 of this Part) are distinguished from one another in respect of motion and rest; so (Part 1, Proposition 28) each must necessarily be determined to motion or rest by another individual thing, namely (Proposition 6 of this Part) by another body, which other body is also (Axiom 1 of this Part) in motion or at rest. And this body in turn can only have been set in motion or brought to rest by being determined to motion or rest by a third body; and that third by a fourth, and so on to infinity. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows that a body in motion keeps moving until it is determined to rest by some other body, and a body at rest stays at rest until it is determined to motion by some other body. This is indeed self-evident. For when I suppose, say, that a body A is at rest, and take no account of other bodies in motion, I can affirm nothing about A except that it is at rest. If it afterwards comes about that A is in motion, that cannot have resulted from its having been at rest, since nothing could follow from that but its remaining at rest. If on the other hand A is given as in motion, then so long as we consider A alone we shall be unable to affirm anything about it except that it is in motion. If A is later found to be at rest, that rest cannot be the result of A's previous motion, since motion can only have led to continued motion; the state of rest must therefore have resulted from something that was not in A, namely from an external cause determining A to rest.

Axiom 1. All the ways in which one body is affected by another follow at once from the nature of the body affected and the nature of the body affecting; so that one and the same body may be moved in different ways according to the difference in the nature of the bodies moving it, and different bodies may be moved in different ways by one and the same body.

Axiom 2. When a body in motion strikes another body at rest which it cannot move, it rebounds so as to continue its motion, and the angle made by the line of the rebound with the plane of the body at rest will be equal to the angle made by the line of incidence with the same plane.

So far we have been speaking only of the simplest bodies, which are distinguished from one another only by motion and rest, quickness and slowness. We now pass on to compound bodies.

Definition. When a number of bodies, of the same or different size, are pressed together by other bodies so that they remain in contact, or if they move at the same or different speeds in such a way that they keep a fixed relation of movement among themselves, we say that those bodies are in union, and that together they compose one body, or individual, which is distinguished from other bodies by this union.

Axiom 3. According as the parts of an individual, or compound body, are in contact over a greater or smaller surface, they will be moved from their position with greater or less difficulty; and consequently the individual will be brought to take another form with greater or less difficulty. Bodies whose parts are in contact over large surfaces are called hard; those whose parts are in contact over small surfaces, soft; those whose parts are in motion among one another, fluid.

Lemma 4

If certain bodies are separated from a body, or individual, composed of several bodies, and at the same time an equal number of other bodies of the same nature take their place, the individual will keep its nature as before, without any change in its actuality (forma).

Proof. Bodies (Lemma 1 of this Part) are not distinguished in respect of substance; what constitutes the actuality (formam) of an individual consists (by the Definition before Lemma 4) in a union of bodies; and this union, though there is a continual exchange of bodies, will by our hypothesis be maintained. The individual, therefore, will keep its nature as before, both in respect of substance and in respect of mode. Q.E.D.

Lemma 5

If the parts composing an individual grow greater or smaller, but in such proportion that they all keep the same mutual relations of motion and rest, the individual will still keep its original nature, and its actuality will not be changed.

Proof. The same as for the last Lemma.

Lemma 6

If certain bodies composing an individual are made to change the motion they have in one direction for motion in another, but in such a way that they can continue their motions and keep the same mutual relations as before, the individual will keep its own nature without any change of its actuality.

Proof. This proposition is self-evident, for the individual is supposed to keep everything that we spoke of in its definition as its actual being.

Lemma 7

Furthermore, the individual so composed keeps its nature whether it is moving as a whole or at rest, and whether it moves in this direction or that, so long as each part keeps its own motion and preserves its communication with the other parts as before.

Proof. This proposition is evident from the definition of an individual prefixed to Lemma 4 of this Part.

Note. We thus see how a composite individual may be affected in many different ways and yet preserve its nature. So far we have conceived an individual as composed of bodies distinguished from one another only in respect of motion and rest, speed and slowness—that is, of bodies of the simplest kind. If we now conceive another individual composed of several individuals of different natures, we shall find that the number of ways in which it can be affected without losing its nature is greatly multiplied. Each of its parts would consist of several bodies, and therefore (by Lemma 6 of this Part) each part would admit, without any change of its nature, of quicker or slower motion, and would consequently be able to transmit its motions to the remaining parts more quickly or more slowly. And if we further conceive a third kind of individual, composed of individuals of this second kind, we shall find that it can be affected in a still greater number of ways without any change in its actuality. We may easily go on in this way to infinity, and conceive the whole of nature as one individual whose parts—that is, all bodies—vary in infinite ways without any change in the individual as a whole. I should feel bound to explain and demonstrate this at greater length if I were writing a treatise on body. But I have already said that this is not my object; I have touched on the question only because it lets me prove easily what I have in view.

Postulates, Propositions 14 to 39

Postulates

1. The human body is composed of a number of individual parts, of different natures, each of which is in itself extremely complex.

2. Of the individual parts composing the human body, some are fluid, some soft, some hard.

3. The individual parts composing the human body, and consequently the human body itself, are affected in a great variety of ways by external bodies.

4. The human body needs, for its preservation, a great many other bodies by which it is, so to speak, continually regenerated.

5. When a fluid part of the human body is determined by an external body to strike often against a soft part, it changes the surface of that soft part, and leaves on it, as it were, the impression of the external body that drives it.

6. The human body can move external bodies, and arrange them in a variety of ways.

Proposition 14

The human mind is capable of perceiving a great many things, and is so in proportion as its body is capable of receiving a great many impressions.

Proof. The human body (by Postulate 3 of this Part; Postulate 6 of this Part) is affected in very many ways by external bodies, and is capable of affecting external bodies in very many ways. But (Proposition 12 of this Part) the human mind must perceive everything that takes place in the human body. The human mind is therefore capable of perceiving a great many things, and is so in proportion, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 15

The idea constituting the actual being of the human mind is not simple, but composed of a great many ideas.

Proof. The idea constituting the actual being of the human mind is the idea of the body (Proposition 13 of this Part), which (Postulate 1 of this Part) is composed of a great many complex individual parts. But there is necessarily in God the idea of each individual part of which the body is composed (Proposition 8 of this Part, Corollary); therefore (Proposition 7 of this Part) the idea of the human body is composed of these many ideas of its component parts. Q.E.D.

Proposition 16

The idea of every way in which the human body is affected by external bodies must involve the nature of the human body and also the nature of the external body.

Proof. All the ways in which any given body is affected follow from the nature of the body affected and also from the nature of the body affecting it (by Axiom 1, after the Corollary to Lemma 3), so their idea also necessarily (by Part 1, Axiom 4) involves the nature of both bodies. Therefore the idea of every way in which the human body is affected by external bodies involves the nature of the human body and of the external body. Q.E.D.

Corollary 1. It follows, first, that the human mind perceives the nature of a great many bodies along with the nature of its own.

Corollary 2. It follows, second, that the ideas we have of external bodies indicate the constitution of our own body rather than the nature of the external bodies. I have illustrated this at length in the Appendix to Part One.

Proposition 17

If the human body is affected in a way that involves the nature of some external body, the human mind will regard that external body as actually existing, or as present to itself, until the human body is affected in a way that excludes the existence or presence of that external body.

Proof. This proposition is self-evident. For as long as the human body continues to be so affected, so long will the human mind (Proposition 12 of this Part) regard this modification of the body—that is (by Proposition 16 of this Part), it will have the idea of the mode as actually existing, and this idea involves the nature of the external body. In other words, it will have an idea that does not exclude but posits the existence or presence of the nature of the external body. Therefore the mind (by Proposition 16 of this Part, Corollary 1) will regard the external body as actually existing, until it is affected, and so on. Q.E.D.

Corollary. The mind is able to regard as present those external bodies by which the human body has once been affected, even though they no longer exist or are not present.

Proof. When external bodies so determine the fluid parts of the human body that they often strike against the softer parts, they change the surface of the softer parts (Postulate 5 of this Part); hence (Axiom 2, after the Corollary to Lemma 3) the fluid parts rebound from them differently than they did before that change. And afterwards, when they strike the new surfaces by their own spontaneous motion, they will rebound in the same way as when they were driven against those surfaces by external bodies. Consequently, so long as they go on rebounding in that way, they will affect the human body in the same way, and the mind (Proposition 12 of this Part) will again take note of it—that is (Proposition 17 of this Part), the mind will again regard the external body as present, and will do so as often as the fluid parts of the human body strike those surfaces by their own spontaneous motion. So although the external bodies by which the human body was once affected no longer exist, the mind will nevertheless regard them as present as often as this action of the body is repeated. Q.E.D.

Note. We thus see how it comes about, as often happens, that we regard as present many things that are not. It is possible that the same result may come about by other causes; but I think it is enough here to have indicated one possible explanation, as good as if I had pointed out the true cause. Indeed I do not think I am far from the truth, since all my assumptions rest on postulates that are based, almost without exception, on experience—experience that cannot be disputed by those who have shown, as we have, that the human body exists as we feel it (Proposition 13 of this Part, Corollary). Furthermore (Proposition 7 of this Part, Corollary; Proposition 16 of this Part, Corollary 2), we clearly understand the difference between, say, the idea of Peter that constitutes the essence of Peter's mind, and the idea of that same Peter which is in another man, say Paul. The first answers directly to the essence of Peter's own body, and implies existence only so long as Peter exists. The second indicates the disposition of Paul's body rather than the nature of Peter; and so, while that disposition of Paul's body lasts, Paul's mind will regard Peter as present to itself, even though Peter no longer exists. Further, to keep the usual terms, we will call the modifications of the human body whose ideas represent external bodies to us as present the images of things, though they do not reproduce the shapes of things. And when the mind regards bodies in this way, we say that it imagines. I want to draw attention here, in order to indicate where error lies, to the fact that the imaginations of the mind, looked at in themselves, contain no error. The mind does not err simply by imagining; it errs only insofar as it is considered to be without the idea that excludes the existence of the things it imagines to be present to it. If the mind, while imagining non-existent things as present to it, is at the same time aware that they do not really exist, this power of imagining must be set down to the strength of its nature and not to a fault—especially if this power of imagining depends on its own nature alone, that is (Part 1, Definition 7), if it is free.

Proposition 18

If the human body has once been affected by two or more bodies at the same time, then when the mind afterwards imagines one of them it will at once remember the others too.

Proof. The mind (Proposition 17 of this Part, Corollary) imagines a given body because the human body is affected and disposed by the impressions of an external body in the same way as it was affected when certain of its parts were acted on by that external body. But by our hypothesis the body was then so disposed that the mind imagined two bodies at once; therefore it will in the second case also imagine two at once, and the mind, when it imagines one, will at once remember the other. Q.E.D.

Note. We now clearly see what memory is. It is simply a certain linking of ideas involving the nature of things outside the human body, a linking that occurs in the mind according to the order and linking of the modifications of the human body. I say, first, a linking of those ideas only which involve the nature of things outside the human body, not of ideas that express the nature of those things; for ideas of the modifications of the human body are, strictly speaking (Proposition 16 of this Part), ideas that involve the nature both of the human body and of external bodies. I say, second, that this linking occurs according to the order and linking of the modifications of the human body, in order to distinguish it from the linking of ideas that follows the order of the intellect, by which the mind perceives things through their first causes, and which is the same in all men. And from this we can further understand clearly why the mind should pass at once from the thought of one thing to the thought of another that has no likeness to it. For instance, a Roman would pass at once from the thought of the word pomum to the thought of the fruit, an apple, which has no likeness to that articulate sound and nothing in common with it, except that the man's body has often been affected by the two together—that is, the man has often heard the word pomum while looking at the fruit. In the same way every man will pass from one thought to another according as habit has arranged the images of things in his body. A soldier, for instance, seeing the tracks of a horse in sand, will pass at once from the thought of a horse to the thought of a horseman, and from that to the thought of war; while a countryman will pass from the thought of a horse to the thought of a plough, a field, and so on. Thus every man will follow one train of thought or another according as he has been in the habit of joining and linking the images of things in this way or that.

Proposition 19

The human mind has no knowledge of the body, and does not know that it exists, except through the ideas of the modifications by which the body is affected.

Proof. The human mind is the very idea, or knowledge, of the human body (Proposition 13 of this Part), which (Proposition 9 of this Part) is in God insofar as he is regarded as affected by another idea of a particular thing actually existing—since (Postulate 4 of this Part) the human body needs a great many bodies by which it is, as it were, continually regenerated, and the order and connection of ideas is the same as the order and connection of causes (Proposition 7 of this Part). This idea will therefore be in God insofar as he is regarded as affected by the ideas of a great many particular things. Thus God has the idea of the human body, or knows the human body, insofar as he is affected by a great many other ideas, and not insofar as he constitutes the nature of the human mind; that is (by Proposition 11 of this Part, Corollary), the human mind does not know the human body. But the ideas of the modifications of the body are in God insofar as he constitutes the nature of the human mind, that is, the human mind perceives those modifications (Proposition 12 of this Part), and consequently (Proposition 16 of this Part) perceives the human body itself, and as actually existing. So the mind perceives the human body only to that extent. Q.E.D.

Proposition 20

The idea, or knowledge, of the human mind is also in God, following in God in the same way, and being referred to God in the same way, as the idea or knowledge of the human body.

Proof. Thought is an attribute of God (Proposition 1 of this Part); therefore (Proposition 3 of this Part) there must necessarily be in God the idea both of thought itself and of all its modifications, and consequently of the human mind as well (Proposition 11 of this Part). Further, this idea or knowledge of the mind does not follow from God insofar as he is infinite, but insofar as he is affected by another idea of an individual thing (Proposition 9 of this Part). But (Proposition 7 of this Part) the order and connection of ideas is the same as the order and connection of causes; therefore this idea or knowledge of the mind is in God, and is referred to God, in the same way as the idea or knowledge of the body. Q.E.D.

Proposition 21

This idea of the mind is united to the mind in the same way as the mind is united to the body.

Proof. That the mind is united to the body we have shown from the fact that the body is the object of the mind (Proposition 12 of this Part; Proposition 13 of this Part); and so, for the same reason, the idea of the mind must be united with its object, that is, with the mind, in the same way as the mind is united to the body. Q.E.D.

Note. This proposition is understood much more clearly from what we said in the note to Proposition 7 of this Part. We there showed that the idea of the body and the body itself—that is, mind and body (Proposition 13 of this Part)—are one and the same individual, conceived now under the attribute of thought and now under the attribute of extension. So the idea of the mind and the mind itself are one and the same thing, conceived under one and the same attribute, namely thought. The idea of the mind, I repeat, and the mind itself are in God by the same necessity, and follow from him by the same power of thinking. Strictly speaking, the idea of the mind—that is, the idea of an idea—is nothing but the distinctive character (forma) of the idea insofar as it is conceived as a mode of thought without reference to its object. If a man knows anything, he by that very fact knows that he knows it, and at the same time knows that he knows that he knows it, and so on to infinity. But I will treat of this later.

Proposition 22

The human mind perceives not only the modifications of the body but also the ideas of those modifications.

Proof. The ideas of the ideas of modifications follow in God in the same way, and are referred to God in the same way, as the ideas of those modifications; this is proved just as Proposition 20 of this Part was. But the ideas of the modifications of the body are in the human mind (Proposition 12 of this Part), that is, in God insofar as he constitutes the essence of the human mind; therefore the ideas of these ideas will be in God insofar as he has the knowledge, or idea, of the human mind—that is (Proposition 21 of this Part), they will be in the human mind itself, which therefore perceives not only the modifications of the body but also the ideas of those modifications. Q.E.D.

Proposition 23

The mind does not know itself except insofar as it perceives the ideas of the modifications of the body.

Proof. The idea, or knowledge, of the mind (Proposition 20 of this Part) follows in God in the same way, and is referred to God in the same way, as the idea or knowledge of the body. But since (Proposition 19 of this Part) the human mind does not know the human body itself, that is (Proposition 11 of this Part, Corollary), since the knowledge of the human body is not referred to God insofar as he constitutes the nature of the human mind, neither is the knowledge of the mind referred to God insofar as he constitutes the essence of the human mind. Therefore (by Proposition 11 of this Part, Corollary) the human mind, so far, has no knowledge of itself. Further, the ideas of the modifications by which the body is affected involve the nature of the human body itself (Proposition 16 of this Part), that is (Proposition 13 of this Part), they agree with the nature of the mind; so the knowledge of these ideas necessarily involves knowledge of the mind. But (by Proposition 22 of this Part) the knowledge of these ideas is in the human mind itself; so the human mind, so far and no further, has knowledge of itself. Q.E.D.

Proposition 24

The human mind does not involve an adequate knowledge of the parts composing the human body.

Proof. The parts composing the human body do not belong to the essence of that body except insofar as they communicate their motions to one another in a certain fixed relation (the Definition after Lemma 3), and not insofar as they can be regarded as individuals without reference to the human body. The parts of the human body are highly complex individuals (Postulate 1 of this Part), whose parts (Lemma 4 of this Part) can be separated from the human body without destroying its nature or distinctive character in any way, and can communicate their motions (Axiom 1, after Lemma 3) to other bodies in another relation. Therefore (Proposition 3 of this Part) the idea, or knowledge, of each part will be in God insofar (Proposition 9 of this Part) as he is regarded as affected by another idea of a particular thing, which particular thing is prior in the order of nature to that part (Proposition 7 of this Part). And we may say the same of every part of every individual composing the human body. So the knowledge of each part composing the human body is in God insofar as he is affected by a great many ideas of things, and not insofar as he has the idea of the human body alone—that is, the idea constituting the nature of the human mind (Proposition 13 of this Part). Therefore (Proposition 11 of this Part, Corollary) the human mind does not involve an adequate knowledge of the human body. Q.E.D.

Proposition 25

The idea of each modification of the human body does not involve an adequate knowledge of the external body.

Proof. We have shown that the idea of a modification of the human body involves the nature of an external body insofar as that external body determines the human body in a given way. But insofar as the external body is an individual with no reference to the human body, the knowledge or idea of it is in God (Proposition 9 of this Part) insofar as God is regarded as affected by the idea of a further thing which (Proposition 7 of this Part) is naturally prior to that external body. Therefore an adequate knowledge of the external body is not in God insofar as he has the idea of the modification of the human body; in other words, the idea of the modification of the human body does not involve an adequate knowledge of the external body. Q.E.D.

Proposition 26

The human mind does not perceive any external body as actually existing except through the ideas of the modifications of its own body.

Proof. If the human body is in no way affected by a given external body, then (Proposition 7 of this Part) neither is the idea of the human body—in other words, the human mind—affected in any way by the idea of the existence of that external body, nor does it perceive its existence at all. But insofar as the human body is affected in some way by a given external body, to that extent (Proposition 16 of this Part, Corollary) the mind perceives that external body. Q.E.D.

Corollary. Insofar as the human mind imagines an external body, it does not have an adequate knowledge of it.

Proof. When the human mind regards external bodies through the ideas of the modifications of its own body, we say that it imagines (see Proposition 17 of this Part, Note); and the mind can only imagine external bodies as actually existing. Therefore (by Proposition 25 of this Part), insofar as the mind imagines external bodies, it does not have an adequate knowledge of them. Q.E.D.

Proposition 27

The idea of each modification of the human body does not involve an adequate knowledge of the human body itself.

Proof. Every idea of a modification of the human body involves the nature of the human body insofar as the human body is regarded as affected in a given way (Proposition 16 of this Part). But since the human body is an individual that can be affected in a great many other ways, the idea of that modification, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 28

The ideas of the modifications of the human body, insofar as they are referred only to the human mind, are not clear and distinct but confused.

Proof. The ideas of the modifications of the human body involve the nature both of the human body and of external bodies (Proposition 16 of this Part); and they must involve the nature not only of the human body but of its parts as well, since the modifications are ways (Postulate 3 of this Part) in which the parts of the human body, and consequently the human body as a whole, are affected. But (by Proposition 24 of this Part; Proposition 25 of this Part) an adequate knowledge of external bodies, and of the parts composing the human body, is not in God insofar as he is regarded as affected by the human mind, but insofar as he is regarded as affected by other ideas. These ideas of modifications, insofar as they are referred to the human mind alone, are like conclusions without premisses—in other words, confused ideas. Q.E.D.

Note. The idea constituting the nature of the human mind is proved in the same way not to be clear and distinct when considered in itself alone; and so likewise is the idea of the human mind, and the ideas of the ideas of the modifications of the human body, insofar as they are referred to the mind only, as anyone may easily see.

Proposition 29

The idea of the idea of each modification of the human body does not involve an adequate knowledge of the human mind.

Proof. The idea of a modification of the human body (Proposition 27 of this Part) does not involve an adequate knowledge of that body—in other words, does not adequately express its nature; that is (Proposition 13 of this Part), it does not adequately agree with the nature of the mind. Therefore (Part 1, Axiom 6) the idea of this idea does not adequately express the nature of the human mind, or involve an adequate knowledge of it.

Corollary. It follows that the human mind, when it perceives things in the common order of nature, has no adequate knowledge of itself, of its own body, or of external bodies, but only a confused and fragmentary knowledge. For the mind does not know itself except insofar as it perceives the ideas of the modifications of the body (Proposition 23 of this Part). It perceives its own body (Proposition 19 of this Part) only through the ideas of the modifications, and perceives external bodies only by the same means. So insofar as it has such ideas of modifications, it has no adequate knowledge of itself (Proposition 29 of this Part), nor of its own body (Proposition 27 of this Part), nor of external bodies (Proposition 25 of this Part), but only a fragmentary and confused knowledge of them (Proposition 28 of this Part, Note). Q.E.D.

Note. I say expressly that the mind has no adequate knowledge, but only a confused knowledge, of itself, of its own body and of external bodies, whenever it perceives things in the common order of nature—that is, whenever it is determined from outside, by the chance play of circumstance, to regard this or that; and not when it is determined from within, by regarding several things at once, to understand where they agree, differ and conflict. Whenever it is determined from within in any way, it regards things clearly and distinctly, as I will show below.

Proposition 30

We can have only a very inadequate knowledge of the duration of our body.

Proof. The duration of our body does not depend on its essence (Axiom 1 of this Part), nor on the absolute nature of God (Part 1, Proposition 21). But (Part 1, Proposition 28) it is determined to exist and act by causes which are themselves determined to exist and act in a fixed and definite way by other causes, and those again by others, and so on to infinity. The duration of our body therefore depends on the common order of nature, on the constitution of things. Now however a thing is constituted, adequate knowledge of it is in God insofar as he has the ideas of all things, and not insofar as he has the idea of the human body only (Proposition 9 of this Part, Corollary). Therefore the knowledge of the duration of our body is very inadequate in God insofar as he is regarded only as constituting the nature of the human mind; that is (Proposition 11 of this Part, Corollary), this knowledge is very inadequate in our mind. Q.E.D.

Proposition 31

We can have only a very inadequate knowledge of the duration of particular things outside ourselves.

Proof. Every particular thing, like the human body, must be determined by another particular thing to exist and act in a fixed and definite way; and this other particular thing must likewise be determined by a third, and so on to infinity (Part 1, Proposition 28). And since, as we showed in the previous proposition, this common property of particular things means that we have only a very inadequate knowledge of the duration of our own body, we must draw the same conclusion about the duration of particular things: that we can have only a very inadequate knowledge of it. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows that all particular things are contingent and perishable. For we can have no adequate idea of their duration (by Proposition 31 of this Part), and that is what we must understand by the contingency and perishableness of things (Part 1, Proposition 33, Note 1). For (Part 1, Proposition 29) nothing is contingent in any other sense.

Proposition 32

All ideas, insofar as they are referred to God, are true.

Proof. All ideas that are in God agree in every respect with their objects (Proposition 7 of this Part, Corollary); therefore (Part 1, Axiom 6) they are all true. Q.E.D.

Proposition 33

There is nothing positive in ideas that makes them be called false.

Proof. Deny it if you can, and conceive a positive mode of thinking that constitutes the distinctive character of falsehood. Such a mode of thinking cannot be in God (Proposition 32 of this Part), and outside God it can neither be nor be conceived (Part 1, Proposition 15). Therefore there is nothing positive in ideas that makes them be called false. Q.E.D.

Proposition 34

Every idea which in us is absolute, or adequate and perfect, is true.

Proof. When we say that an idea in us is adequate and perfect, we are saying, in other words (Proposition 11 of this Part, Corollary), that the idea is adequate and perfect in God insofar as he constitutes the essence of our mind; and consequently (Proposition 32 of this Part) we are saying that such an idea is true. Q.E.D.

Proposition 35

Falsity consists in the privation of knowledge which inadequate, fragmentary or confused ideas involve.

Proof. There is nothing positive in ideas that makes them be called false (Proposition 33 of this Part). But falsity cannot consist in simple privation, since it is minds and not bodies that are said to err and be mistaken; nor can it consist in absolute ignorance, since ignorance and error are not the same. So it consists in the privation of knowledge which inadequate, fragmentary or confused ideas involve. Q.E.D.

Note. In the note to Proposition 17 of this Part I explained how error consists in the privation of knowledge; but to throw more light on the subject I will give an example. Men are mistaken in thinking themselves free; their opinion is made up of consciousness of their own actions and ignorance of the causes by which those actions are determined. Their idea of freedom, therefore, is simply their not knowing any cause of their actions. As for their saying that human actions depend on the will, this is a mere phrase with no idea to answer to it. What the will is, and how it moves the body, none of them know; those who boast of such knowledge, and invent dwellings and lodgings for the soul, usually provoke either laughter or disgust. So too, when we look at the sun, we imagine it to be about two hundred feet away from us. The error does not lie simply in that fancy, but in the fact that while we imagine it so we do not know the sun's true distance, or the cause of the fancy. For although we afterwards learn that the sun is more than six hundred of the earth's diameters away from us, we shall still fancy it to be near; we imagine the sun as near us not because we are ignorant of its true distance, but because the modification of our body involves the essence of the sun insofar as our body is affected by it.

Proposition 36

Inadequate and confused ideas follow by the same necessity as adequate, or clear and distinct, ideas.

Proof. All ideas are in God (Part 1, Proposition 15), and insofar as they are referred to God they are true (Proposition 32 of this Part) and (Proposition 7 of this Part, Corollary) adequate. So there are no confused or inadequate ideas except in respect of a particular mind (compare Proposition 24 of this Part; Proposition 28 of this Part). Therefore all ideas, adequate or inadequate, follow by the same necessity (Proposition 6 of this Part). Q.E.D.

Proposition 37

That which is common to all things (compare Lemma 2 of this Part), and which is equally in a part and in the whole, does not constitute the essence of any particular thing.

Proof. Deny it if you can, and conceive that it constitutes the essence of some particular thing—say the essence of B. Then (Definition 2 of this Part) it can neither exist nor be conceived without B; but that is against our hypothesis. Therefore it does not belong to B's essence, nor does it constitute the essence of any particular thing. Q.E.D.

Proposition 38

Those things which are common to all, and which are equally in a part and in the whole, cannot be conceived except adequately.

Proof. Let A be something common to all bodies and equally present in a part of any given body and in the whole. I say that A cannot be conceived except adequately. For the idea of it in God will necessarily be adequate (Proposition 7 of this Part, Corollary), both insofar as God has the idea of the human body and insofar as he has the idea of the modifications of the human body, which (Proposition 16 of this Part; Proposition 25 of this Part; Proposition 27 of this Part) involve in part the nature of the human body and the nature of external bodies; that is (Proposition 12 of this Part; Proposition 13 of this Part), the idea in God will necessarily be adequate both insofar as he constitutes the human mind and insofar as he has the ideas that are in the human mind. Therefore the mind (Proposition 11 of this Part, Corollary) necessarily perceives A adequately, and perceives it adequately both insofar as it perceives itself and insofar as it perceives its own body or any external body; nor can A be conceived in any other way. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows that there are certain ideas, or notions, common to all men; for (by Lemma 2 of this Part) all bodies agree in certain respects, which (by Proposition 38 of this Part) must be perceived by everyone adequately, that is, clearly and distinctly.

Proposition 39

That which is common to, and a property of, the human body and those other bodies which usually affect the human body, and which is equally present in each part of either and in the whole, will be represented in the mind by an adequate idea.

Proof. Let A be that which is common to, and a property of, the human body and external bodies, and equally present in the human body and in those external bodies, in each part of each external body and in the whole. There will be an adequate idea of A in God (Proposition 7 of this Part, Corollary), both insofar as he has the idea of the human body and insofar as he has the ideas of those external bodies. Now suppose the human body is affected by an external body through what it has in common with it, namely A. The idea of this modification will involve the property A (Proposition 16 of this Part), and therefore (Proposition 7 of this Part, Corollary) the idea of this modification, insofar as it involves the property A, will be adequate in God insofar as God is affected by the idea of the human body; that is (Proposition 13 of this Part), insofar as he constitutes the nature of the human mind. Therefore (Proposition 11 of this Part, Corollary) this idea is also adequate in the human mind. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows that the mind is fitted to perceive more things adequately in proportion as its body has more in common with other bodies.

Propositions 40 to 49

Proposition 40

Whatever ideas in the mind follow from ideas that are adequate in it are themselves adequate too.

Proof. This proposition is self-evident. For when we say that an idea in the human mind follows from ideas that are adequate in it, we are saying, in other words (Proposition 11 of this Part, Corollary), that there is an idea in the divine intellect of which God is the cause—not insofar as he is infinite, nor insofar as he is affected by the ideas of a great many particular things, but only insofar as he constitutes the essence of the human mind.

Note 1. I have now set out the cause of those notions which are common to all men and which form the basis of our reasoning. But there are other causes of certain axioms or notions which it would be worth setting out by this method of ours, for we should then see which notions are more useful than others and which have hardly any use at all; and further, which notions are common to all men, and which are clear and distinct only to those who are free of prejudice; and we should detect those which are ill founded. We should also see where the notions called secondary come from, and consequently the axioms founded on them, along with other points of interest connected with these questions. But I have decided to pass over the subject here, partly because I have set it aside for another treatise, and partly because I am afraid of wearying the reader at too great length. Still, so as not to leave out anything that needs to be known, I will briefly set down where the terms called transcendental come from—terms such as Being, Thing, Something. These arose from the fact that the human body, being limited, is only capable of forming a certain number of images distinctly within itself at the same time (Proposition 17 of this Part, Note). If that number is exceeded, the images begin to be confused; and if the number the body can form distinctly is greatly exceeded, they will all run together into total confusion. This being so, it is evident (from Proposition 17 of this Part, Corollary; Proposition 18 of this Part) that the human mind can distinctly imagine as many things at once as its body can form images at once. When the images become quite confused in the body, the mind also imagines all bodies confusedly, without distinction, and comprehends them as it were under one attribute, namely under the attribute of Being, Thing, and so on. The same conclusion can be drawn from the fact that images are not always equally vivid, and from other similar causes which there is no need to explain here; for our purpose it is enough to consider one. They all come down to this: that these terms stand for ideas confused in the highest degree. From similar causes arise the notions we call general, such as man, horse, dog. They arise from this, that so many images—of men, for instance—are formed at once in the human mind that the power of imagining breaks down, not entirely, but far enough for the mind to lose count of the small differences between individuals (their colour, their size, and so on) and of their exact number, and to imagine distinctly only that in which all the individuals agree insofar as the body is affected by them; for that is the point at which each of them chiefly affected the body. This the mind expresses by the name man, and predicates of an infinite number of particular individuals; for, as we have said, it cannot imagine their exact number. We must note, however, that these general notions are not formed by all men in the same way. They vary from one person to another according as the point varies by which the body has most often been affected, and which the mind most easily imagines or remembers. Those who have most often looked with admiration at the stature of a man will understand by the name man an animal of upright stature; those used to attending to some other feature will form a different general image of man—that man is an animal that laughs, a two-footed animal without feathers, a rational animal—and so in other cases, everyone will form general images of things according to the habit of his body.

It is not to be wondered at, then, that so many controversies have arisen among philosophers who try to explain natural things merely by the images they have formed of them.

Note 2. From all that has been said it is clear that we perceive things and form our general notions in several ways: first (Proposition 1 of this Part), from particular things presented to our intellect through the senses in a fragmentary, confused and unordered way (Proposition 29 of this Part, Corollary); I have settled on calling such perceptions knowledge from the mere suggestions of experience.

Footnote: A Baconian phrase. Nov. Org. Aph. 100. [Pollock, p. 126, n.]

Second (Proposition 2 of this Part), from symbols—from having read or heard certain words, for instance, we remember things and form ideas of them like those through which we imagine things (Proposition 18 of this Part, Note). I shall call both these ways of regarding things knowledge of the first kind, opinion, or imagination. Third (Proposition 3 of this Part), from having notions common to all men and adequate ideas of the properties of things (Proposition 38 of this Part, Corollary; Proposition 39 of this Part, Corollary; Proposition 40 of this Part); this I call reason, and knowledge of the second kind. Besides these two kinds of knowledge there is, as I shall show later, a third, which we will call intuition. This kind of knowledge proceeds from an adequate idea of the absolute essence of certain attributes of God to an adequate knowledge of the essence of things. I will illustrate all three kinds by a single example. Three numbers are given, and a fourth is to be found which is to the third as the second is to the first. Tradesmen multiply the second by the third and divide the product by the first without hesitating, either because they have not forgotten the rule they were given by a teacher without any proof, or because they have often tried it with simple numbers, or by virtue of the proof of the nineteenth proposition of the seventh book of Euclid, that is, by the general property of proportionals.

But with very simple numbers none of this is needed. Given one, two and three, everyone can see that the fourth proportional is six; and this is much clearer, because we infer the fourth number from an intuitive grasp of the ratio the first bears to the second.

Proposition 41

Knowledge of the first kind is the only source of falsity; knowledge of the second and third kinds is necessarily true.

Proof. To knowledge of the first kind we assigned (in Proposition 40 of this Part, Note 2) all those ideas that are inadequate and confused; therefore this kind of knowledge is the only source of falsity (Proposition 35 of this Part). And we assigned to the second and third kinds those ideas that are adequate; therefore these kinds are necessarily true (Proposition 34 of this Part). Q.E.D.

Proposition 42

Knowledge of the second and third kinds, and not of the first, teaches us to tell the true from the false.

Proof. This proposition is self-evident. Anyone who knows how to distinguish true from false must have an adequate idea of true and false; that is (Proposition 40 of this Part, Note 2), he must know the true and the false by the second or third kind of knowledge.

Proposition 43

He who has a true idea knows at the same time that he has a true idea, and cannot doubt the truth of what he perceives.

Proof. A true idea in us is an idea which is adequate in God insofar as he is displayed through the nature of the human mind (Proposition 11 of this Part, Corollary). Suppose there is in God, insofar as he is displayed through the human mind, an adequate idea A. The idea of this idea must also necessarily be in God, and be referred to him in the same way as the idea A (by Part 2, Proposition 20, the proof of which is of universal application). But the idea A is supposed to be referred to God insofar as he is displayed through the human mind; therefore the idea of the idea A must be referred to God in the same way. That is (by Proposition 11 of this Part, Corollary), the adequate idea of the idea A will be in the mind that has the adequate idea A. Therefore he who has an adequate idea, or who knows a thing truly (Proposition 34 of this Part), must at the same time have an adequate idea, or true knowledge, of his own knowledge—that is, plainly, he must be certain of it. Q.E.D.

Note. I explained in the note to Proposition 21 of this Part what is meant by the idea of an idea; but the proposition above is plain enough in itself. No one who has a true idea is unaware that a true idea involves the highest certainty. To have a true idea is only another way of saying that one knows a thing perfectly, or as well as possible. Indeed no one can doubt this unless he thinks that an idea is something lifeless, like a picture on a panel, and not a mode of thinking—the very act of understanding. And who, I ask, can know that he understands something unless he first understands it? Who can know that he is certain of a thing unless he is first certain of it? And what can be clearer or more certain than a true idea, as a standard of truth? As light displays both itself and the dark, so truth is the standard both of itself and of falsity.

I think I have thus sufficiently answered these questions: if a true idea is distinguished from a false one only insofar as it is said to agree with its object, then a true idea has no more reality or perfection than a false one, since the two are distinguished only by an extrinsic mark; and consequently a man who has true ideas has no advantage over one who has only false ones. And further, how does it come about that men have false ideas? And last, how can anyone be sure that he has ideas which agree with their objects? These questions, I repeat, I have in my opinion sufficiently answered. The difference between a true and a false idea is plain: from what was said in Proposition 35 of this Part, the first is related to the second as being is to not-being. The causes of falsity I have set out very clearly in Proposition 19 of this Part and Proposition 35 of this Part with its note, and from what is said there the difference between a man who has true ideas and a man who has only false ones is clear. As for the last question—how a man can be sure that he has ideas which agree with their objects—I have just pointed out plainly enough that his knowledge arises from the simple fact that he has an idea which corresponds with its object; in other words, that truth is its own standard. We may add that our mind, insofar as it perceives things truly, is part of the infinite intellect of God (Proposition 11 of this Part, Corollary), and therefore the clear and distinct ideas of the mind are as necessarily true as the ideas of God.

Proposition 44

It is not in the nature of reason to regard things as contingent, but as necessary.

Proof. It is in the nature of reason to perceive things truly (Proposition 41 of this Part), namely (Part 1, Axiom 6) as they are in themselves—that is (Part 1, Proposition 29), not as contingent but as necessary. Q.E.D.

Corollary 1. It follows that it is only through our imagination that we regard things, whether past or future, as contingent.

Note. I will briefly explain how this way of looking at things arises. We showed above (Proposition 17 of this Part, Corollary) that the mind always regards things as present to itself, even when they do not exist, until causes arise which exclude their existence and presence. And we showed (Proposition 18 of this Part) that if the human body has once been affected by two external bodies at the same time, then when the mind afterwards imagines one of them it will at once remember the other—that is, it will regard both as present to itself, unless causes arise which exclude their existence and presence. Further, no one doubts that we imagine time from imagining some bodies to move more slowly than others, some more quickly, and some at equal speed. Suppose, then, that a child saw Peter yesterday for the first time in the morning, Paul at noon and Simon in the evening, and that today he sees Peter again in the morning. It is evident, from Proposition 18 of this Part, that as soon as he sees the morning light he will imagine the sun travelling the same part of the sky as it did when he saw it the day before—in other words, he will imagine a whole day; and along with his imagining of the morning he will imagine Peter, with noon Paul, and with evening Simon. That is, he will imagine the existence of Paul and Simon in relation to a future time. If on the other hand he sees Simon in the evening, he will refer Peter and Paul to a past time, imagining them along with his imagining of a time past. And if it should happen that on some other evening he sees James instead of Simon, then on the following morning he will associate with his imagining of evening sometimes Simon and sometimes James, but not both together, since he is supposed to have seen one or the other in the evening, not both. So his imagination will waver, and with the imagining of future evenings he will associate first one and then the other—that is, he will imagine neither of them in the future as certain, but both as contingent. This wavering of the imagination will be the same if the imagination is concerned with things we regard in this way as standing in relation to past or present time. Consequently we may imagine things as contingent whether they are referred to the present, the past or the future.

Corollary 2. It is in the nature of reason to perceive things under a certain form of eternity (sub quâdam æternitatis specie).

Proof. It is in the nature of reason to regard things not as contingent but as necessary (Proposition 44 of this Part). Reason perceives this necessity of things truly (Proposition 41 of this Part)—that is (Part 1, Axiom 6), as it is in itself. But (Part 1, Proposition 16) this necessity of things is the very necessity of God's eternal nature; therefore it is in the nature of reason to regard things under this form of eternity. We may add that the foundations of reason are the notions (Proposition 38 of this Part) which answer to what is common to all things, and which (Proposition 37 of this Part) do not answer to the essence of any particular thing; and which must therefore be conceived without any relation to time, under a certain form of eternity.

Proposition 45

Every idea of every body, or of every particular thing actually existing, necessarily involves the eternal and infinite essence of God.

Proof. The idea of a particular thing actually existing necessarily involves both the existence and the essence of that thing (Proposition 8 of this Part). Now particular things cannot be conceived without God (Part 1, Proposition 15); and since (Proposition 6 of this Part) they have God for their cause insofar as he is regarded under the attribute of which they are modes, their ideas must necessarily involve (Part 1, Axiom 4) the conception of that attribute—that is (Part 1, Proposition 6), the eternal and infinite essence of God. Q.E.D.

Note. By existence I do not here mean duration—that is, existence conceived abstractly, as a kind of quantity. I am speaking of the very nature of existence, which is assigned to particular things because they follow in infinite numbers and in infinite ways from the eternal necessity of God's nature (Part 1, Proposition 16).
