I am speaking, I repeat, of the very existence of particular things insofar as they are in God. For although each particular thing is determined by another particular thing to exist in a given way, the force by which each particular thing perseveres in existing follows from the eternal necessity of God's nature (compare Part 1, Proposition 24, Corollary).

Proposition 46

The knowledge of God's eternal and infinite essence which every idea involves is adequate and perfect.

Proof. The proof of the last proposition is universal; and whether a thing is considered as a part or as a whole, the idea of it—whether of the whole or of the part (by Proposition 45 of this Part)—will involve God's eternal and infinite essence. So what gives knowledge of God's eternal and infinite essence is common to all things, and is equally in the part and in the whole; therefore (Proposition 38 of this Part) this knowledge will be adequate. Q.E.D.

Proposition 47

The human mind has an adequate knowledge of God's eternal and infinite essence.

Proof. The human mind has ideas (Proposition 22 of this Part) by which (Proposition 23 of this Part) it perceives itself, and its own body (Proposition 19 of this Part), and external bodies (Proposition 16 of this Part, Corollary 1; Proposition 17 of this Part) as actually existing; therefore (Proposition 45 of this Part; Proposition 46 of this Part) it has an adequate knowledge of God's eternal and infinite essence. Q.E.D.

Note. We see from this that God's infinite essence and his eternity are known to all. And since all things are in God and are conceived through God, we can infer from this knowledge a great many things which we may know adequately, and can form that third kind of knowledge I spoke of in the note to Proposition 40 of this Part, whose excellence and use I shall have occasion to speak of in Part Five. Men do not have as clear a knowledge of God as they have of general notions, because they cannot imagine God as they imagine bodies, and because they have attached the name God to the images of things they are used to seeing—which they can hardly avoid, being men and continually affected by external bodies. Indeed, many errors can be traced to this one head: that we do not apply names to things correctly. When a man says that the lines drawn from the centre of a circle to its circumference are not equal, he is certainly attaching some other meaning to the word circle than the one mathematicians give it. So too, when men make mistakes in calculation, they have one set of figures in their minds and another on the paper. If we could see into their minds, they are not making a mistake; they seem to be, because we think they have the same numbers in their minds as they have on the paper. Otherwise we should not believe them to be in error, any more than I thought a man was in error whom I recently heard shouting that his hall had flown into his neighbour's hen—for his meaning seemed clear enough to me. A great many controversies have arisen from men not explaining their meaning properly, or not interpreting other people's meaning properly. For in fact, when they flatly contradict themselves, they are taking now one side of the argument and now the other, in order to oppose the opinions they think mistaken and absurd in their opponents.

Proposition 48

In the mind there is no absolute or free will; the mind is determined to will this or that by a cause which has itself been determined by another cause, and that by another, and so on to infinity.

Proof. The mind is a fixed and definite mode of thought (Proposition 11 of this Part), and therefore cannot be the free cause of its own actions (Part 1, Proposition 17, Corollary 2); in other words, it cannot have an absolute faculty of willing or refusing. It must (by Part 1, Proposition 28) be determined by a cause which has itself been determined by another cause, and that by another, and so on. Q.E.D.

Note. It is proved in the same way that there is in the mind no absolute faculty of understanding, desiring, loving and so on. It follows that these and similar faculties are either entirely fictitious or merely abstract and general terms of the sort we habitually form from particular things. Intellect and will stand in the same relation to this or that idea, or this or that volition, as "stoniness" does to this or that stone, or "man" to Peter and Paul. The cause that leads men to think themselves free has been set out in the Appendix to Part One. But before going further I should remark that by the will to affirm and decide I mean the faculty, not the desire. I mean, I repeat, the faculty by which the mind affirms or denies what is true or false, not the desire by which the mind wants a thing or turns away from it. Having proved that these faculties of ours are general notions which cannot be distinguished from the particular instances they are drawn from, we must now ask whether volitions themselves are anything besides the ideas of things. We must ask, that is, whether there is in the mind any affirmation or negation beyond what the idea, insofar as it is an idea, involves. On this see the following proposition, and Definition 3 of this Part, so that the idea of pictures does not suggest itself. For by ideas I do not mean images such as are formed at the back of the eye, or in the middle of the brain, but conceptions of thought.

Proposition 49

There is in the mind no volition, no affirmation and no negation, beyond what an idea, insofar as it is an idea, involves.

Proof. There is in the mind no absolute faculty of willing or refusing, but only particular volitions—this or that affirmation, this or that negation. Let us take a particular volition: the mode of thinking by which the mind affirms that the three interior angles of a triangle are equal to two right angles. This affirmation involves the conception, or idea, of a triangle; that is, without the idea of a triangle it cannot be conceived. To say that the concept A must involve the concept B is the same as saying that A cannot be conceived without B. Further, this affirmation cannot be made (Axiom 3 of this Part) without the idea of a triangle. Therefore this affirmation can neither be nor be conceived without the idea of a triangle. And the idea of a triangle must itself involve this same affirmation, that its three interior angles are equal to two right angles. So conversely this idea of a triangle can neither be nor be conceived without this affirmation; and therefore this affirmation belongs to the essence of the idea of a triangle, and is nothing besides. What we have said of this volition—which we picked at random—may be said of any other: that it is nothing but an idea. Q.E.D.

Corollary. Will and understanding are one and the same.

Proof. Will and understanding are nothing beyond individual volitions and ideas (Proposition 48 of this Part, Note). But a particular volition and a particular idea are one and the same (by Proposition 48 of this Part); therefore will and understanding are one and the same. Q.E.D.

Note. We have thus removed the cause commonly assigned for error. For we showed above that falsity consists solely in the privation of knowledge involved in fragmentary and confused ideas. So a false idea, insofar as it is false, does not involve certainty. When we say that a man rests content in what is false and has no doubts about it, we are not saying that he is certain, only that he does not doubt, or that he rests content in what is false because there are no reasons to make his imagination waver (see Proposition 44 of this Part, Note). So although the man is supposed to rest content in what is false, we shall never say he is certain; for by certainty we mean something positive (Proposition 43 of this Part, Note), not merely the absence of doubt.

However, so that the foregoing proposition may be fully explained, I will draw attention to a few further points, and then answer the objections that may be brought against our doctrine. Last, in order to remove every scruple, I have thought it worth pointing out some of the advantages that follow from it. I say "some", for they will be better appreciated from what we shall set out in the fifth part.

I begin, then, with the first point, and warn my readers to make an accurate distinction between an idea, or conception of the mind, and the images of things that we imagine. And they must further distinguish between ideas and the words by which we signify things. These three—images, words and ideas—are by many people either run together entirely or not distinguished with sufficient care; and so people are generally ignorant of how absolutely necessary a knowledge of this doctrine of the will is, both for philosophy and for the wise ordering of life. Those who think ideas consist in images formed in us by contact with external bodies persuade themselves that the ideas of things we can form no mental picture of are not ideas at all, but only fictions we invent by the free decree of our will. They thus regard ideas as though they were lifeless pictures on a panel, and, full of this misconception, do not see that an idea, insofar as it is an idea, involves an affirmation or a negation. And those who confuse words with ideas, or with the affirmation an idea involves, think they can will something contrary to what they feel, affirm or deny. This misconception is easily laid aside by anyone who reflects on the nature of thought, which in no way involves the conception of extension; he will then clearly understand that an idea, being a mode of thinking, consists neither in the image of anything nor in words. The essence of words and images is made up of bodily motions, which in no way involve the conception of thought.

These few words will do on that subject; I pass on to the objections that may be raised against our doctrine. The first is advanced by those who think that the will has a wider scope than the understanding and is therefore different from it. Their reason for believing the will has a wider scope is that they claim they need no increase in their faculty of assent—that is, of affirming or denying—in order to assent to an infinity of things we do not perceive, but that they do need an increase in their faculty of understanding. The will is thus distinguished from the intellect, the latter being finite and the former infinite. Second, it may be objected that experience seems to teach us very plainly that we are able to suspend judgment before assenting to what we perceive; and this is confirmed by the fact that no one is said to be deceived insofar as he perceives something, but only insofar as he assents or dissents.

A man who imagines a winged horse, for instance, does not by that admit that a winged horse exists; that is, he is not deceived unless he also admits that a winged horse does exist. So nothing seems more clearly taught by experience than that the will, the faculty of assent, is free and different from the faculty of understanding. Third, it may be objected that one affirmation does not seem to contain more reality than another; in other words, that we do not seem to need any greater power to affirm that what is true is true than to affirm that what is false is true. Yet we have seen that one idea has more reality or perfection than another, for as some objects are more excellent than others, so are the ideas of them; and this too seems to point to a difference between the understanding and the will. Fourth, it may be objected: if man does not act from free will, what will happen when the incentives to action are equally balanced, as with Buridan's ass? Will he die of hunger and thirst? If I say he will, I shall seem to be thinking of an ass, or a statue of a man, rather than an actual man. If I say he will not, then he would be determining his own action, and would consequently have the power to go and do whatever he liked. Other objections might be raised too; but since I am not bound to set out everything anyone may dream up, I will only undertake to refute the ones I have mentioned, and that as briefly as I can.

To the first objection I answer that I admit the will has a wider scope than the understanding, if by understanding is meant only clear and distinct ideas; but I deny that the will has a wider scope than perception, than the faculty of forming conceptions. Nor do I see why the faculty of willing should be called infinite any more than the faculty of feeling. For just as by the same faculty of willing we can affirm an infinite number of things—one after another, since we cannot affirm an infinite number at once—so by the same faculty of feeling we can feel or perceive, in succession, an infinite number of bodies. If it is said that there is an infinite number of things we cannot perceive, I answer that we cannot reach such things by any thinking, and consequently not by any faculty of willing either. But it may still be urged that if God wished us to perceive them, he would have to endow us with a greater faculty of perception, though not with a greater faculty of willing than we already have. This is the same as saying that if God wished us to understand an infinite number of other entities, he would have to give us a greater understanding, but not a more universal idea of entity than the one we already have, in order to grasp those infinite entities. For we have shown that the will is a universal entity, or idea, by which we explain all particular volitions—that is, what is common to them all.

Since, then, our opponents hold that this idea, common or universal to all volitions, is a faculty, it is hardly surprising that they claim such a faculty extends into the infinite, beyond the limits of the understanding; for what is universal is predicated alike of one, of many and of an infinite number of individuals.

To the second objection I reply by denying that we have a free power of suspending judgment. When we say that someone suspends judgment, we mean simply that he sees he does not perceive the matter adequately. Suspension of judgment is therefore, strictly speaking, a perception and not an act of free will. To illustrate the point, suppose a boy imagining a horse and perceiving nothing else. Since this imagining involves the existence of the horse (Proposition 17 of this Part, Corollary), and the boy perceives nothing that would exclude its existence, he will necessarily regard the horse as present; he will not be able to doubt its existence, though he is not certain of it. We have daily experience of this in dreams; and I do not suppose anyone would maintain that while he is dreaming he has the free power of suspending judgment about the things in his dream, and of bringing it about that he should not dream what he dreams he sees. Yet it happens all the same that even in dreams we suspend judgment—namely, when we dream that we are dreaming.

Further, I grant that no one is deceived so far as actual perception goes; that is, I grant that the mind's imaginings, regarded in themselves, involve no error (Proposition 17 of this Part, Note). But I deny that a man makes no affirmation in the act of perceiving. For what is the perception of a winged horse but affirming that a horse has wings? If the mind could perceive nothing but the winged horse, it would regard the horse as present to itself; it would have no reason to doubt its existence and no power of dissent, unless the imagining of a winged horse were joined to an idea that ruled out the existence of such a horse, or unless the mind perceived that its idea of a winged horse was inadequate—in which case it will either necessarily deny the existence of such a horse or necessarily be in doubt about it.

I think I have already anticipated my answer to the third objection: that the will is something universal, predicated of all ideas, and signifies only what is common to them all, namely an affirmation. Its adequate essence must therefore, insofar as it is conceived in the abstract, be in every idea, and in that respect alone the same in all—not insofar as it is considered as constituting an idea's essence, for in that respect particular affirmations differ from one another as much as ideas do. The affirmation involving the idea of a circle, for instance, differs from the one involving the idea of a triangle as much as the idea of a circle differs from the idea of a triangle.

Further, I absolutely deny that we need an equal power of thinking to affirm that what is true is true and to affirm that what is false is true. These two affirmations, if we regard the mind, stand to one another as being to not-being; for there is nothing positive in ideas which constitutes the actual reality of falsehood (Proposition 35 of this Part, Note; Proposition 47 of this Part, Note).

We must therefore conclude that we are easily deceived when we confuse universals with singulars, and beings of reason and abstractions with real things. As for the fourth objection, I am quite ready to admit that a man placed in the balance described—perceiving nothing but hunger and thirst, a certain food and a certain drink, each equally far from him—would die of hunger and thirst. And if I am asked whether such a man should not be considered an ass rather than a man, I answer that I do not know; neither do I know how we should consider a man who hangs himself, or how we should consider children, fools and madmen.

It remains to point out the advantages of knowing this doctrine as it bears on conduct, and these may easily be gathered from what has been said. The doctrine is good:

1. Because it teaches us to act solely according to the decree of God, and to share in the divine nature, and the more so the more perfect the actions we perform and the more we understand God. Such a doctrine not only completely calms our spirit but also shows us where our highest happiness or blessedness lies, namely in the knowledge of God alone, by which we are led to act only as love and piety bid us. We may thus clearly see how far from a true estimate of virtue are those who expect to be decorated by God with high rewards for their virtue and their best actions, as though for having endured the direst slavery—as if virtue and the service of God were not in themselves happiness and perfect freedom.

2. Because it teaches us how we ought to conduct ourselves with respect to the gifts of fortune, or matters not in our power and not following from our own nature. For it shows us that we should await and endure fortune's smiles and frowns with an equal mind, seeing that everything follows from the eternal decree of God by the same necessity by which it follows from the essence of a triangle that its three angles are equal to two right angles.

3. This doctrine improves social life, since it teaches us to hate no one, to despise no one, to mock, envy or be angry with no one. Further, it tells us that each should be content with his own and helpful to his neighbour, not from any womanish pity, favour or superstition, but solely under the guidance of reason, as the time and occasion require, as I shall show in Part Three.

4. Last, this doctrine confers no small advantage on the commonwealth, for it teaches how citizens should be governed and led—not so as to become slaves, but so that they may freely do what is best.

I have now fulfilled the promise I made at the beginning of this note, and with it I bring the second part of my treatise to a close. I think I have explained the nature and properties of the human mind at sufficient length, and, considering the difficulty of the subject, with sufficient clearness. I have laid a foundation on which many excellent conclusions may be raised, of the highest use and most necessary to be known, as will in part be made plain in what follows.

Part Three: On the Origin and Nature of the Emotions

Preface, Definitions, Postulates, Propositions 1 to 17

Preface

Most writers on the emotions and on human conduct seem to be treating not of natural things that follow nature's general laws but of matters outside nature altogether. They appear to conceive man to be situated in nature as a kingdom within a kingdom: they believe that he disturbs rather than follows nature's order, that he has absolute control over his actions, and that he is determined by nothing but himself. They put human weakness and inconstancy down not to the power of nature in general but to some mysterious flaw in human nature, which they accordingly bewail, deride, despise or, as usually happens, abuse; and whoever succeeds in hitting off the weakness of the human mind more eloquently or more sharply than the rest is looked on as a seer. There has been no lack of very excellent men, to whose labour and industry I confess myself much indebted, who have written many admirable things about the right way of life and given mankind a great deal of wise advice. But no one, so far as I know, has defined the nature and strength of the emotions, or what the mind can do to restrain them.

I have not forgotten that the illustrious Descartes, though he believed the mind has absolute power over its own actions, tried to explain the human emotions by their first causes and at the same time to point out a way by which the mind might reach absolute dominion over them. But in my opinion he achieves nothing beyond a display of the sharpness of his own great intellect, as I shall show in the proper place. For the present I want to come back to those who would rather abuse or deride the human emotions than understand them. Such people will no doubt think it strange that I should undertake to treat of human vice and folly geometrically, and should want to set out by rigorous reasoning the very things they cry out against as contrary to reason, as trifling, absurd and horrifying. But this is my plan. Nothing happens in nature that can be set down to a flaw in nature; for nature is always the same, and everywhere one and the same in her power and capacity to act. That is, nature's laws and rules, by which everything happens and changes from one form to another, are everywhere and always the same; and so there must be one and the same way of understanding the nature of all things whatever, namely through nature's universal laws and rules. So the passions of hatred, anger and envy, considered in themselves, follow from this same necessity and power of nature; they answer to definite causes through which they are understood, and have properties as much worth knowing as the properties of anything else that gives us delight simply to contemplate. I shall therefore treat of the nature and strength of the emotions by the same method I used before in my investigations concerning God and the mind. I shall consider human actions and desires exactly as if I were concerned with lines, planes, and solids.

Definitions

1. By an adequate cause I mean a cause through which its effect can be clearly and distinctly perceived. By an inadequate, or partial, cause I mean a cause through which, taken by itself, its effect cannot be understood.

2. I say that we act when something takes place, within us or outside us, of which we are the adequate cause; that is (by Definition 1 of this Part), when through our nature something takes place within us or outside us which can be clearly and distinctly understood through our nature alone. On the other hand, I say that we are passive when something takes place within us, or follows from our nature outside us, of which we are only the partial cause.

3. By emotion I mean the modifications of the body by which the body's power of acting is increased or diminished, helped or checked, together with the ideas of those modifications.

Note. If we can be the adequate cause of any of these modifications, I then call the emotion an activity; otherwise I call it a passion, a state in which the mind is passive.

Postulates

1. The human body can be affected in many ways which increase or diminish its power of acting, and in other ways which make its power of acting neither greater nor less.

Note. This postulate, or axiom, rests on Postulate 1 of this Part and Part 2, Lemma 5 and Part 2, Lemma 7, which see after Part 2, Proposition 13.

2. The human body can undergo many changes and still retain the impressions, or traces, of objects (compare Part 2, Postulate 5), and consequently the same images of things (see Part 2, Proposition 17, Note).

Proposition 1

Our mind is in some cases active and in some cases passive. Insofar as it has adequate ideas it is necessarily active, and insofar as it has inadequate ideas it is necessarily passive.

Proof. In every human mind there are some adequate ideas and some that are fragmentary and confused (Part 2, Proposition 40, Note). Those ideas which are adequate in the mind are adequate also in God, insofar as he constitutes the essence of that mind (Part 2, Proposition 40, Corollary); and those which are inadequate in the mind are likewise (by Part 2, Proposition 40, Corollary) adequate in God—not insofar as he contains in himself the essence of that mind alone, but insofar as he contains, at the same time, the minds of other things as well. Again, from any given idea some effect must necessarily follow (Part 1, Proposition 36); and of that effect God is the adequate cause (Definition 1 of this Part), not insofar as he is infinite but insofar as he is conceived as affected by the given idea (Part 2, Proposition 9). But of the effect of which God is the cause insofar as he is affected by an idea which is adequate in a given mind, of that effect, I say, the mind in question is the adequate cause (Part 2, Proposition 11, Corollary). Therefore our mind, insofar as it has adequate ideas (Definition 2 of this Part), is in some cases necessarily active; that was our first point. Again, whatever necessarily follows from an idea which is adequate in God not by virtue of his having in himself the mind of one man only, but by virtue of his containing, along with that man's mind, the minds of other things too—of such an effect (Part 2, Proposition 11, Corollary) that man's mind is not an adequate but only a partial cause. So (Definition 2 of this Part) the mind, insofar as it has inadequate ideas, is in some cases necessarily passive; that was our second point. Therefore our mind, and so on. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows that the mind is the more liable to be acted upon the more inadequate ideas it has, and conversely is the more active the more adequate ideas it has.

Proposition 2

Body cannot determine mind to think, nor can mind determine body to motion or rest, or to anything else, if there is anything else.

Proof. All modes of thinking have God for their cause by virtue of his being a thinking thing, and not by virtue of his being displayed under any other attribute (Part 2, Proposition 6). What determines the mind to thought, therefore, is a mode of thought and not a mode of extension; that is (Part 2, Definition 1), it is not body. That was our first point. Again, the motion and rest of a body must arise from another body, which has itself been determined to motion or rest by a third; and absolutely everything that takes place in a body must spring from God insofar as he is regarded as affected by some mode of extension, and not by some mode of thought (Part 2, Proposition 6). That is, it cannot spring from the mind, which is a mode of thought. That was our second point. Therefore body cannot determine mind, and so on. Q.E.D.

Note. This is made clearer by what was said in the note to Part 2, Proposition 7, namely that mind and body are one and the same thing, conceived first under the attribute of thought and second under the attribute of extension. It follows that the order, or chain, of things is the same whether nature is conceived under one attribute or the other; and consequently that the order of activity and passivity in our body runs together in nature with the order of activity and passivity in the mind. The same conclusion is evident from the way we proved Part 2, Proposition 12.

Nevertheless, though this is the case and there is no further room for doubt, I can scarcely believe—until the fact is proved by experience—that men can be brought to consider the question calmly and fairly, so firmly are they convinced that it is at the mind's bidding alone that the body is set in motion or brought to rest, or performs any number of actions depending solely on the mind's will or its exercise of thought. Yet no one has so far laid down the limits of the body's powers; that is, no one has yet been taught by experience what the body can do by the laws of nature alone, insofar as nature is regarded as extension. No one so far has gained such accurate knowledge of the mechanism of the body that he can explain all its functions. Nor need I point out that many actions are observed in animals which far surpass human ingenuity, and that sleepwalkers do many things in their sleep that they would not dare to do awake. These instances are enough to show that the body can, by the laws of its own nature alone, do many things at which its own mind is amazed.

Again, no one knows how, or by what means, the mind moves the body, nor how many degrees of motion it can give the body, nor how fast it can move it. So when men say that this or that bodily action has its origin in the mind, which has dominion over the body, they are using words without meaning, or confessing in fine language that they do not know the cause of the action and are not troubled by their ignorance.

But, they will say, whether or not we know the means by which the mind acts on the body, we do at least have experience of the fact that unless the mind is fit to think, the body remains inert. And we have experience that the mind alone decides whether we speak or stay silent, along with a variety of similar things which we accordingly say depend on the mind's decree. As to the first point, I ask such objectors whether experience does not also teach that if the body is inactive the mind is at the same time unfit for thinking. When the body is at rest in sleep, the mind is at the same time in a state of torpor and has no power of thinking such as it has when the body is awake. And I think everyone's experience will confirm that the mind is not at all times equally fit for thinking on a given subject: as the body is more or less fit to be stimulated by the image of this or that object, so the mind is more or less fit to contemplate that object.

But it will be urged that from the laws of nature considered simply as extended substance we could never deduce the causes of buildings, of paintings, and of things of that kind, which are produced by human art alone; and that the human body, unless it were determined and guided by the mind, would not be capable of building a single temple. But I have just pointed out that the objectors cannot fix the limits of the body's power, or say what can be concluded from its nature alone, while they have experience of many things being done by the laws of nature alone which they would never have believed possible except under the direction of a mind—such as the actions sleepwalkers perform in their sleep, and marvel at when awake. I would further call attention to the mechanism of the human body, which far surpasses in complexity anything human art has put together—not to repeat what I have already shown, that from nature, under whatever attribute she is considered, infinite results follow. As for the second objection, I suggest that the world would be much happier if men were as able to keep silence as they are to speak. Experience shows abundantly that men can govern anything more easily than their tongues, and restrain anything more easily than their appetites. Hence it comes about that many believe we are free only in respect of things we desire moderately, because our desire for such things can easily be checked by the thought of something else often remembered, but that we are not free at all in respect of what we seek with violent emotion, since our desire then cannot be quieted by the remembrance of anything else. Yet if such people had not learned from experience that we do many things we afterwards repent of, and that we often, when torn by conflicting emotions, see the better and follow the worse, there would be nothing to stop them believing we are free in everything. So an infant believes it freely desires milk, an angry child believes it freely desires revenge, a timid child believes it freely desires to run away; a drunken man believes he utters from the free decision of his mind words he would rather have kept back when sober; and so a raving man, a chattering woman, a child and others of the same sort believe they speak from the free decision of their minds, when in fact they cannot restrain their impulse to talk. Experience teaches us no less clearly than reason that men believe themselves free simply because they are conscious of their actions and unaware of the causes by which those actions are determined; and it is plain, further, that the mind's decrees are only another name for the appetites, and therefore vary as the state of the body varies. Everyone shapes his actions by his emotions; those torn between conflicting emotions do not know what they want; those under no emotion at all are easily swayed this way or that. All this clearly shows that a decision of the mind and an appetite, or determined state, of the body are simultaneous—or rather are one and the same thing, which we call a decision when it is regarded under the attribute of thought and explained through it, and a determined state when it is regarded under the attribute of extension and deduced from the laws of motion and rest. This will be plainer still in what follows. For now I want to call attention to another point: that we cannot act by a decision of the mind unless we remember having done so. We cannot say a word, for instance, without remembering that we have. Again, it is not in the mind's free power to remember or forget a thing at will. So the mind's freedom must in any case be limited to the power of uttering or not uttering what it remembers. But when we dream that we speak, we believe that we speak from a free decision of the mind—and yet we do not speak, or if we do, it is by a spontaneous motion of the body. Again, we dream that we are concealing something, and we seem to act by the same decision of the mind by which, awake, we keep silent about something we know. Last, we dream that by a free decision of our mind we do something we should not dare to do awake.

Now I should like to know whether there are two sorts of decisions in the mind, one sort illusory and the other free. If our folly does not carry us that far, we must admit that the decision of the mind which is believed to be free is not distinguishable from imagination or memory, and is nothing more than the affirmation which an idea, by virtue of being an idea, necessarily involves (Part 2, Proposition 49). So these decisions of the mind arise in the mind by the same necessity as the ideas of things actually existing. And therefore those who believe that they speak, or keep silent, or act in any way by the free decision of their minds are dreaming with their eyes open.

Proposition 3

The activities of the mind arise solely from adequate ideas; its passive states depend solely on inadequate ideas.

Proof. The first element constituting the essence of the mind is nothing but the idea of the actually existing body (Part 2, Proposition 11; Part 2, Proposition 13), which (Part 2, Proposition 15) is composed of many other ideas, some of them adequate and some inadequate (Part 2, Proposition 29, Corollary; Part 2, Proposition 38, Corollary). Whatever therefore follows from the nature of the mind, and has the mind for its proximate cause through which it must be understood, must follow either from an adequate idea or from an inadequate one. But insofar as the mind (Proposition 1 of this Part) has inadequate ideas it is necessarily passive; so the activities of the mind follow solely from adequate ideas, and the mind is passive only insofar as it has inadequate ideas. Q.E.D.

Note. We see, then, that passive states are attributed to the mind only insofar as it contains something involving negation, or insofar as it is regarded as a part of nature which cannot be clearly and distinctly perceived through itself without the other parts. I could show in the same way that passive states are attributed to individual things just as they are to the mind, and cannot be perceived in any other way; but my purpose is only to treat of the human mind.

Proposition 4

Nothing can be destroyed except by a cause outside itself.

Proof. This proposition is self-evident. The definition of anything affirms the essence of that thing and does not deny it; it posits the thing's essence and does not take it away. So as long as we consider only the thing itself, without taking external causes into account, we shall not be able to find in it anything that could destroy it. Q.E.D.

Proposition 5

Things are naturally contrary—that is, cannot exist in the same subject—insofar as one is capable of destroying the other.

Proof. If they could agree together, or exist together in the same subject, then there would be in that subject something which could destroy it; but that, by the last proposition, is absurd. Therefore things, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 6

Everything, insofar as it is in itself, endeavours to persist in its own being.

Proof. Individual things are modes by which the attributes of God are expressed in a definite and determined way (Part 1, Proposition 25, Corollary); that is (Part 1, Proposition 34), they are things which express in a definite and determined way the power of God, by which God is and acts. Now nothing contains in itself anything by which it can be destroyed, or which can take away its existence (Proposition 4 of this Part); on the contrary, it is opposed to everything that could take away its existence (Proposition 5 of this Part). Therefore, so far as it can and so far as it is in itself, it endeavours to persist in its own being. Q.E.D.

Proposition 7

The endeavour by which each thing endeavours to persist in its own being is nothing but the actual essence of that thing.

Proof. From the given essence of anything certain consequences necessarily follow (Part 1, Proposition 36), and things have no power except what necessarily follows from their determined nature (Part 1, Proposition 29). So the power of any given thing, the endeavour by which it acts or tries to act, alone or with other things—that is (Proposition 6 of this Part), the power or endeavour by which it endeavours to persist in its own being—is nothing but the given, or actual, essence of the thing itself. Q.E.D.

Proposition 8

The endeavour by which a thing endeavours to persist in its own being involves no finite time, but an indefinite time.

Proof. If it involved a limited time, which fixed the thing's duration, then it would follow from the very power by which the thing exists that the thing could not exist beyond the limits of that time but must be destroyed; and that (Proposition 4 of this Part) is absurd. So the endeavour by which a thing exists involves no definite time. On the contrary, since (Proposition 4 of this Part) by the same power by which it already exists it will always continue to exist unless destroyed by some external cause, this endeavour involves an indefinite time.

Proposition 9

The mind, both insofar as it has clear and distinct ideas and insofar as it has confused ones, endeavours to persist in its being for an indefinite time, and is conscious of this endeavour.

Proof. The essence of the mind is constituted by adequate and inadequate ideas (Proposition 3 of this Part); therefore (Proposition 7 of this Part), both insofar as it has the one and insofar as it has the other, it endeavours to persist in its own being, and that for an indefinite time (Proposition 8 of this Part). And since the mind (Part 2, Proposition 23) is necessarily conscious of itself through the ideas of the modifications of the body, the mind is therefore (Proposition 7 of this Part) conscious of its own endeavour.

Note. This endeavour, when referred to the mind alone, is called will; when referred to mind and body together it is called appetite. Appetite is nothing but the very essence of man, from whose nature there necessarily follow the things that serve his preservation, and which he is determined by that nature to do.

Further, there is no difference between appetite and desire, except that desire is generally used of men insofar as they are conscious of their appetite, and may accordingly be defined thus: desire is appetite together with the consciousness of it. It is plain from all this that we do not strive for, wish for, long for or desire anything because we judge it to be good; on the contrary, we judge a thing to be good because we strive for it, wish for it, long for it, desire it.

Proposition 10

An idea which excludes the existence of our body cannot be in our mind, but is contrary to it.

Proof. Whatever can destroy our body cannot be in it (Proposition 5 of this Part). So neither can the idea of such a thing be in God insofar as he has the idea of our body (Part 2, Proposition 9, Corollary); that is (Part 2, Proposition 11; Part 2, Proposition 13), the idea of that thing cannot be in our mind. On the contrary, since (Part 2, Proposition 11; Part 2, Proposition 13) the first element constituting the essence of the mind is the idea of the human body as actually existing, it follows that the first and chief endeavour of our mind is the endeavour to affirm the existence of our body. So an idea which denies the existence of our body is contrary to our mind, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 11

Whatever increases or diminishes, helps or hinders our body's power of acting, the idea of it increases or diminishes, helps or hinders our mind's power of thinking.

Proof. This proposition is evident from Part 2, Proposition 7 or from Part 2, Proposition 14.

Note. We see, then, that the mind can undergo great changes, passing sometimes to a greater and sometimes to a lesser perfection. These passive transitions explain to us the emotions of pleasure and pain. By pleasure, therefore, in what follows I shall mean a passive state in which the mind passes to a greater perfection; by pain, a passive state in which the mind passes to a lesser perfection. Further, the emotion of pleasure referred to mind and body together I shall call stimulation (titillatio) or merriment (hilaritas); the emotion of pain in the same relation I shall call suffering or melancholy. But we must bear in mind that stimulation and suffering are attributed to a man when one part of his nature is affected more than the rest, merriment and melancholy when all the parts are affected alike. What I mean by desire I have explained in the note to Proposition 9 of this Part. Beyond these three I recognise no other primary emotion; I shall show as I go on that all the rest arise from these three. But before going further I should like to explain Proposition 10 of this Part at greater length, so that we may clearly understand how one idea is contrary to another. In the note to Part 2, Proposition 17 we showed that the idea constituting the essence of the mind involves the existence of the body so long as the body exists. Again, it follows from what we pointed out in the Corollary to Part 2, Proposition 8 that the present existence of our mind depends solely on the fact that the mind involves the actual existence of the body. And last, we showed (Part 2, Proposition 17; Part 2, Proposition 18, Note) that the mind's power of imagining and remembering things also depends on its involving the actual existence of the body. From which it follows that the present existence of the mind and its power of imagining are removed as soon as the mind ceases to affirm the present existence of the body. Now the cause why the mind ceases to affirm this existence of the body cannot be the mind itself (Proposition 4 of this Part), nor can it be the fact that the body ceases to exist. For (by Part 2, Proposition 6) the cause why the mind affirms the existence of the body is not that the body began to exist; so, for the same reason, it does not cease to affirm the body's existence because the body ceases to exist. Rather (Part 2, Proposition 17) this follows from another idea which excludes the present existence of our body, and consequently of our mind, and which is therefore contrary to the idea constituting the essence of our mind.

Proposition 12

The mind, as far as it can, endeavours to conceive those things which increase or help the body's power of acting.

Proof. So long as the human body is affected in a way that involves the nature of some external body, the human mind will regard that external body as present (Part 2, Proposition 17); and consequently (Part 2, Proposition 7), so long as the human mind regards an external body as present—that is (Part 2, Proposition 17, Note), conceives it—the human body is affected in a way that involves the nature of that external body. So, so long as the mind conceives things which increase or help our body's power of acting, the body is affected in ways which increase or help its power of acting (Postulate 1 of this Part); and consequently (Proposition 11 of this Part) the mind's power of thinking is for that time increased or helped. Therefore (Proposition 6 of this Part; Proposition 9 of this Part) the mind, as far as it can, endeavours to imagine such things. Q.E.D.

Proposition 13

When the mind conceives things which diminish or hinder the body's power of acting, it endeavours as far as it can to remember things which exclude their existence.

Proof. So long as the mind conceives anything of that kind, the power of mind and body is diminished or checked (compare Proposition 12 of this Part, Proof); and it will go on conceiving it until the mind conceives something else which excludes its present existence (Part 2, Proposition 17). That is, as I have just shown, the power of mind and body is diminished or checked until the mind conceives something else which excludes the existence of the first thing conceived. Therefore the mind (Proposition 9 of this Part), as far as it can, will endeavour to conceive or remember that other thing. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows that the mind shrinks from conceiving those things which diminish or check its own power and the body's.

Note. From what has been said we may clearly understand the nature of love and hate. Love is nothing but pleasure accompanied by the idea of an external cause; hate is nothing but pain accompanied by the idea of an external cause. We see, further, that a man who loves necessarily endeavours to have, and to keep with him, the object of his love, while a man who hates endeavours to remove and destroy the object of his hatred. But I shall treat of these matters at greater length later.

Proposition 14

If the mind has once been affected by two emotions at the same time, then whenever it is afterwards affected by one of them it will be affected by the other too.

Proof. If the human body has once been affected by two bodies at once, then whenever the mind afterwards conceives one of them it will at once remember the other as well (Part 2, Proposition 18). But the mind's conceptions indicate the emotions of our body rather than the nature of external bodies (Part 2, Proposition 16, Corollary 2). Therefore, if the body, and consequently the mind (Definition 3 of this Part), has once been affected by two emotions at the same time, then whenever it is afterwards affected by one of them it will be affected by the other too.

Proposition 15

Anything can accidentally be the cause of pleasure, pain or desire.

Proof. Suppose the mind is affected by two emotions at once, one of which neither increases nor diminishes its power of acting, while the other does increase or diminish it (Postulate 1 of this Part). It is evident from the last proposition that whenever the mind is afterwards affected by the first, through its true cause, which by hypothesis neither increases nor diminishes its power of acting, it will at the same time be affected by the second, which does increase or diminish that power—that is (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note), it will be affected with pleasure or pain. So the first of the two emotions will be the cause of pleasure or pain, not in itself, but accidentally. It can easily be shown in the same way that a thing may accidentally be the cause of desire. Q.E.D.

Corollary. Simply from having regarded a thing with the emotion of pleasure or pain, though that thing is not the efficient cause of the emotion, we can love it or hate it.

Proof. For from that fact alone it comes about (Proposition 14 of this Part) that the mind, afterwards conceiving that thing, is affected with the emotion of pleasure or pain—that is (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note), that the power of mind and body is increased or diminished, and so on; and consequently (Proposition 12 of this Part) that the mind desires or shrinks from conceiving it (Proposition 13 of this Part, Corollary); in other words (Proposition 13 of this Part, Note), that it loves or hates it. Q.E.D.

Note. From this we understand how it can happen that we love or hate a thing without knowing any cause for our emotion—merely, as the phrase is, from sympathy or antipathy. And we should put in the same class those objects which affect us pleasurably or painfully simply because they resemble other objects that affect us in the same way. This I will show in Proposition 16 of this Part. I am aware that the authors who first introduced the terms "sympathy" and "antipathy" meant by them certain occult qualities in things; but I think we may nevertheless use the same terms for known and manifest qualities.

Proposition 16

Simply from conceiving that a given object has some point of resemblance with another object which usually affects the mind with pleasure or pain, we shall regard the first object with love or hate, even though the point of resemblance is not the efficient cause of those emotions.

Proof. The point of resemblance was in the object (by hypothesis) when we regarded it with pleasure or pain; so (Proposition 14 of this Part), when the mind is affected by the image of it, the mind will at once be affected by one emotion or the other; and consequently the thing which we perceive to have that same point of resemblance will accidentally (Proposition 15 of this Part) be a cause of pleasure or pain. So (by Proposition 15 of this Part, Corollary), although the point in which the two objects resemble one another is not the efficient cause of the emotion, we shall still regard the first object with love or hate. Q.E.D.

Proposition 17

If we conceive that a thing which usually affects us with pain has some point of resemblance with another thing which usually affects us with an equally strong pleasure, we shall hate it and at the same time love it.

Proof. The thing in question is (by hypothesis) in itself a cause of pain, and (Proposition 13 of this Part, Note) insofar as we imagine it with that emotion we shall hate it. And insofar as we conceive that it has some point of resemblance to something else which usually affects us with an equally strong pleasure, we shall love it with an equally strong impulse of pleasure (Proposition 16 of this Part). So we shall both hate and love the same thing. Q.E.D.

Note. This state of mind, arising from two contrary emotions, is called vacillation. It stands to the emotions as doubt stands to the imagination (Part 2, Proposition 44, Note); vacillation and doubt differ only as the greater differs from the less. But we should bear in mind that I have deduced this vacillation from causes which give rise to one of the emotions through themselves and to the other accidentally. I have done so in order that they might be more easily deduced from what went before; but I do not deny that vacillation generally arises from an object which is the efficient cause of both emotions. The human body is composed (Part 2, Postulate 1) of a great many individual parts of different natures, and may therefore (Axiom 1, after Lemma 3, which follows Part 2, Proposition 13) be affected in a great many different ways by one and the same body; and conversely, since one and the same thing can be affected in many ways, it can also affect one and the same part of the body in many different ways. So we can easily conceive that one and the same object may be the cause of many and conflicting emotions.

Propositions 18 to 40

Proposition 18

A man is affected with pleasure or pain by the image of a thing past or future as much as by the image of a thing present.

Proof. So long as a man is affected by the image of anything, he will regard that thing as present even though it does not exist (Part 2, Proposition 17, Corollary), and will not conceive it as past or future except insofar as its image is joined to the image of time past or future (Part 2, Proposition 44, Note). So the image of a thing, regarded in itself alone, is the same whether it is referred to time past, time future or time present; that is (Part 2, Proposition 16, Corollary), the disposition, or emotion, of the body is the same whether the image is of a thing past, future or present. So the emotion of pleasure or pain is the same whether the image is of a thing past or future. Q.E.D.

Note 1. I call a thing past or future according as we have been or shall be affected by it: according as we have seen it or are about to see it, according as it has refreshed us or will refresh us, according as it has harmed us or will harm us. For in conceiving it so, we affirm its existence; that is, the body is affected by no emotion which excludes the thing's existence, and therefore (Part 2, Proposition 17) the body is affected by the image of the thing just as if the thing were actually present. However, since it generally happens that those who have had many experiences waver so long as they regard a thing as past or future, and are usually in doubt about the outcome (Part 2, Proposition 44, Note), it follows that the emotions arising from such images are not constant, but are generally disturbed by the images of other things until men become certain of the outcome.

Note 2. From what has just been said we understand what is meant by hope, fear, confidence, despair, joy and disappointment. Hope is nothing but an inconstant pleasure arising from the image of something past or future whose outcome we are unsure of. Fear is an inconstant pain, likewise arising from the image of something we are in doubt about. If the element of doubt is removed from these emotions, hope becomes confidence and fear becomes despair—in other words, the pleasure or pain arising from the image of something we had hoped for or feared. Joy, again, is the pleasure arising from the image of something past whose outcome we had doubted; disappointment is the pain opposed to joy.

Footnote: Conscientiæ morsus—thus rendered by Mr. Pollock.

Proposition 19

He who conceives that the object of his love is destroyed will feel pain; if he conceives that it is preserved, he will feel pleasure.

Proof. The mind endeavours as far as it can to conceive those things which increase or help the body's power of acting (Proposition 12 of this Part); in other words (Proposition 12 of this Part, Note), the things it loves. But conception is helped by whatever posits the existence of a thing and hindered by whatever excludes it (Part 2, Proposition 17). Therefore the images of things which posit the existence of the object of love help the mind's endeavour to conceive that object—in other words (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note), affect the mind with pleasure; while those which exclude the existence of the object of love hinder that endeavour, and so affect the mind with pain. He, therefore, who conceives that the object of his love is destroyed will feel pain, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 20

He who conceives that the object of his hate is destroyed will feel pleasure.

Proof. The mind (Proposition 13 of this Part) endeavours to conceive those things which exclude the existence of whatever diminishes or checks the body's power of acting; that is (Proposition 13 of this Part, Note), it endeavours to conceive things which exclude the existence of what it hates. So the image of a thing which excludes the existence of what the mind hates helps that endeavour—in other words (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note), affects the mind with pleasure. Thus he who conceives that the object of his hate is destroyed will feel pleasure. Q.E.D.

Proposition 21

He who conceives that the object of his love is affected with pleasure or pain will himself be affected with pleasure or pain; and the emotion will be greater or less in the lover according as it is greater or less in the thing loved.

Proof. The images of things which posit the existence of the object of love (as we showed in Proposition 19 of this Part) help the mind's endeavour to conceive that object. But pleasure posits the existence of the thing feeling it, and the more so the greater the pleasure, since it is (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note) a transition to a greater perfection. So the image of pleasure in the object of love helps the lover's mental endeavour—that is, affects the lover with pleasure, and the more so the greater that emotion was in the object of love. That was our first point. Further, insofar as a thing is affected with pain it is to that extent destroyed, the extent being proportional to the pain (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note); therefore (Proposition 19 of this Part) he who conceives that the object of his love is affected with pain will himself be affected with pain, in proportion as that emotion is greater or less in the object of his love. Q.E.D.

Proposition 22

If we conceive that someone affects with pleasure an object of our love, we shall be affected with love towards him; if we conceive that he affects it with pain, we shall be affected with hatred towards him.

Proof. He who affects the object of our love with pleasure or pain affects us also with pleasure or pain—that is, if we conceive the loved object as affected with that pleasure or pain (Proposition 21 of this Part). But this pleasure or pain is supposed to come to us accompanied by the idea of an external cause; therefore (Proposition 13 of this Part, Note), if we conceive that someone affects an object of our love with pleasure or pain, we shall be affected with love or hatred towards him. Q.E.D.

Note. Proposition 21 of this Part explains the nature of pity, which we may define as pain arising from another's hurt. What term to use for pleasure arising from another's good, I do not know.

We will call the love towards someone who benefits another approval, and the hatred towards someone who injures another indignation. And we should note further that we feel pity not only for a thing we have loved (as shown in Proposition 21 of this Part) but also for one we have hitherto regarded without any emotion, provided we judge it to resemble ourselves. So we bestow approval on anyone who has benefited something resembling ourselves, and are indignant with anyone who has injured it.

Proposition 23

He who conceives that an object of his hatred is affected with pain will feel pleasure; and if he thinks it is affected with pleasure, he will feel pain. Each of these emotions will be greater or less according as its contrary is greater or less in the object of hatred.

Proof. Insofar as an object of hatred is affected with pain it is destroyed, to an extent proportional to the pain (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note). Therefore he (Proposition 20 of this Part) who conceives that an object of his hatred is affected with pain will feel pleasure, to an extent proportional to the pain he conceives in that object. That was our first point. Again, pleasure posits the existence of the thing affected with it (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note), in proportion as the pleasure is greater or less. So if anyone imagines that an object of his hatred is affected with pleasure, this conception (Proposition 13 of this Part) will hinder his own endeavour to persist; in other words (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note), the hater will be affected with pain. Q.E.D.

Note. This pleasure can scarcely be felt unmixed, and without some conflict of mind. For (as I am about to show in Proposition 27 of this Part), insofar as a man conceives that something like himself is affected with pain, he will be affected in the same way himself; and he will have the contrary emotion in contrary circumstances. But here we are considering hatred only.

Proposition 24

If we conceive that someone affects an object of our hate with pleasure, we shall feel hatred towards him too; if we conceive that he affects it with pain, we shall feel love towards him.

Proof. This proposition is proved in the same way as Proposition 22 of this Part, which see.

Note. These and similar emotions of hatred belong to envy, which accordingly is nothing but hatred insofar as it is regarded as disposing a man to rejoice in another's hurt and to grieve at another's good.

Proposition 25

We endeavour to affirm, of ourselves and of what we love, everything we conceive to affect ourselves or the loved object with pleasure; and we endeavour to deny everything we conceive to affect ourselves or the loved object with pain.

Proof. What we conceive to affect an object of our love with pleasure or pain affects us also with pleasure or pain (Proposition 21 of this Part). But the mind (Proposition 12 of this Part) endeavours as far as it can to conceive the things which affect us with pleasure—in other words (Part 2, Proposition 17, Corollary), it endeavours to regard them as present; and conversely (Proposition 13 of this Part) it endeavours to exclude the existence of the things which affect us with pain. Therefore we endeavour to affirm of ourselves, and of the loved object, whatever we conceive to affect ourselves or the loved object with pleasure. Q.E.D.

Proposition 26

We endeavour to affirm, of what we hate, everything we conceive to affect it with pain; and conversely to deny of it everything we conceive to affect it with pleasure.

Proof. This proposition follows from Proposition 23 of this Part as the previous one followed from Proposition 21 of this Part.

Note. We see from this that it can easily happen that a man thinks too highly of himself, or of an object he loves, and too meanly of an object he hates. This feeling, when a man thinks too highly of himself, is called pride, and is a kind of madness in which a man dreams with his eyes open, thinking he can do everything that falls within his imagination, and taking those things for real, and exulting in them, so long as he cannot conceive anything which excludes their existence and limits his own power of acting. Pride, then, is pleasure arising from a man's thinking too highly of himself. The pleasure arising from thinking too highly of another is called over-esteem; and the pleasure arising from thinking too little of a man is called disdain.

Proposition 27

By the very fact of conceiving that a thing like ourselves, which we have regarded with no emotion, is affected with some emotion, we are ourselves affected with a like emotion (affectus).

Proof. The images of things are modifications of the human body whose ideas represent external bodies to us as present (Part 2, Proposition 17); in other words (Part 2, Proposition 10), whose ideas involve the nature of our body and at the same time the nature of the external bodies as present. If, therefore, the nature of the external body is like the nature of our body, then the idea we form of that external body will involve a modification of our own body like the modification of the external body. Consequently, if we conceive someone like ourselves as affected by some emotion, this conception will express a modification of our body like that emotion. So by the very fact of conceiving a thing like ourselves to be affected with some emotion, we are affected with a like emotion ourselves. If, however, we hate that thing like ourselves, then to that extent we shall be affected by a contrary emotion, not a like one. Q.E.D.

Note 1. This imitation of the emotions, when it is referred to pain, is called compassion (compare Proposition 22 of this Part, Note); when it is referred to desire, it is called emulation, which is nothing but the desire of a thing engendered in us by our conceiving that others have the same desire.

Corollary 1. If we conceive that someone we have hitherto regarded with no emotion affects with pleasure something like ourselves, we shall be affected with love towards him. If, on the other hand, we conceive that he affects it with pain, we shall be affected with hatred towards him.

Proof. This is proved from the last proposition in the same way as Proposition 22 of this Part is proved from Proposition 21 of this Part.

Corollary 2. We cannot hate a thing we pity, because its misery affects us with pain.

Proof. If we could hate it for that reason, we should rejoice in its pain, which is contrary to the hypothesis.

Corollary 3. We seek to free from misery, as far as we can, a thing we pity.

Proof. What affects the object of our pity with pain affects us also with a like pain (by Proposition 26 of this Part). So we shall endeavour to recall everything which removes its existence, or destroys it (compare Proposition 13 of this Part); in other words (Proposition 9 of this Part, Note), we shall desire to destroy it, or shall be determined to destroy it. So we shall endeavour to free from misery a thing we pity. Q.E.D.

Note 2. This will, or appetite, for doing good, arising from pity for the thing we would benefit, is called benevolence, and is nothing but desire arising from compassion. As for love or hatred towards someone who has done good or harm to something we conceive to be like ourselves, see Proposition 22 of this Part, Note.

Proposition 28

We endeavour to bring about whatever we conceive to conduce to pleasure; and we endeavour to remove or destroy whatever we conceive to be truly opposed to it, or to conduce to pain.

Proof. We endeavour as far as we can to conceive what we imagine conduces to pleasure (Proposition 12 of this Part); in other words (Part 2, Proposition 17), we shall endeavour as far as we can to conceive it as present, or actually existing. But the endeavour of the mind, its power of thinking, is equal to and simultaneous with the endeavour of the body, its power of acting (this is clear from Part 2, Proposition 7, Corollary, and Part 2, Proposition 11, Corollary). So we make an absolute endeavour for its existence—in other words (Proposition 9 of this Part, Note), we desire it and strive for it. That was our first point. Again, if we conceive that something we believed to be a cause of pain—that is (Proposition 13 of this Part, Note), something we hate—is destroyed, we shall rejoice (Proposition 20 of this Part). So we shall endeavour (by the first part of this proof) to destroy it, or (Proposition 13 of this Part) to remove it from us, so that we may not regard it as present. That was our second point. Therefore whatever conduces to pleasure, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 29

We shall also endeavour to do whatever we conceive men to regard with pleasure, and shall shrink from doing what we conceive men to shrink from.

Footnote: By "men" in this and the following propositions, I mean men whom we regard without any particular emotion.

Proof. From imagining that men love or hate something, we shall love or hate the same thing (Proposition 27 of this Part); that is (Proposition 13 of this Part, Note), from that fact alone we shall feel pleasure or pain at the thing's presence. And so we shall endeavour to do whatever we conceive men to love or regard with pleasure, and so on. Q.E.D.

Note. This endeavour to do a thing or leave it undone solely in order to please men we call ambition, especially when we try so eagerly to please the crowd that we do or leave undone certain things to our own or another's hurt; otherwise it is generally called kindliness. Further, I give the name of praise to the pleasure with which we conceive another's action by which he has tried to please us, and of blame to the pain with which we regard his action with aversion.

Proposition 30

If a man has done something which he conceives affects other men with pleasure, he will be affected with pleasure accompanied by the idea of himself as its cause; in other words, he will regard himself with pleasure. If he has done something which he conceives affects others with pain, he will regard himself with pain.

Proof. He who conceives that he affects others with pleasure or pain will by that very fact be affected with pleasure or pain himself (Proposition 27 of this Part). And since a man (Part 2, Proposition 19; Part 2, Proposition 23) is conscious of himself through the modifications by which he is determined to act, it follows that he who conceives that he affects others with pleasure will be affected with pleasure accompanied by the idea of himself as cause—in other words, he will regard himself with pleasure. And so, with the necessary changes, in the case of pain. Q.E.D.

Note. Since love (Proposition 13 of this Part) is pleasure accompanied by the idea of an external cause, and hatred is pain accompanied by the idea of an external cause, the pleasure and pain in question will be a kind of love and hatred. But since the terms love and hatred are used of external objects, we will use other names for the emotions now under discussion: pleasure accompanied by the idea of an external cause we will call honour, and the emotion contrary to it shame—I mean in cases where the pleasure or pain arises from a man's belief that he is being praised or blamed. Otherwise, pleasure accompanied by the idea of an external cause is called self-approval, and the pain contrary to it repentance. Again, since it may happen (Part 2, Proposition 17, Corollary) that the pleasure with which a man conceives that he affects others exists only in his own imagination, and since (Proposition 25 of this Part) everyone endeavours to conceive of himself whatever he conceives will affect him with pleasure, it may easily come about that a vain man is proud and imagines himself pleasing to everyone when in fact he is an annoyance to everyone.

Footnote: So Van Vloten and Bruder. The Dutch version and Camerer read, "an internal cause." "Honor" = Gloria.

Footnote: See previous footnote.

Proposition 31

If we conceive that someone loves, desires or hates a thing which we ourselves love, desire or hate, we shall on that account regard the thing with a more settled love, and so on. If, on the other hand, we think that someone shrinks from what we love, we shall waver.

Proof. From merely conceiving that someone loves a thing, we shall ourselves love that thing (Proposition 27 of this Part); but we are supposed to love it already, so there is a new cause of love by which our former emotion is fed, and we shall therefore love it more steadily. Again, from merely conceiving that someone shrinks from a thing, we shall ourselves shrink from it (Proposition 27 of this Part); and if we suppose that at the same time we love it, we shall love it and shrink from it at once—in other words, we shall waver (Proposition 17 of this Part, Note). Q.E.D.

Corollary. From this, and from Proposition 28 of this Part, it follows that everyone endeavours as far as he can to make others love what he loves and hate what he hates; as the poet says: "As lovers let us share every hope and every fear: ironhearted were he who should love what the other leaves."

Footnote: Ovid, "Amores," II. xix. 4,5. Spinoza transposes the verses. "Speremus pariter, pariter metuamus amantes; Ferreus est, si quis, quod sinit alter, amat."

	Speremus pariter, pariter metuamus amantes;
	Ferreus est, si quis, quod sinit alter, amat.

Note. This endeavour to make our own likes and dislikes meet with universal approval is really ambition (see Proposition 29 of this Part, Note). So we see that everyone by nature desires that the rest of mankind should live according to his own individual temper; and when such a desire is equally present in all, everyone stands in everyone else's way, and in wanting to be loved or praised by all, all become hateful to one another.

Proposition 32

If we conceive that someone takes delight in something which only one person can possess, we shall endeavour to bring it about that he does not get possession of it.

Proof. From merely conceiving that another person takes delight in a thing (Proposition 27 of this Part, Corollary), we shall ourselves love that thing and desire to take delight in it. But we supposed that our pleasure in it would be prevented by his delight in it; so we shall endeavour to prevent his possessing it (Proposition 28 of this Part). Q.E.D.

Note. We see, then, that men are generally so constituted that they pity those who fare badly and envy those who fare well, with a hatred proportional to their own love of the goods those others possess. Further, we see that from the same property of human nature which makes men merciful it also follows that they are envious and ambitious. And last, if we appeal to experience, we shall find that she entirely confirms what we have said, especially if we turn our attention to the first years of our life. We find that children, whose bodies are continually as it were in equilibrium, laugh or cry simply because they see others laughing or crying; they want at once to imitate whatever they see others doing, and to have for themselves whatever they conceive as delighting others—since the images of things are, as we have said, modifications of the human body, ways in which the human body is affected and disposed by external causes to act in this way or that.

Proposition 33

When we love a thing like ourselves, we endeavour as far as we can to make it love us in return.

Proof. What we love we endeavour as far as we can to conceive in preference to anything else (Proposition 12 of this Part). If the thing is like ourselves, we shall endeavour to affect it with pleasure in preference to anything else (Proposition 29 of this Part); in other words, we shall endeavour as far as we can to bring it about that the thing is affected with pleasure accompanied by the idea of ourselves—that is (Proposition 13 of this Part, Note), that it loves us in return. Q.E.D.

Proposition 34

The greater the emotion with which we conceive a loved object to be affected towards us, the greater will be our self-approval.

Proof. We endeavour (Proposition 33 of this Part), as far as we can, to make what we love love us in return; in other words, to make what we love be affected with pleasure accompanied by the idea of ourselves as cause. So in proportion as the loved object is affected with more pleasure because of us, our endeavour will be helped—that is (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note), the greater will be our pleasure. But when we take pleasure in the fact that we affect something like ourselves with pleasure, we regard ourselves with pleasure (Proposition 30 of this Part). Therefore the greater the emotion with which we conceive a loved object to be affected, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 35

If anyone conceives that an object of his love is joining itself to another with closer bonds of friendship than he has himself attained, he will be affected with hatred towards the loved object and with envy towards his rival.

Proof. In proportion as a man thinks a loved object is well disposed towards him, so will be the strength of his self-approval (by Proposition 34 of this Part), that is (Proposition 30 of this Part, Note), of his pleasure. He will therefore (Proposition 28 of this Part) endeavour as far as he can to imagine the loved object as bound to him as closely as possible; and this endeavour, or desire, will be increased if he thinks someone else has a like desire (Proposition 31 of this Part). But this endeavour, or desire, is supposed to be checked by the image of the loved object together with the image of the man it has joined itself to; therefore (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note) he will for that reason be affected with pain accompanied by the idea of the loved object as cause together with the image of his rival. That is, he will be (Proposition 13 of this Part) affected with hatred towards the loved object and towards his rival too (Proposition 15 of this Part, Corollary), whom he will envy for enjoying the beloved object. Q.E.D.

Note. This hatred towards an object of love, joined with envy, is called jealousy, which accordingly is nothing but a wavering of temper arising from love and hatred together, accompanied by the idea of a rival who is envied. Further, this hatred towards the object of love will be greater in proportion to the pleasure the jealous man used to derive from that object's returned love, and also in proportion to the feelings he had previously had towards his rival. If he had hated him, he will at once hate the object of his love, because he conceives it as affected with pleasure by someone he himself hates, and also because he is forced to associate the image of the one he loves with the image of the one he hates. This generally comes into play in the case of love for a woman; for a man who thinks that a woman he loves gives herself to another will feel pain not only because his own desire is checked, but also because, being forced to associate the image of the woman he loves with the private parts and the excretions of another, he shrinks from her.

We must add that a jealous man is not greeted by his beloved with the same glad face as before, and this too gives him pain as a lover, as I shall now show.

Proposition 36

He who remembers a thing in which he once took delight desires to possess it in the same circumstances as when he first took delight in it.

Proof. Everything a man has seen along with the object of his love will be to him accidentally a cause of pleasure (Proposition 15 of this Part); so he will desire to possess it together with the thing he took delight in—in other words, he will desire to possess the object of his love in the same circumstances as when he first took delight in it. Q.E.D.

Corollary. A lover will therefore feel pain if one of those attendant circumstances is missing.

Proof. Insofar as he finds a circumstance missing, he conceives something which excludes its existence; and since he is supposed to desire that thing or circumstance for love's sake (by Proposition 35 of this Part), he will, insofar as he conceives it to be missing, feel pain (Proposition 19 of this Part). Q.E.D.

Note. This pain, insofar as it has to do with the absence of the object of love, is called regret.

Proposition 37

Desire arising from pain or pleasure, hatred or love, is greater in proportion as the emotion is greater.

Proof. Pain diminishes or checks a man's power of acting (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note); in other words (Proposition 7 of this Part), it diminishes or checks the endeavour by which he endeavours to persist in his own being.
