So (Proposition 5 of this Part) it is contrary to that endeavour, and all the endeavours of a man affected with pain are directed to removing that pain. But (by the definition of pain) in proportion as the pain is greater, so is it necessarily opposed to a greater part of a man's power of acting; therefore the greater the pain, the greater the power of acting employed to remove it—that is, the greater the desire or appetite to remove it. Again, since pleasure (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note) increases or helps a man's power of acting, it may easily be shown in the same way that a man affected with pleasure desires nothing but to keep it, and that his desire is proportional to the greatness of the pleasure.

And last, since hatred and love are themselves emotions of pain and pleasure, it follows in the same way that the endeavour, appetite or desire arising from hatred or love will be greater in proportion to the hatred or love. Q.E.D.

Proposition 38

If a man has begun to hate an object of his love, so that his love is entirely destroyed, he will, other causes being equal, regard it with more hatred than if he had never loved it, and his hatred will be proportional to the strength of his former love.

Proof. If a man begins to hate what he had loved, more of his appetites are checked than if he had never loved it. For love is a pleasure (Proposition 13 of this Part, Note) which a man endeavours as far as he can to make lasting (Proposition 28 of this Part); he does so by regarding the object of his love as present and by affecting it with pleasure as far as he can. This endeavour is greater in proportion as the love is greater, and so is the endeavour to make the beloved return his affection (Proposition 33 of this Part). Now these endeavours are checked by hatred towards the object of love (Proposition 13 of this Part, Corollary; Proposition 23 of this Part); so the lover (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note) will for this reason also be affected with pain, the more so in proportion as his love was greater. That is, besides the pain caused by hatred there is a pain caused by his having loved the object; and so the lover will regard the beloved with greater pain—in other words, will hate it more than if he had never loved it, and the more intensely in proportion as his former love was greater. Q.E.D.

Proposition 39

He who hates someone will endeavour to injure him, unless he fears a greater injury to himself as a result; and conversely he who loves someone will by the same law seek to benefit him.

Proof. To hate a man is (Proposition 13 of this Part, Note) to conceive him as a cause of pain; so he who hates a man will endeavour to remove or destroy him. But if something more painful—a greater evil, that is—would come to the hater as a result, and if he thinks he can avoid that evil by not carrying out the injury he had planned against the object of his hate, then he will desire to refrain from inflicting it (Proposition 28 of this Part); and the strength of this endeavour (Proposition 37 of this Part) will be greater than his earlier endeavour to injure, and will therefore prevail over it, as we said. The second part of the proof proceeds in the same way. Therefore he who hates another, and so on. Q.E.D.

Note. By good I mean here every kind of pleasure and everything that conduces to it, especially whatever satisfies our longings, of whatever kind. By evil I mean every kind of pain, especially whatever frustrates our longings. For I have shown (Proposition 9 of this Part, Note) that we never desire a thing because we judge it good; on the contrary, we judge a thing good because we desire it, and judge evil what we shrink from. So everyone, according to his own emotions, judges or estimates what is good, what is bad, what is better, what is worse, and last what is best and what is worst. A miser thinks abundance of money the best thing and want of money the worst; an ambitious man desires nothing so much as glory and fears nothing so much as shame; to an envious man nothing is more delightful than another's misfortune and nothing more painful than another's success. So every man, according to his emotions, judges a thing good or bad, useful or useless. The emotion which makes a man turn from what he wishes, or wish for what he turns from, is called timidity, which may accordingly be defined as the fear by which a man is led to avoid an evil he regards as coming by meeting a lesser evil (Proposition 28 of this Part). But if the evil he fears is shame, timidity becomes bashfulness. And last, if the desire to avoid a future evil is checked by the fear of another, so that the man does not know which to choose, fear becomes consternation, especially if both the evils feared are very great.

Proposition 40

He who conceives himself to be hated by another, and believes he has given him no cause for hatred, will hate that other in return.

Proof. He who conceives another as affected with hatred will thereupon be affected with hatred himself (Proposition 27 of this Part)—that is, with pain accompanied by the idea of an external cause. But by hypothesis he conceives no cause for this pain except the man who is his enemy; so from conceiving that he is hated by someone, he will be affected with pain accompanied by the idea of his enemy—in other words, he will hate his enemy in return. Q.E.D.

Note. He who thinks he has given just cause for hatred will (Proposition 30 of this Part, Note) be affected with shame; but this (Proposition 25 of this Part) rarely happens. This return of hatred may also arise from the hatred which follows an endeavour to injure the object of our hate (Proposition 39 of this Part). So he who conceives that he is hated by another will conceive his enemy as the cause of some evil or pain; and so will be affected with pain, or fear, accompanied by the idea of his enemy as cause—in other words, he will be affected with hatred towards his enemy, as I said above.

Corollary 1. He who conceives that one he loves hates him will be torn between hatred and love. For insofar as he conceives himself to be an object of hatred, he is determined to hate his enemy in return; but by hypothesis he nevertheless loves him. So he will be torn between hatred and love.

Corollary 2. If a man conceives that someone he has hitherto regarded without emotion has done him some injury out of hatred, he will at once seek to repay the injury in kind.

Proof. He who conceives that another hates him will (by Proposition 39 of this Part) hate his enemy in return, and (Proposition 26 of this Part) will endeavour to recall everything which can affect him with pain; and he will further endeavour to injure him (Proposition 39 of this Part). Now the first thing of this kind he conceives is the injury done to himself; so he will at once endeavour to repay it in kind. Q.E.D.

Note. The endeavour to injure one we hate is called anger; the endeavour to repay in kind an injury done to ourselves is called revenge.

Propositions 41 to 59

Proposition 41

If anyone conceives that he is loved by another, and believes he has given no cause for such love, he will love that other in return (compare Proposition 15 of this Part, Corollary; Proposition 16 of this Part).

Proof. This proposition is proved in the same way as the one before it. See also the note added to it.

Note. If he believes he has given just cause for the love, he will take pride in it (Proposition 30 of this Part, Note); and this is what most often happens (Proposition 25 of this Part), whereas we said the contrary took place whenever a man conceives himself to be hated by another (see Proposition 41 of this Part). This returned love, and consequently the desire to benefit one who loves us (Proposition 39 of this Part) and who endeavours to benefit us, is called gratitude or thankfulness. From which it appears that men are far readier to take revenge than to return a benefit.

Corollary. He who imagines that he is loved by one he hates will be torn between hatred and love. This is proved in the same way as the first corollary of the previous proposition.

Note. If hatred is the stronger emotion, he will endeavour to injure the man who loves him; and this emotion is called cruelty, especially if the victim is believed to have given no ordinary cause for hatred.

Proposition 42

He who has done someone a kindness out of love or the hope of honour will feel pain if he sees the kindness received ungratefully.

Proof. When a man loves something like himself, he endeavours as far as he can to make it love him in return (Proposition 33 of this Part). So a man who does a kindness does it out of the desire he feels to be loved in return—that is (Proposition 34 of this Part), out of the hope of honour, or (Proposition 30 of this Part, Note) of pleasure. He will therefore endeavour as far as he can to conceive this cause of honour, or to regard it as actually existing. But by hypothesis he conceives something else which excludes the existence of that cause of honour; so he will feel pain at it (Proposition 19 of this Part). Q.E.D.

Proposition 43

Hatred is increased by being returned, and can on the other hand be destroyed by love.

Proof. He who conceives that an object of his hatred hates him in return will thereupon feel a new hatred, while the former hatred (by hypothesis) still remains (Proposition 40 of this Part). But if instead he conceives that the object of his hate loves him, he will to that extent (Proposition 38 of this Part) regard himself with pleasure, and (Proposition 29 of this Part) will endeavour to please the cause of that emotion—in other words, he will endeavour not to hate him (Proposition 41 of this Part) and not to affect him with pain. This endeavour (Proposition 37 of this Part) will be greater or less in proportion to the emotion it arises from; so if it is greater than the endeavour arising from hatred, by which he tries to affect with pain the thing he hates, it will get the better of it and drive the hatred from his mind. Q.E.D.

Proposition 44

Hatred completely conquered by love passes into love; and the love is then greater than if hatred had not gone before it.

Proof. The proof runs as in Proposition 38 of this Part. For a man who begins to love a thing he used to hate, or to regard with pain, feels pleasure by the very act of loving; and to this pleasure involved in love is added the pleasure arising from the help given to his endeavour to remove the pain involved in hatred (Proposition 37 of this Part), accompanied by the idea of the former object of hatred as its cause.

Note. Even so, no one will endeavour to hate a thing, or to be affected with pain, for the sake of enjoying this greater pleasure. That is, no one will wish to be injured in the hope of recovering from the injury, or long to be ill for the sake of getting well. For everyone will always endeavour to persist in his being and to keep off pain as far as he can. If the contrary were conceivable—if a man could desire to hate someone in order to love him the more afterwards—he would always desire to go on hating him. For the strength of the love is proportional to the strength of the hatred, so the man would want the hatred to grow greater and greater; and for the same reason he would want to grow more and more ill in order to take a greater pleasure in being restored to health. So he would always endeavour to be ill, which (Proposition 6 of this Part) is absurd.

Proposition 45

If a man conceives that someone like himself hates a thing like himself which he loves, he will hate that person.

Proof. The beloved object returns the hatred of the man who hates it (Proposition 40 of this Part). So the lover, in conceiving that someone hates the beloved object, conceives the beloved thing as affected with hatred—in other words (Proposition 13 of this Part), with pain. Consequently he is himself affected with pain accompanied by the idea of the one who hates the beloved thing as its cause; that is, he will hate the man who hates what he himself loves (Proposition 13 of this Part, Note). Q.E.D.

Proposition 46

If a man has been affected with pleasure or pain by someone of a class or nation different from his own, and if the pleasure or pain was accompanied by the idea of that stranger as its cause under the general heading of his class or nation, the man will feel love or hatred not only towards that individual but towards the whole class or nation he belongs to.

Proof. This is evident from Proposition 16 of this Part.

Proposition 47

The joy arising from the destruction or other injury of something we hate is never unmixed with a certain pain in ourselves.

Proof. This is evident from Proposition 27 of this Part. For insofar as we conceive a thing like ourselves to be affected with pain, we feel pain ourselves.

Note. This proposition can also be proved from the Corollary to Part 2, Proposition 17. Whenever we remember anything, even if it does not actually exist, we regard it only as present, and the body is affected in the same way. So insofar as the memory of the thing is strong, a man is determined to regard it with pain; and this determination, while the image of the thing lasts, is checked by the memory of other things which exclude that thing's existence, but is not destroyed. A man therefore feels pleasure only insofar as that determination is checked; and this is why the joy arising from an injury done to what we hate is repeated every time we remember the object of our hatred. For, as we have said, when the image of the thing is roused, it involves the thing's existence and so determines the man to regard it with the same pain as he used to when it did actually exist. But since he has joined to the image of the thing other images which exclude its existence, this determination to pain is at once checked, and the man rejoices afresh each time it happens. This is why men take pleasure in recalling past evils, and delight in telling of dangers they have escaped: when men conceive a danger, they conceive it as still to come and are determined to fear it, and this determination is checked afresh by the idea of the freedom that came to be associated with the idea of the danger when they escaped it. This makes them feel safe again, and so they rejoice again.

Proposition 48

Love or hatred towards, say, Peter is destroyed if the pleasure involved in the one, or the pain involved in the other, comes to be associated with the idea of another cause; and it will be diminished in proportion as we conceive Peter not to have been the sole cause of the emotion.

Proof. This proposition is evident from the mere definition of love and hatred (Proposition 13 of this Part, Note). For pleasure is called love towards Peter, and pain is called hatred towards Peter, simply insofar as Peter is regarded as the cause of the one emotion or the other. When this relation of cause is wholly or partly removed, the emotion towards Peter wholly or partly vanishes too. Q.E.D.

Proposition 49

Love or hatred towards a thing we conceive to be free must, other things being equal, be greater than towards a thing acting by necessity.

Proof. A thing we conceive as free must (Part 1, Definition 7) be perceived through itself, without anything else. So if we conceive it as the cause of pleasure or pain, we shall (Proposition 13 of this Part, Note) love it or hate it, and shall do so with the utmost love or hatred that can arise from the emotion in question. But if the thing that causes the emotion is conceived as acting by necessity, we shall (by Part 1, Definition 7) conceive it not as the sole cause but as one of the causes of the emotion, and our love or hatred towards it will therefore be less. Q.E.D.

Note. It follows that men, thinking themselves free, feel more love and hatred towards one another than towards anything else; and to this we must add the imitation of the emotions treated of in Proposition 27 of this Part, Proposition 34 of this Part, Proposition 40 of this Part and Proposition 43 of this Part.

Proposition 50

Anything whatever can accidentally be a cause of hope or fear.

Proof. This proposition is proved in the same way as Proposition 15 of this Part, which see, together with the note to Proposition 18 of this Part.

Note. Things which are accidentally the causes of hope or fear are called good or bad omens. And insofar as such omens are the cause of hope or fear, they are (by Proposition 18 of this Part, Note) causes of pleasure and pain as well; so to that extent we regard them with love or hatred, and endeavour either to invoke them as means to what we hope for, or to remove them as obstacles, or as causes of what we fear. It further follows, from Proposition 25 of this Part, that we are naturally so constituted that we believe readily what we hope for and with difficulty what we fear, and that we are apt to value such things above or below their true worth. This is where the superstitions come from which harass men everywhere. However, I do not think it worth pointing out here the waverings that spring from hope and fear; it follows from the definition of these emotions that there can be no hope without fear and no fear without hope, as I shall explain in the proper place. Further, insofar as we hope for or fear anything, we regard it with love or hatred; so anyone can apply to hope and fear for himself what we have said about love and hatred.

Proposition 51

Different men may be affected differently by the same object, and the same man may be affected differently at different times by the same object.

Proof. The human body is affected by external bodies in a great variety of ways (Part 2, Postulate 3). Two men may therefore be affected differently at the same time, and therefore (by Axiom 1, after Lemma 3, which follows Part 2, Proposition 13) may be affected differently by one and the same object. Further (by Part 2, Postulate 3), the human body can be affected sometimes in one way and sometimes in another; and consequently (by that same Axiom 1) it may be affected differently at different times by one and the same object. Q.E.D.

Note. We see, then, that it is possible for one man to love what another hates, and for one man to fear what another does not; and again, that one and the same man may love what he once hated, or be bold where he was once timid, and so on. And since everyone judges according to his own emotions what is good, what bad, what better and what worse (Proposition 39 of this Part, Note), it follows that men's judgments may vary as much as their emotions do. So when we compare one man with another, we distinguish them solely by the difference of their emotions, and call some intrepid, others timid, and others by some other name. I shall call a man intrepid, for instance, if he despises an evil I am used to fearing; and if I further take into account that in his desire to injure his enemies and benefit those he loves he is not held back by the fear of an evil which is enough to hold me back, I shall call him daring. Again, a man will seem timid to me if he fears an evil I am used to despising; and if I further take into account that his desire is held back by the fear of an evil which is not enough to hold me back, I shall say he is cowardly. And so everyone will pass judgment.

Footnote: This is possible, though the human mind is part of the divine intellect, as I have shown in Part 2, Proposition 13, Note.

Last, from this inconstancy in human judgment—since a man often judges things solely by his emotions, and since the things he believes cause pleasure or pain, and therefore tries to bring about or prevent, are often purely imaginary, quite apart from the uncertainty of things mentioned in Proposition 28 of this Part—we may readily conceive that a man may at one time be affected with pleasure and at another with pain, accompanied in each case by the idea of himself as cause. So we can easily understand what repentance and self-approval are. Repentance is pain accompanied by the idea of oneself as cause; self-approval is pleasure accompanied by the idea of oneself as cause. And these emotions are the most intense of all, because men believe themselves free (Proposition 49 of this Part).

Proposition 52

An object we have previously seen along with others, and which we do not conceive to have any property that is not common to many things, will not hold our attention as long as an object we conceive to have some property peculiar to itself.

Proof. As soon as we conceive an object we have seen along with others, we at once remember those others (Part 2, Proposition 18, Note), and so pass directly from contemplating the one object to contemplating another. And this is the case with an object we conceive to have no property that is not common to many, since we are then assuming that we see in it nothing we have not seen before along with other objects. But when we suppose we are conceiving something special in an object, something we have never seen before, we must say that while the mind regards that object it has nothing else in itself which it could fall to regarding instead; so it is determined to contemplate that object only. Therefore an object, and so on. Q.E.D.

Note. This modification of the mind, or imagining of a particular thing insofar as it is alone in the mind, is called wonder. If it is excited by an object of fear, it is called consternation, because wonder at an evil keeps a man so absorbed in simply contemplating it that he has no power to think of anything else by which he might avoid it. If what we wonder at is a man's prudence, or industry, or anything of that sort, then, since we regard him as far surpassing ourselves in it, the wonder is called veneration; but if it is a man's anger or envy that we wonder at, the emotion is called horror. Again, if it is the prudence or industry of a man we love that we wonder at, our love will be the greater for it (Proposition 12 of this Part), and love joined to wonder or veneration is called devotion. We may conceive hatred, hope, confidence and the other emotions as associated with wonder in the same way, and we should thus be able to deduce more emotions than have names in ordinary speech—which shows that the names of the emotions have been given according to how the emotions ordinarily show themselves, rather than from any accurate knowledge of their nature.

The opposite of wonder is contempt, which generally arises like this. Because we see someone wondering at, loving or fearing a thing, or because a thing at first sight seems like things we ourselves wonder at, love or fear, we are (Proposition 15 of this Part, Corollary; Proposition 27 of this Part) determined to wonder at, love or fear it. But if from its presence, or from contemplating it more accurately, we are forced to deny of it everything that could cause wonder, love or fear, then the mind, by the thing's very presence, remains determined to think of the qualities it does not have rather than of those it does—whereas normally the presence of an object would make the mind regard chiefly what is in it. As devotion springs from wonder at a thing we love, so derision springs from contempt for a thing we hate or fear, and scorn from contempt for folly, as veneration springs from wonder at prudence. And last, we can conceive love, hope, honour and the rest as associated with contempt, and can deduce from that still other emotions which have no recognised names to tell them apart.

Proposition 53

When the mind regards itself and its own power of acting, it feels pleasure; and that pleasure is greater in proportion as it conceives itself and its power of acting more distinctly.

Proof. A man does not know himself except through the modifications of his body and the ideas of them (Part 2, Proposition 19; Part 2, Proposition 23). So when the mind is able to contemplate itself, it is by that very fact supposed to pass to a greater perfection—that is (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note), to feel pleasure; and the pleasure will be greater in proportion as it can conceive itself and its power of acting more distinctly. Q.E.D.

Corollary. This pleasure is fed more and more in proportion as a man conceives himself to be praised by others. For the more he conceives himself as praised, the more he will imagine them to be affected with pleasure accompanied by the idea of himself (Proposition 29 of this Part, Note); and so he is (Proposition 27 of this Part) himself affected with greater pleasure accompanied by the idea of himself. Q.E.D.

Proposition 54

The mind endeavours to conceive only those things which assert its power of acting.

Proof. The endeavour, or power, of the mind is its actual essence (Proposition 7 of this Part); and the essence of the mind obviously affirms only what the mind is and can do, not what it is not and cannot do. Therefore the mind endeavours to conceive only those things which assert, or affirm, its power of acting. Q.E.D.

Proposition 55

When the mind contemplates its own weakness, it feels pain.

Proof. The essence of the mind affirms only what the mind is, or can do; in other words, it is the mind's nature to conceive only such things as assert its power of acting (Proposition 54 of this Part). So when we say that the mind contemplates its own weakness, we are merely saying that while the mind is trying to conceive something which asserts its power of acting, it is checked in that endeavour—in other words (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note), it feels pain. Q.E.D.

Corollary. This pain is fed more and more if a man conceives that he is blamed by others; this may be proved in the same way as the corollary to Proposition 53 of this Part.

Note. This pain, accompanied by the idea of our own weakness, is called humility; the pleasure that springs from contemplating ourselves is called self-love, or self-approval. And since this feeling is renewed as often as a man contemplates his own virtues, or his own power of acting, it follows that everyone is fond of telling the story of his own exploits and displaying the strength of his body and mind—and that men are for this reason a trial to one another. It follows too that men are naturally envious (Proposition 24 of this Part, Note; Proposition 32 of this Part, Note), rejoicing in the shortcomings of their equals and feeling pain at their virtues. For whenever a man conceives his own actions, he is affected with pleasure (Proposition 53 of this Part) in proportion as those actions display more perfection and as he conceives them more distinctly—that is (Part 2, Proposition 40, Note), in proportion as he can tell them apart from other men's and regard them as something special. So a man takes most pleasure in contemplating himself when he contemplates some quality he denies to others. But if what he affirms of himself belongs to the idea of man, or of animals in general, he will not be nearly so pleased; and he will feel pain if he conceives his own actions to fall short of others'. This pain (Proposition 28 of this Part) he will endeavour to remove by putting a wrong construction on the actions of his equals, or by embellishing his own as far as he can.

It is plain, then, that men are naturally prone to hatred and envy, and that envy is fed by their upbringing. For parents are accustomed to spur their children to virtue solely by honour and envy. But perhaps some will hesitate to agree with what I have said, because we not seldom admire men's virtues and venerate those who have them. To remove such doubts, I add the following corollary.

Corollary. No one envies the virtue of anyone who is not his equal.

Proof. Envy is a kind of hatred (Proposition 24 of this Part, Note), or (Proposition 13 of this Part, Note) pain; that is (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note), a modification by which a man's power of acting, or his endeavour towards acting, is checked. But a man does not endeavour, or desire, to do anything which cannot follow from his nature as it is; therefore he will not desire to have attributed to him any power of acting, or virtue, which belongs to another's nature and is foreign to his own. So his desire cannot be checked, nor can he be pained, by contemplating virtue in someone unlike himself; and consequently he cannot envy such a person. But he can envy his equal, who is supposed to have the same nature as himself. Q.E.D.

Note. So when, as we said in the note to Proposition 52 of this Part, we venerate a man out of wonder at his prudence or fortitude, we do so because we conceive those qualities as peculiar to him and not as common to our own nature. We therefore no more envy him for them than we envy trees for being tall or lions for being brave.

Proposition 56

There are as many kinds of pleasure, of pain, of desire, and of every emotion compounded of these (such as waverings of the mind) or derived from them (such as love, hatred, hope and fear), as there are kinds of objects by which we are affected.

Proof. Pleasure and pain, and consequently the emotions compounded of or derived from them, are passions, or passive states (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note). Now we are necessarily passive (Proposition 1 of this Part) insofar as we have inadequate ideas, and only insofar as we have such ideas are we passive (Proposition 3 of this Part); that is, we are necessarily passive (Part 2, Proposition 40, Note) only insofar as we conceive, or (Part 2, Proposition 17, Note) insofar as we are affected by an emotion which involves the nature of our own body and the nature of an external body. So the nature of every passive state must be explained in such a way that the nature of the object affecting us is expressed. The pleasure arising from, say, the object A involves the nature of A, and the pleasure arising from the object B involves the nature of B; so these two pleasures are by nature different, since the causes they arise from are by nature different. And so the pain arising from one object is by nature different from the pain arising from another, and likewise with love, hatred, hope, fear, wavering and the rest.

There are therefore necessarily as many kinds of pleasure, pain, love, hatred and so on as there are kinds of objects by which we are affected. Now desire is each man's essence, or nature, insofar as it is conceived as determined to a particular action by some given modification of itself (Proposition 9 of this Part, Note). So according as a man is affected through external causes by this or that kind of pleasure, pain, love, hatred and so on—in other words, according as his nature is disposed this way or that—his desire will be of one kind or another; and the nature of one desire must differ from the nature of another as widely as the emotions differ that each arose from. So there are as many kinds of desire as there are kinds of pleasure, pain, love and the rest; and consequently, by what has been shown, as many kinds of desire as there are kinds of objects by which we are affected. Q.E.D.

Note. Of the kinds of emotion, which by the last proposition must be very numerous, the chief are gluttony, drunkenness, lust, avarice and ambition. These are merely kinds of love or desire, displaying the nature of those emotions in ways that vary with the object they are concerned with. For by gluttony, drunkenness, lust, avarice and ambition we simply mean the immoderate love of feasting, of drinking, of sex, of riches and of fame. Furthermore, these emotions, insofar as we distinguish them from others merely by the objects they are concerned with, have no contraries. For temperance, sobriety and chastity, which we usually set against gluttony, drunkenness and lust, are not emotions or passive states at all, but indicate a power of the mind which moderates those emotions. I cannot explain the remaining kinds of emotion here, since they are as numerous as the kinds of objects; nor, if I could, would it be necessary. For our purpose—to determine the strength of the emotions and the mind's power over them—it is enough to have a general definition of each emotion. It is enough, I repeat, to understand the general properties of the emotions and of the mind, so as to determine the quality and extent of the mind's power in moderating and checking them. So although there is a great difference between one emotion of love, hatred or desire and another—between love felt towards children and love felt towards a wife, for instance—there is no need for us to take notice of such differences, or to track the nature and origin of the emotions any further.

Proposition 57

Any emotion of a given individual differs from the emotion of another individual only insofar as the essence of the one differs from the essence of the other.

Proof. This proposition is evident from Ax. i. (which see after Lemma 3, following Part 2, Proposition 13). Nevertheless, we will prove it from the nature of the three primary emotions.

All emotions belong to desire, pleasure or pain, as the definitions given above show. But desire is each man's nature, or essence (Proposition 9 of this Part, Note); so desire in one individual differs from desire in another only insofar as the nature, or essence, of the one differs from that of the other. Again, pleasure and pain are passive states, or passions, by which every man's power or endeavour to persist in his being is increased or diminished, helped or hindered (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note). But by the endeavour to persist in its being, insofar as it belongs to mind and body together, we mean appetite and desire (Proposition 9 of this Part, Note). So pleasure and pain are the same as desire, or appetite, insofar as external causes increase or diminish, help or hinder it—in other words, they are every man's nature. So the pleasure and pain felt by one man differ from the pleasure and pain felt by another only insofar as the nature, or essence, of the one differs from that of the other; and consequently any emotion of one individual only differs, and so on. Q.E.D.

Note. It follows that the emotions of the animals called irrational—for after learning the origin of mind we cannot doubt that animals feel—differ from human emotions only to the extent that animal nature differs from human nature. Horse and man are alike carried away by the desire to breed; but the desire of the one is equine and the desire of the other human. And so the lusts and appetites of insects, fishes and birds must vary according to their several natures. So although each individual lives content and rejoices in the nature it has, the life in which each is content and rejoices is nothing but the idea, or soul, of that individual; and hence the joy of one differs in nature from the joy of another to the extent that the essence of one differs from the essence of the other. And last, it follows from the previous proposition that there is no small difference between the joy which moves, say, a drunkard and the joy possessed by a philosopher—which I mention here only in passing. So far I have treated of the emotions belonging to man insofar as he is passive. It remains to add a few words on those belonging to him insofar as he is active.

Proposition 58

Besides the pleasure and desire which are passive states, there are other emotions of pleasure and desire which belong to us insofar as we are active.

Proof. When the mind conceives itself and its own power of acting, it feels pleasure (Proposition 53 of this Part); and the mind necessarily contemplates itself when it conceives a true, or adequate, idea (Part 2, Proposition 43). But the mind does conceive certain adequate ideas (Part 2, Proposition 40, Note 2). Therefore it feels pleasure insofar as it conceives adequate ideas—that is, insofar as it is active (Proposition 1 of this Part). Again, the mind, both insofar as it has clear and distinct ideas and insofar as it has confused ones, endeavours to persist in its own being (Proposition 9 of this Part); and by such an endeavour we mean desire (by Proposition 57 of this Part). So desire also belongs to us insofar as we understand—that is (Proposition 1 of this Part), insofar as we are active. Q.E.D.

Proposition 59

Among all the emotions belonging to the mind as active, there are none that cannot be referred to pleasure or desire.

Proof. All emotions can be referred to desire, pleasure or pain, as the definitions already given show. Now by pain we mean that the mind's power of thinking is diminished or checked (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note); so insofar as the mind feels pain, its power of understanding—that is, of acting—is diminished or checked (Proposition 1 of this Part). Therefore no painful emotions can be attributed to the mind in virtue of its being active, but only emotions of pleasure and desire, which (by Proposition 58 of this Part) do belong to the mind in that condition. Q.E.D.

Note. All actions following from emotion which belong to the mind in virtue of its understanding I set down to strength of character (fortitudo), which I divide into courage (animositas) and high-mindedness (generositas). By courage I mean the desire by which every man strives to preserve his own being under the guidance of reason alone. By high-mindedness I mean the desire by which every man endeavours, under the guidance of reason alone, to help other men and to join them to himself in friendship. Actions which look solely to the good of the agent I therefore set down to courage, and those which aim at the good of others to high-mindedness. Temperance, sobriety and presence of mind in danger are kinds of courage; courtesy and mercy are kinds of high-mindedness.

I think I have now explained, and displayed through their first causes, the principal emotions and waverings of mind which arise from the combination of the three primary emotions—desire, pleasure and pain. It is evident from what I have said that we are driven about in many ways by external causes, and that like waves of the sea driven by contrary winds we are tossed to and fro, not knowing our outcome or our fate. But I have said that I have set out only the chief conflicting emotions, not all that might be given. For by proceeding in the same way we can easily show that love is united with repentance, with scorn, with shame, and so on. I think everyone will agree from what has been said that the emotions may be compounded with one another in so many ways, and that so many variations may arise from them, as to be beyond all counting. For my purpose, however, it is enough to have listed the most important; to reckon up the rest would be more curious than useful. It remains to remark, about love, that it very often happens that while we are enjoying a thing we longed for, the body acquires from the act of enjoyment a new disposition by which it is determined otherwise; other images of things are roused in it, and the mind begins to conceive and desire something fresh. For example, when we conceive something whose flavour generally delights us, we desire to enjoy it—that is, to eat it. But while we are enjoying it the stomach is filled and the body is otherwise disposed; and if, when the body is thus otherwise disposed, the image of the food that is present is stimulated, and consequently the endeavour or desire to eat it is stimulated too, the new disposition of the body will feel repugnance to that desire or attempt, and consequently the presence of the food we formerly longed for will become hateful. This revulsion of feeling is called satiety, or weariness. As for the rest, I have passed over the outward modifications of the body observable in the emotions—trembling, pallor, sobbing, laughter and the like—since these belong to the body only, with no reference to the mind. And last, the definitions of the emotions need supplementing on a few points; I will therefore repeat them, adding such observations as I think should be made here and there.

Definitions of the Emotions

1. Desire is the actual essence of man, insofar as it is conceived as determined to a particular action by some given modification of itself.

Explanation. We said above, in the note to Proposition 9 of this Part, that desire is appetite together with the consciousness of it, and that appetite is the essence of man insofar as it is determined to act in ways that serve his own persistence. But in that same note I also remarked that, strictly speaking, I recognise no distinction between appetite and desire; for whether a man is conscious of his appetite or not, it remains one and the same appetite. So, to avoid the appearance of saying the same thing twice, I have refrained from explaining desire by appetite, and have taken care to define it in a way that brings under one head all those endeavours of human nature which we distinguish by the names appetite, will, desire and impulse. I might indeed have said that desire is the essence of man insofar as it is conceived as determined to a particular action; but from such a definition (compare Part 2, Proposition 23) it would not follow that the mind can be conscious of its desire or appetite. So, in order to imply the cause of that consciousness, it was necessary to add insofar as it is determined by some given modification, and so on. For by a modification of a man's essence we understand any disposition of that essence, whether inborn, or conceived under the attribute of thought alone, or under the attribute of extension alone, or referred to both attributes at once. By the term desire, then, I mean here all a man's endeavours, impulses, appetites and volitions, which vary with each man's disposition and are therefore not seldom opposed to one another, so that a man is drawn in different directions and does not know which way to turn.

2. Pleasure is a man's transition from a lesser to a greater perfection.

3. Pain is a man's transition from a greater to a lesser perfection.

Explanation. I say transition, for pleasure is not perfection itself. If a man were born with the perfection he passes to, he would possess it without the emotion of pleasure. This appears more clearly if we consider the contrary emotion, pain. No one can deny that pain consists in the transition to a lesser perfection and not in the lesser perfection itself, for a man cannot be pained insofar as he shares in perfection of any degree. Nor can we say that pain consists in the absence of a greater perfection, for absence is nothing, whereas the emotion of pain is an activity; and this activity can only be the activity of the transition from a greater to a lesser perfection—in other words, an activity by which a man's power of acting is lessened or checked (compare Proposition 11 of this Part, Note). I pass over the definitions of merriment, stimulation, melancholy and suffering, because these terms are generally used of the body and are merely kinds of pleasure or pain.

4. Wonder is the conceiving (imaginatio) of a thing at which the mind comes to a stand, because the particular idea in question has no connection with other ideas (compare Proposition 52 of this Part, Note).

Explanation. In the note to Part 2, Proposition 18 we showed why the mind passes at once from contemplating one thing to contemplating another: namely, because the images of the two things are so associated and arranged that one follows the other. Such association is impossible if the image of the thing is new, and the mind will then be at a stand in contemplating it until other causes determine it to think of something else.

So the conceiving of a new object, considered in itself, is of the same nature as any other conceiving; and this is why I do not count wonder among the emotions. Nor do I see why I should, since this arrest of the mind arises from no positive cause drawing it away from other objects, but merely from the absence of a cause to determine it to pass from contemplating one object to contemplating another.

I therefore recognise only three primitive, or primary, emotions (Proposition 11 of this Part, Note): pleasure, pain and desire. I have spoken of wonder simply because it is customary to give different names to certain emotions springing from the three primary ones when they are referred to objects we wonder at. The same reason leads me to add a definition of contempt.

5. Contempt is the conceiving of a thing which touches the mind so little that its presence leads the mind to imagine the qualities the thing does not have rather than those it does (compare Proposition 52 of this Part, Note).

The definitions of veneration and scorn I pass over here, since I am not aware of any emotions being named after them.

6. Love is pleasure accompanied by the idea of an external cause.

Explanation. This definition explains the essence of love clearly enough. The definition given by those who say that love is the lover's wish to unite himself to the loved object expresses a property of love but not its essence; and since those authors have not adequately grasped love's essence, they have been unable to form a true idea of its properties, so that their definition is on all hands admitted to be very obscure. It must be noted, however, that when I say it is a property of love that the lover should wish to unite himself to the beloved object, I do not mean by wish a consent, or a conclusion, or a free decision of the mind—for I have shown such things to be fictions in Part 2, Proposition 48—nor do I mean a desire to be united to the loved object when it is absent, or to remain in its presence when it is at hand, for love can be conceived without either of these desires. By wish I mean the contentment which is in the lover because of the presence of the beloved object, by which the lover's pleasure is strengthened, or at least maintained.

7. Hatred is pain accompanied by the idea of an external cause.

Explanation. These points are easily grasped after what has been said in the explanation of the previous definition (compare Proposition 13 of this Part, Note).

8. Inclination is pleasure accompanied by the idea of something which is accidentally a cause of pleasure.

9. Aversion is pain accompanied by the idea of something which is accidentally a cause of pain (compare Proposition 15 of this Part, Note).

10. Devotion is love towards someone we wonder at.

Explanation. Wonder (admiratio) arises, as we showed in Proposition 52 of this Part, from the novelty of a thing. So if it happens that we often conceive the object of our wonder, we shall cease to wonder at it; and so we see that the emotion of devotion readily degenerates into simple love.

11. Derision is pleasure arising from our conceiving that something we despise is present in a thing we hate.

Explanation. Insofar as we despise a thing we hate, we deny its existence (Proposition 52 of this Part, Note), and to that extent we rejoice (Proposition 20 of this Part). But since we are supposing that a man hates what he derides, it follows that the pleasure in question is not unmixed (compare Proposition 47 of this Part, Note).

12. Hope is an inconstant pleasure arising from the idea of something past or future whose outcome we to some extent doubt.

13. Fear is an inconstant pain arising from the idea of something past or future whose outcome we to some extent doubt (compare Proposition 18 of this Part, Note).

Explanation. It follows from these definitions that there is no hope unmixed with fear and no fear unmixed with hope. For he who lives in hope, and doubts the outcome of something, is supposed to conceive something which excludes that thing's future existence; so to that extent he feels pain (compare Proposition 19 of this Part), and consequently, while he lives in hope, he fears for the outcome. And conversely, he who fears—that is, doubts the outcome of something he hates—also conceives something which excludes that thing's existence; so to that extent he feels pleasure, and consequently to that extent hopes it will turn out as he wishes (Proposition 20 of this Part).

14. Confidence is pleasure arising from the idea of something past or future from which all cause of doubt has been removed.

15. Despair is pain arising from the idea of something past or future from which all cause of doubt has been removed.

Explanation. So confidence springs from hope, and despair from fear, when all cause for doubting the outcome of an event has been removed. This happens either because a man conceives something past or future as present and regards it so, or because he conceives other things which exclude the existence of the causes of his doubt. For although we can never be absolutely certain of the outcome of any particular event (Part 2, Proposition 31, Corollary), it may still happen that we feel no doubt about it; for we have shown that feeling no doubt about a thing is not the same as being certain of it (Part 2, Proposition 49, Note). So it may happen that we are affected with the same emotion of pleasure or pain about a thing past or future as about the conceiving of a thing present; this I have already shown in Proposition 18 of this Part, to which, with its note, I refer the reader.

16. Joy is pleasure accompanied by the idea of something past which has turned out beyond our hope.

17. Disappointment is pain accompanied by the idea of something past which has turned out contrary to our hope.

18. Pity is pain accompanied by the idea of an evil which has befallen someone else whom we conceive to be like ourselves (compare Proposition 22 of this Part, Note; Proposition 27 of this Part, Note).

Explanation. Between pity and compassion (misericordia) there seems to be no difference, unless perhaps that the first is used of a particular act and the second of a settled disposition.

19. Approval is love towards someone who has done good to another.

20. Indignation is hatred towards someone who has done evil to another.

Explanation. I am aware that these terms are used in senses rather different from those usually given them. But my purpose is to explain the nature of things, not the meaning of words; and I therefore use such terms as convey my meaning without any violent departure from their ordinary sense. One statement of my method will do. As for the cause of the emotions just named, see Proposition 27 of this Part, Corollary 1, and Proposition 22 of this Part, Note.

21. Partiality is thinking too highly of someone out of love for him.

22. Disparagement is thinking too meanly of someone because we hate him.

Explanation. Partiality, then, is an effect of love and disparagement an effect of hatred; so partiality may also be defined as love insofar as it leads a man to think too highly of a beloved object, and disparagement as hatred insofar as it leads a man to think too meanly of a hated object. Compare Proposition 26 of this Part, Note.

23. Envy is hatred insofar as it leads a man to be pained by another's good fortune and to rejoice in another's ill fortune.

Explanation. Envy is generally set against compassion, which, by doing some violence to the word, may therefore be defined thus:

24. Compassion (misericordia) is love insofar as it leads a man to feel pleasure at another's good fortune and pain at another's ill fortune.

Explanation. Concerning envy, see the notes to Proposition 24 of this Part and Proposition 32 of this Part. These emotions also arise from pleasure or pain accompanied by the idea of something external as their cause, either in itself or accidentally. I now pass on to other emotions which are accompanied by the idea of something within us as their cause.

25. Self-approval is pleasure arising from a man's contemplation of himself and of his own power of acting.

26. Humility is pain arising from a man's contemplation of his own weakness of body or mind.

Explanation. Self-approval is opposed to humility insofar as we mean by it the pleasure arising from contemplating our own power of acting; but insofar as we mean by it the pleasure accompanied by the idea of some action we believe we performed by the free decision of our mind, it is opposed to repentance, which we may define thus:

27. Repentance is pain accompanied by the idea of some action we believe we performed by the free decision of our mind.

Explanation. The causes of these emotions we have set out in Proposition 51 of this Part, Note, and in Proposition 53 of this Part, Proposition 54 of this Part and Proposition 55 of this Part, Note. As for the free decision of the mind, see Part 2, Proposition 35, Note. This is perhaps the place to point out that it is nothing to wonder at that all the actions commonly called wrong are followed by pain, and all those called right by pleasure. We can easily gather from what has been said that this depends in great measure on upbringing. Parents, by condemning the first kind of action and often scolding their children for it, and by urging and praising the second kind, have brought it about that the one is associated with pain and the other with pleasure. This is confirmed by experience. For custom and religion are not the same among all peoples: what some hold sacred others hold profane, and what some think honourable others think disgraceful. So each man, according to how he was brought up, either repents of a given action or glories in it.

28. Pride is thinking too highly of oneself out of self-love.

Explanation. Pride differs from partiality, then, in that the latter is used of an external object while pride is used of a man thinking too highly of himself. And as partiality is the effect of love, so pride is the effect, or property, of self-love, which may therefore be defined as love of self, or self-approval, insofar as it leads a man to think too highly of himself. To this emotion there is no contrary; for no one thinks too meanly of himself out of self-hatred. I say no one thinks too meanly of himself insofar as he conceives that he is incapable of doing this or that. For whatever a man imagines himself incapable of doing, he imagines it of necessity, and by that very idea he is so disposed that he really cannot do what he conceives he cannot do; for so long as he conceives that he cannot do it, he is not determined to do it, and so long as that is so, it is impossible for him to do it. However, if we consider matters that depend only on opinion, we shall find it conceivable that a man may think too meanly of himself. It may happen that a man, regarding his own weakness with sorrow, imagines that everyone despises him, while the rest of the world are thinking of nothing less. Again, a man may think too meanly of himself if he denies of himself in the present something in relation to a future time he is uncertain about—if he says, for instance, that he is unable to form any clear ideas, or that he can desire and do nothing but what is wicked and base. And we may also say that a man thinks too meanly of himself when we see him refusing, out of excessive fear of shame, to do things which others, his equals, venture. So we can set down as a contrary to pride an emotion I shall call self-abasement; for as pride springs from self-approval, so self-abasement springs from humility, and I define it accordingly:

29. Self-abasement is thinking too meanly of oneself by reason of pain.

Explanation. We are nevertheless generally accustomed to set pride against humility, but in doing so we attend more to the effect of either emotion than to its nature. We usually call proud the man who boasts too much (Proposition 30 of this Part, Note), who talks of nothing but his own virtues and other people's faults, who wants to come first, and who goes through life with a style and pomp that would suit men far above him in station. And we call humble the man who blushes too often, who confesses his faults, who sets out other men's virtues, and who walks with bent head and neglects his dress. However, these emotions, humility and self-abasement, are extremely rare, for human nature considered in itself strives against them as much as it can (see Proposition 13 of this Part; Proposition 54 of this Part). And so those who are believed to be the most self-abasing and humble are generally in fact the most ambitious and envious.

30. Honour is pleasure accompanied by the idea of some action of our own which we believe others praise.

Footnote: Gloria.

31. Shame is pain accompanied by the idea of some action of our own which we believe others blame.

Explanation. On this subject see the note to Proposition 30 of this Part. But we should note here the difference between shame and modesty. Shame is the pain that follows a deed we are ashamed of; modesty is the fear or dread of shame which keeps a man from committing a base action. Modesty is usually set against shamelessness, but shamelessness is not an emotion, as I shall show in due course; the names of the emotions, as I have remarked already, have to do with how they are exercised rather than with their nature.

I have now finished explaining the emotions arising from pleasure and pain. I therefore go on to treat of those I refer to desire.

32. Regret is the desire, or appetite, to possess something, kept alive by the memory of it and at the same time checked by the memory of other things which exclude its existence.

Explanation. When we remember a thing we are by that very fact, as I have said more than once, disposed to contemplate it with the same emotion as if it were present. But this disposition, or endeavour, is generally checked while we are awake by the images of things which exclude the existence of what we remember. So when we remember something that affected us with pleasure, we endeavour by that very fact to regard it with the same pleasure as though it were present, and this endeavour is at once checked by the memory of things which exclude its existence. Regret, then, is strictly speaking a pain opposed to the pleasure which arises from the absence of something we hate (compare Proposition 47 of this Part, Note). But since the name regret seems to refer to desire, I set this emotion down among those springing from desire.

33. Emulation is the desire of a thing engendered in us by our conceiving that others have the same desire.

Explanation. A man who runs away because he sees others running away, or who is afraid because he sees others afraid, or who, on seeing another burn his hand, draws back his own hand and moves his body as though his own were burnt—such a man can be said to imitate another's emotion, but not to emulate him. This is not because the causes of emulation and imitation are different, but because it has become customary to speak of emulation only in a man who imitates what we judge honourable, useful or pleasant. As for the cause of emulation, compare Proposition 27 of this Part, Note. Why this emotion is generally coupled with envy may be seen from Proposition 32 of this Part, Note.

34. Thankfulness, or gratitude, is the desire, or eagerness, springing from love, by which we endeavour to benefit someone who out of the same feeling of love has done us a kindness. Compare Proposition 39 of this Part, Note and Proposition 40 of this Part.

35. Benevolence is the desire to benefit someone we pity. Compare Proposition 27 of this Part, Note.

36. Anger is the desire by which hatred leads us to injure someone we hate. Proposition 39 of this Part.

37. Revenge is the desire by which mutual hatred leads us to injure someone who, out of the same feeling, has injured us (see Proposition 40 of this Part, Corollary 2, Note).

38. Cruelty, or savageness, is the desire by which a man is driven to injure someone he loves or pities.

Explanation. Against cruelty stands clemency, which is not a passive state of the mind but a power by which a man restrains his anger and his desire for revenge.

39. Timidity is the desire to avoid a greater evil we dread by undergoing a lesser one. Compare Proposition 39 of this Part, Note.

40. Daring is the desire by which a man is set on to do something dangerous which his equals are afraid to attempt.

41. Cowardice is attributed to a man whose desire is checked by the fear of a danger which his equals dare to face.

Explanation. Cowardice, then, is nothing but the fear of an evil which most men are not accustomed to fear; so I do not count it among the emotions springing from desire. I have chosen to explain it here nevertheless, because, so far as we look to the desire, it is truly the opposite of daring.

42. Consternation is attributed to a man whose desire to avoid an evil is checked by amazement at the evil he fears.

Explanation. Consternation is therefore a kind of cowardice. But since it arises from a double fear, it may be more conveniently defined as a fear which leaves a man so bewildered and wavering that he cannot remove the evil. I say bewildered insofar as we understand his desire to remove the evil to be checked by his amazement; I say wavering insofar as we understand that desire to be checked by the fear of another evil which torments him equally, so that he does not know which of the two to ward off. On this subject see Proposition 39 of this Part, Note, and Proposition 52 of this Part, Note. On cowardice and daring, see Proposition 51 of this Part, Note.

43. Courtesy, or deference (Humanitas seu modestia), is the desire to act in a way that pleases men and to refrain from what displeases them.

44. Ambition is the immoderate desire for honour.

Explanation. Ambition is the desire by which all the emotions (compare Proposition 27 of this Part; Proposition 31 of this Part) are fed and strengthened; and this is why it can hardly be overcome. For so long as a man is bound by any desire, he is at the same time necessarily bound by this one. "The best men," says Cicero, "are especially led by honour. Even philosophers, when they write a book condemning honour, sign their names to it," and so on.

45. Luxury is the excessive desire, or even love, of living sumptuously.

46. Intemperance is the excessive desire and love of drinking.

47. Avarice is the excessive desire and love of riches.

48. Lust is desire and love in the matter of sexual intercourse.

Explanation. Whether this desire is excessive or not, it is still called lust. These last five emotions (as I showed in Proposition 56 of this Part) have no contraries; for deference is a kind of ambition. Compare Proposition 29 of this Part, Note.

Again, I have already pointed out that temperance, sobriety and chastity indicate a power rather than a passivity of the mind. It may nevertheless happen that an avaricious, an ambitious or a timid man abstains from excess in eating, drinking or sex; yet avarice, ambition and fear are not the contraries of luxury, drunkenness and debauchery. For an avaricious man is often glad to gorge himself with food and drink at another man's expense. An ambitious man will restrain himself in nothing so long as he thinks his indulgences are secret, and if he lives among drunkards and debauchees he will, simply because he is ambitious, be the more prone to those vices. And last, a timid man does what he would rather not do: an avaricious man who throws his riches into the sea to avoid death remains avaricious all the same, and a lustful man who is downcast because he cannot follow his bent does not cease to be lustful on the ground of abstaining. In fact these emotions have less to do with the actual feasting and drinking than with the appetite and the love of them. So nothing can be set against these emotions but high-mindedness and courage, of which I shall speak presently.

The definitions of jealousy and the other waverings of the mind I pass over in silence, first because they arise from compounding the emotions already described, and second because many of them have no distinctive names, which shows that for practical purposes a general knowledge of them is enough. It is established, however, from the definitions of the emotions we have set out, that they all spring from desire, pleasure or pain—or rather that there is nothing besides these three, and that each is called by a variety of names according to its various relations and outward marks. If we now turn our attention to these primary emotions, and to what has been said about the nature of the mind, we shall be able to define the emotions as follows, insofar as they are referred to the mind only.

General Definition of the Emotions

Emotion, which is called a passive state of the soul, is a confused idea by which the mind affirms of its body, or of some part of it, a force for existence (existendi vis) greater or less than before, and by whose presence the mind is determined to think of one thing rather than another.

Explanation. I say, first, that an emotion, or passion of the soul, is a confused idea. For we have shown that the mind is passive only insofar as it has inadequate or confused ideas (Proposition 3 of this Part). I say, further, by which the mind affirms of its body, or of some part of it, a force for existence greater than before. For all the ideas we have of bodies indicate the actual disposition of our own body (Part 2, Proposition 16, Corollary 2) rather than the nature of an external body; and the idea which constitutes the form of an emotion must indicate, or express, the disposition of the body, or of some part of it, because its power of acting, or force for existence, is increased or diminished, helped or hindered. But it should be noted that when I speak of a greater or lesser force for existence than before, I do not mean that the mind compares the body's present disposition with its past one, but that the idea which constitutes the form of the emotion affirms something of the body which in fact involves more or less reality than before.

And since the essence of the mind consists in this (Part 2, Proposition 11; Part 2, Proposition 13), that it affirms the actual existence of its own body, and since by perfection we understand the very essence of a thing, it follows that the mind passes to a greater or lesser perfection when it comes to affirm of its own body, or of some part of it, something involving more or less reality than before.

So when I said above that the mind's power is increased or diminished, I meant only that the mind had formed of its own body, or of some part of it, an idea involving more or less reality than it had previously affirmed of its body. For the excellence of ideas, and the actual power of thinking, are measured by the excellence of the object. And last, I added by whose presence the mind is determined to think of one thing rather than another, so that besides the nature of pleasure and pain, which the first part of the definition explains, I might also express the nature of desire.

Part Four: Of Human Bondage, or the Strength of the Emotions

Preface, Definitions, Axioms, Propositions 1 to 18

Preface

Human infirmity in moderating and checking the emotions I name bondage: for a man who is at the mercy of his emotions is not his own master but is at the mercy of fortune, so much so that he is often forced, while seeing the better course, to follow the worse. Why this is so, and what is good and what evil in the emotions, I propose to show in this part of my treatise. But before I begin it would be well to make a few prefatory remarks about perfection and imperfection, and about good and evil.

When a man has set out to make a given thing and has brought it to completion, his work will be pronounced perfect, not only by himself but by everyone who rightly knows, or thinks he knows, the intention and aim of its maker. Suppose someone sees a work—which I assume is not yet finished—and knows that its maker's aim is to build a house: he will call the work imperfect, and will call it perfect as soon as he sees it carried through to the end its maker intended. But if a man sees a work the like of which he has never seen before, and does not know the maker's intention, he plainly cannot know whether the work is perfect or imperfect. Such seems to be the original meaning of these terms.

But once men began to form general ideas, and to think out types of houses, buildings, towers and so on, and to prefer some types to others, it came about that each man called perfect whatever he saw agreeing with the general idea he had formed of the thing in question, and imperfect whatever he saw agreeing less with his own preconceived type—even when it had clearly been finished exactly as its maker intended. This seems to be the only reason for calling natural things, which are not made by human hands, perfect or imperfect. For men habitually form general ideas of natural things no less than of artificial ones, and hold those ideas as types, believing that Nature (who they think does nothing without a purpose) has them in view and has set them before herself as models. So when they see something in Nature which does not wholly conform to the type they have preconceived, they say that Nature has fallen short or blundered and has left her work incomplete. We see, then, that men are in the habit of calling natural things perfect or imperfect from their own prejudices rather than from any true knowledge of what they are pronouncing on.

Now we showed in the Appendix to Part One that Nature does not act with an end in view. For the eternal and infinite Being which we call God or Nature acts by the same necessity by which it exists; for we have shown that it acts by the same necessity of its nature by which it exists (Part 1, Proposition 16). The reason, or cause, why God or Nature exists and the reason why he acts are one and the same. Therefore, just as he does not exist for the sake of an end, so neither does he act for the sake of an end; his existence and his action have neither beginning nor end. So what is called a final cause is nothing but human desire, insofar as it is considered as the origin, or cause, of something. When we say that being lived in is the final cause of this or that house, we mean nothing more than that a man, conceiving the conveniences of household life, had a desire to build a house. So being lived in, insofar as it is regarded as a final cause, is nothing but this particular desire, which is really the efficient cause; and it is taken for the first cause because men are generally ignorant of the causes of their own desires. They are, as I have often said, conscious of their actions and appetites but unaware of the causes by which they are determined to any particular desire. So the common saying that Nature sometimes falls short, or blunders, and produces things that are imperfect, I set down among the fictions treated of in the Appendix to Part One. Perfection and imperfection, then, are in reality merely ways of thinking—notions we form by comparing individuals of the same species with one another. This is why I said above (Part 2, Definition 6) that by reality and perfection I mean the same thing. For we habitually refer all individual things in nature to one class, the highest of all, namely the category of Being, to which absolutely everything in nature belongs. So insofar as we refer individual things to this category and, comparing them with one another, find that some possess more being or reality than others, we say to that extent that some are more perfect than others. And insofar as we attribute to them anything implying negation—limit, end, weakness and so on—we call them to that extent imperfect, because they do not affect our mind as strongly as the things we call perfect, and not because they have any deficiency of their own, or because Nature has blundered. For nothing belongs to a thing's nature but what follows from the necessity of the nature of its efficient cause, and whatever follows from the necessity of the nature of its efficient cause necessarily comes to pass.

As for the terms good and bad, they indicate no positive quality in things considered in themselves, but are merely ways of thinking, notions we form by comparing things with one another. Thus one and the same thing can be at the same time good, bad, and indifferent. Music is good for a man who is melancholy, bad for a man in mourning, and neither good nor bad for a man who is deaf.

Nevertheless, we should keep the terms. For since we desire to form an idea of man as a type of human nature to hold before us, it will be useful to keep them in the sense I have indicated.

In what follows, then, by "good" I shall mean what we certainly know to be a means of coming nearer to the type of human nature we set before ourselves, and by "bad" what we certainly know hinders us from coming nearer to it. And we shall say that men are more perfect or less perfect in proportion as they come nearer to that type or fall further from it. It must be particularly noted that when I say a man passes from a lesser to a greater perfection, or the other way round, I do not mean that he is changed from one essence, or reality, into another; for a horse would be as completely destroyed by being changed into a man as by being changed into an insect. What I mean is that we conceive the thing's power of acting, insofar as it is understood through its own nature, to be increased or diminished. And last, by perfection in general I shall, as I have said, mean reality—that is, each thing's essence insofar as it exists and acts in a particular way, with no regard at all to its duration. For no thing can be said to be more perfect because it has spent longer in existence. The duration of things cannot be determined by their essence, since the essence of things involves no fixed and definite period of existing; but every thing, whether more perfect or less, will always be able to persist in existing with the same force by which it began to exist. In this respect, therefore, all things are equal.

Definitions

1. By good I mean what we certainly know to be useful to us.

2. By evil I mean what we certainly know hinders us from attaining some good.

(Concerning these terms see the Preface to this Part, towards the end.)

3. I call particular things contingent insofar as, in regarding their essence alone, we find nothing in it which necessarily asserts their existence or excludes it.

4. I call particular things possible insofar as, in regarding the causes by which they must be produced, we do not know whether those causes are determined to produce them.

(in Part 1, Proposition 33, Note 1)

5. By conflicting emotions I mean those which draw a man in different directions even though they are of the same kind—such as luxury and avarice, which are both kinds of love and are contraries not by nature but by accident.

6. What I mean by an emotion felt towards a thing future, present and past, I explained in Part 3, Proposition 18, Notes 1 and 2, which see.

(But I should also remark here that we can only distinctly conceive distance of space or time up to a certain fixed limit. All objects further from us than two hundred feet—or whose distance from where we stand is greater than we can distinctly conceive—seem to be equally far from us, and all in the same plane; and so too objects whose time of existing is conceived as further from the present than we can distinctly conceive seem to be all equally distant from the present, and are set down, as it were, to the same moment of time.)

7. By an end, for the sake of which we do something, I mean a desire.

8. By virtue (virtus) and power I mean the same thing; that is (Part 3, Proposition 7), virtue, insofar as it is referred to man, is a man's nature or essence insofar as it has the power of bringing about what can be understood through the laws of that nature alone.

Axioms

There is no individual thing in nature than which there is not another more powerful and strong. Whatever thing is given, there is something stronger by which it can be destroyed.

Proposition 1

No positive quality possessed by a false idea is removed by the presence of what is true, in virtue of its being true.

Proof. Falsity consists solely in the privation of knowledge which inadequate ideas involve (Part 2, Proposition 35), and inadequate ideas have no positive quality on account of which they are called false (Part 2, Proposition 33); on the contrary, insofar as they are referred to God they are true (Part 2, Proposition 32). So if the positive quality possessed by a false idea were removed by the presence of what is true, in virtue of its being true, a true idea would be removed by itself, which (Proposition 3 of this Part) is absurd. Therefore no positive quality possessed by a false idea, and so on. Q.E.D.

Note. This proposition is more clearly understood from Part 2, Proposition 16, Corollary 2. For imagination is an idea which indicates the present disposition of the human body rather than the nature of the external body, and does so not distinctly but confusedly; and this is why the mind is said to err. When we look at the sun, for instance, we conceive it to be about two hundred feet away from us; and in this judgment we err so long as we are ignorant of its true distance. When the true distance is known, the error is removed, but not the imagining—that is, not the idea of the sun, which explains the sun's nature only insofar as the body is affected by it. So although we know the real distance, we shall still imagine the sun to be near us. For, as we said in Part 2, Proposition 35, Note, we do not imagine the sun to be so near us because we are ignorant of its true distance, but because the mind conceives the size of the sun to the extent that the body is affected by it. So when the sun's rays, falling on the surface of water, are reflected into our eyes, we imagine the sun as though it were in the water, though we know where it really is. And other imaginings in which the mind is deceived are likewise not contrary to the truth and do not vanish at its presence, whether they indicate the natural disposition of the body or that its power of acting is increased or diminished. It does happen that when we fear an evil mistakenly, the fear vanishes when we hear the truth; but the contrary happens too, that we fear an evil which will certainly come, and our fear vanishes when we hear false news. So imaginings do not vanish at the presence of truth in virtue of its being true, but because other imaginings, stronger than the first, come in and exclude the present existence of what we imagined, as I showed in Part 2, Proposition 17.

Proposition 2

We are passive only insofar as we are a part of Nature which cannot be conceived by itself, without the other parts.

Proof. We are said to be passive when something arises in us of which we are only a partial cause (Part 3, Definition 2)—that is (Part 3, Definition 1), something which cannot be deduced from the laws of our own nature alone. We are therefore passive insofar as we are a part of Nature which cannot be conceived by itself without the other parts. Q.E.D.

Proposition 3

The force by which a man persists in existing is limited, and is infinitely surpassed by the power of external causes.

Proof. This is evident from the axiom of this Part. For given a man, there is something else—say A—more powerful; and given A, there is something else—say B—more powerful than A, and so on to infinity. So the power of man is limited by the power of some other thing, and is infinitely surpassed by the power of external causes. Q.E.D.

Proposition 4

It is impossible that a man should not be a part of Nature, or that he should undergo no changes except those which can be understood through his own nature alone as their adequate cause.

Proof.
