The power by which each particular thing, and consequently man, preserves its being is the power of God, or of Nature (Part 1, Proposition 24, Corollary)—not insofar as it is infinite, but insofar as it can be explained through the actual essence of a man (Part 3, Proposition 7). So a man's power, insofar as it is explained through his own actual essence, is a part of the infinite power of God or Nature, that is, of its essence (Part 1, Proposition 34). That was our first point. Again, if it were possible that a man should undergo no changes except those which can be understood through his own nature alone, it would follow that he could not die but would always necessarily exist. And this would have to follow from a cause whose power was either finite or infinite: either from the man's own power alone, in that he would be able to keep off from himself all the changes that could spring from external causes; or from the infinite power of Nature, by which all individual things would be so ordered that a man could undergo no changes except those tending to his own preservation. But the first alternative is absurd (by Proposition 3 of this Part, the proof of which is universal, and can be applied to all individual things). Therefore, if it were possible that a man should undergo no changes except those explicable through his own nature alone—and consequently, as we have shown, that he must always necessarily exist—it would have to follow from the infinite power of God, and consequently (Part 1, Proposition 16) from the necessity of the divine nature, insofar as it is regarded as affected by the idea of some given man, that the whole order of nature as conceived under the attributes of extension and of thought could be deduced. It would follow therefore (Part 1, Proposition 21) that a man is infinite, which (by the first part of this proof) is absurd. It is therefore impossible that a man should undergo no changes except those of which he is the adequate cause. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows that a man is necessarily always subject to his passions, that he follows and obeys the general order of nature, and that he accommodates himself to it as far as the nature of things requires.

Proposition 5

The force and increase of any passion, and its persistence in existing, are defined not by the power by which we ourselves endeavour to persist in existing, but by the power of an external cause compared with our own.

Proof. The essence of a passion cannot be explained through our own essence alone (Part 3, Definition 1; Part 3, Definition 2); that is (Part 3, Proposition 7), the power of a passion cannot be defined by the power by which we ourselves endeavour to persist in existing, but must (as is shown in Part 2, Proposition 16) be defined by the power of an external cause compared with our own. Q.E.D.

Proposition 6

The force of any passion, or emotion, can overcome the rest of a man's activity, or power, so that the emotion becomes obstinately fixed in him.

Proof. The force and increase of any passion, and its persistence in existing, are defined by the power of an external cause compared with our own (by Proposition 5 of this Part); therefore (Proposition 3 of this Part) it can overcome a man's power, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 7

An emotion can only be checked or destroyed by another emotion contrary to it, and stronger than it.

Proof. An emotion, insofar as it is referred to the mind, is an idea by which the mind affirms of its body a greater or lesser force of existing than before (compare the general Definition of the Emotions at the end of Part 3). So when the mind is assailed by an emotion, the body is at the same time affected with a modification by which its power of acting is increased or diminished. Now this modification of the body (Proposition 5 of this Part) receives from its cause the force to persist in its being, and that force can only be checked or destroyed by a bodily cause (Part 2, Proposition 6), in virtue of the body being affected with a modification contrary to it (Part 3, Proposition 5) and stronger than it (the Axiom of this Part). Therefore (Part 2, Proposition 12) the mind is affected by the idea of a modification contrary to and stronger than the first—in other words (by the general Definition of the Emotions), the mind will be affected by an emotion contrary to and stronger than the first, which will exclude or destroy the existence of the first. So an emotion can neither be destroyed nor checked except by a contrary and stronger emotion. Q.E.D.

Corollary. An emotion, insofar as it is referred to the mind, can only be checked or destroyed by the idea of a bodily modification contrary to and stronger than the one we are undergoing. For the emotion we undergo can only be checked or destroyed by an emotion contrary to and stronger than itself—in other words (by the general Definition of the Emotions), only by the idea of a bodily modification contrary to and stronger than the one we undergo.

Proposition 8

The knowledge of good and evil is nothing but the emotion of pleasure or pain, insofar as we are conscious of it.

Proof. We call a thing good or evil when it serves or hinders the preservation of our being (Definition 1 of this Part; Definition 2 of this Part)—that is (Part 3, Proposition 7), when it increases or diminishes, helps or hinders our power of acting. So insofar as we perceive that a thing affects us with pleasure or pain, we call it good or evil; and therefore the knowledge of good and evil is nothing but the idea of the pleasure or pain which necessarily follows from that pleasurable or painful emotion (Part 2, Proposition 22). But this idea is united to the emotion in the same way as mind is united to body (Part 2, Proposition 21); that is, there is no real distinction between this idea and the emotion, or idea of the bodily modification, except in thought. Therefore the knowledge of good and evil is nothing but the emotion itself, insofar as we are conscious of it. Q.E.D.

Proposition 9

An emotion whose cause we conceive to be with us at the present time is stronger than if we did not conceive the cause to be with us.

Proof. Imagining, or conceiving, is the idea by which the mind regards a thing as present (Part 2, Proposition 17, Note), but which indicates the disposition of the body rather than the nature of the external thing (Part 2, Proposition 16, Corollary 2). An emotion is therefore a conceiving, insofar as it indicates the disposition of the body. But a conceiving (by Part 2, Proposition 17) is stronger so long as we conceive nothing which excludes the present existence of the external object; so an emotion is also stronger, or more intense, when we conceive the cause to be with us at the present time than when we do not. Q.E.D.

Note. When I said above, in Part 3, Proposition 18, that we are affected by the image of a thing past or future with the same emotion as if the thing conceived were present, I expressly stated that this is true only insofar as we look solely to the image of the thing itself; for the thing's nature is unchanged whether we have conceived it or not. I did not deny that the image becomes weaker when we regard as present to us other things which exclude the present existence of the future object; I simply did not call attention to it, because I intended to treat of the strength of the emotions in this part of my work.

Corollary. The image of something past or future—that is, of a thing we regard in relation to time past or time future, to the exclusion of the present—is, other things being equal, weaker than the image of something present. Consequently an emotion felt towards something past or future is, other things being equal, less intense than an emotion felt towards something present.

Proposition 10

Towards something future which we conceive as close at hand we are affected more intensely than if we conceive its time of existing to be separated from the present by a longer interval; and likewise we are affected more intensely by the memory of something we conceive to have passed away not long ago than of something we conceive to have passed away long since.

Proof. Insofar as we conceive a thing as close at hand, or as having passed away not long ago, we conceive less of what excludes the object's presence than if its time of future existence were more distant from the present, or if it had passed away long since—which is obvious. Therefore (by Proposition 9 of this Part) we are so far more intensely affected towards it. Q.E.D.

Corollary. From what was said in Definition 6 of this Part it follows that if objects are separated from the present by a longer period than we can define in thought, they all affect us equally faintly, however widely their dates may be separated from one another.

Proposition 11

An emotion towards something we conceive as necessary is, other things being equal, more intense than an emotion towards something possible, or contingent, or not necessary.

Proof. Insofar as we conceive a thing to be necessary, we to that extent affirm its existence; and on the other hand we deny a thing's existence insofar as we conceive it not to be necessary (Part 1, Proposition 33, Note 1). So (Proposition 9 of this Part) an emotion towards what is necessary is, other things being equal, more intense than an emotion towards what is not necessary. Q.E.D.

Proposition 12

An emotion towards a thing which we know not to exist at the present time, and which we conceive as possible, is more intense, other things being equal, than an emotion towards a thing contingent.

Proof. Insofar as we conceive a thing as contingent, we are affected by the conception of no further thing which would assert its existence (Definition 3 of this Part); on the contrary, we (by hypothesis) conceive certain things which exclude its present existence. But insofar as we conceive a thing to be possible in the future, we do conceive things which assert its existence (Proposition 4 of this Part)—that is (Part 3, Proposition 18), things which promote hope or fear. So an emotion towards something possible is the more vehement. Q.E.D.

Corollary. An emotion towards a thing which we know not to exist in the present, and which we conceive as contingent, is far fainter than if we conceived the thing to be present with us.

Proof. An emotion towards a thing we conceive to exist is more intense than it would be if we conceived the thing as future (Proposition 9 of this Part, Corollary), and much more vehement than if the future time were conceived as far distant from the present (Proposition 10 of this Part). So an emotion towards a thing whose time of existing we conceive to be far distant from the present is far fainter than if we conceived the thing as present; and it is nevertheless more intense than if we conceived the thing as contingent. Therefore an emotion towards a thing we regard as contingent will be far fainter than if we conceived the thing to be present with us. Q.E.D.

Proposition 13

An emotion towards a thing contingent, which we know not to exist in the present, is, other things being equal, fainter than an emotion towards a thing past.

Proof. Insofar as we conceive a thing as contingent, we are not affected by the image of any other thing which asserts its existence (Definition 3 of this Part); on the contrary (by hypothesis), we conceive certain things which exclude its present existence. But insofar as we conceive it in relation to time past, we are supposed to conceive something which recalls it to memory, or rouses its image (Part 2, Proposition 18, Note), which so far comes to the same thing as regarding it as present (Part 2, Proposition 17, Corollary). Therefore (Proposition 9 of this Part) an emotion towards a thing contingent which we know does not exist in the present is, other things being equal, fainter than an emotion towards a thing past. Q.E.D.

Proposition 14

A true knowledge of good and evil cannot check any emotion in virtue of being true, but only insofar as it is itself considered as an emotion.

Proof. An emotion is an idea by which the mind affirms of its body a greater or lesser force of existing than before (by the general Definition of the Emotions); so it has no positive quality which can be destroyed by the presence of what is true, and consequently the knowledge of good and evil cannot restrain any emotion in virtue of being true. But insofar as such knowledge is itself an emotion (Proposition 8 of this Part), if it has more strength for restraining emotion it will to that extent be able to restrain the emotion in question. Q.E.D.

Proposition 15

Desire arising from the knowledge of good and evil can be quenched or checked by many of the other desires arising from the emotions that assail us.

Proof. From the true knowledge of good and evil, insofar as it is an emotion, there necessarily arises desire (Definition 1 of the Emotions), whose strength is proportional to the strength of the emotion it arises from (Part 3, Proposition 37). But since this desire arises (by hypothesis) from our truly understanding something, it follows that it is present in us insofar as we are active (Part 3, Proposition 1), and must therefore be understood through our essence alone (Part 3, Definition 2); consequently (Part 3, Proposition 7) its force and increase can be defined solely by human power. And the desires arising from the emotions that assail us are stronger in proportion as those emotions are more vehement; so their force and increase must be defined solely by the power of external causes, which, compared with our own power, indefinitely surpasses it (Proposition 3 of this Part). So the desires arising from such emotions may be more vehement than the desire arising from a true knowledge of good and evil, and may consequently check or quench it. Q.E.D.

Proposition 16

Desire arising from the knowledge of good and evil, insofar as that knowledge concerns what is future, may be more easily checked or quenched than the desire for what is agreeable at the present moment.

Proof. An emotion towards a thing we conceive as future is fainter than an emotion towards a thing present (Proposition 9 of this Part, Corollary). But desire arising from the true knowledge of good and evil, even when it is concerned with things that are good at the moment, can be quenched or checked by any headstrong desire (by Proposition 16 of this Part, the proof of which is of universal application). Therefore desire arising from such knowledge, when it is concerned with the future, can be more easily checked or quenched, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 17

Desire arising from the true knowledge of good and evil, insofar as that knowledge concerns what is contingent, can be checked far more easily still than desire for things that are present.

Proof. This proposition is proved in the same way as Proposition 16 of this Part is proved from Proposition 12 of this Part, Corollary.

Note. I think I have now shown why men are moved by opinion more readily than by true reason, and why the true knowledge of good and evil stirs up conflicts in the soul and often gives way to every kind of passion. This is what gave rise to the poet's cry: "The better path I gaze at and approve, The worse—I follow."

Footnote: Ov. Met. vii.20, "Video meliora proboque, Deteriora sequor."

Ecclesiastes seems to have had the same thought in mind when he says, "He who increaseth knowledge increaseth sorrow." I have not written all this in order to draw the conclusion that ignorance is better than knowledge, or that a wise man is no better than a fool at controlling his emotions, but because we need to know the power and the weakness of our nature before we can determine what reason can do in restraining the emotions and what is beyond her power. I said that in this part I should treat only of human weakness; the power of reason over the emotions I have decided to treat separately.

Proposition 18

Desire arising from pleasure is, other things being equal, stronger than desire arising from pain.

Proof. Desire is the essence of a man (Definition 1 of the Emotions); that is, it is the endeavour by which a man endeavours to persist in his own being. So desire arising from pleasure is increased or helped by the very fact of pleasure being felt, while desire arising from pain is diminished or hindered by the very fact of pain being felt. So the force of desire arising from pleasure must be defined by human power together with the power of an external cause, whereas the force of desire arising from pain must be defined by human power alone. The former is therefore the stronger of the two. Q.E.D.

Note. In these few remarks I have explained the causes of human weakness and inconstancy, and shown why men do not abide by the precepts of reason. It remains for me to show what course reason marks out for us, which of the emotions agree with the rules of human reason, and which are contrary to them. But before I begin proving my propositions in detailed geometrical fashion, it is worth sketching them briefly in advance, so that everyone may grasp my meaning more readily.

Since reason demands nothing contrary to nature, it demands that every man should love himself, should seek what is useful to him—I mean what is really useful to him—should desire everything that really brings a man to greater perfection, and should, each for himself, endeavour as far as he can to preserve his own being. This is as necessarily true as that a whole is greater than its part (compare Part 3, Proposition 4).

Again, since virtue is nothing but acting according to the laws of one's own nature (Definition 8 of this Part), and since no one endeavours to preserve his own being except according to the laws of his own nature, it follows: first, that the foundation of virtue is the endeavour to preserve one's own being, and that happiness consists in a man's power of preserving his own being; second, that virtue is to be desired for its own sake, and that there is nothing more excellent or more useful to us for whose sake we should desire it; and third and last, that suicides are weak-minded, and are overcome by external causes contrary to their nature. Further, it follows from Part 2, Postulate 4 that we can never do wholly without external things for the preservation of our being, or live so as to have no dealings with what is outside us. And if we consider our mind, we see that our intellect would be more imperfect if the mind were alone and could understand nothing but itself. There are, then, many things outside us which are useful to us and are therefore to be desired. Of these none can be found more excellent than those which agree entirely with our own nature; for if two individuals of entirely the same nature are joined together, they make up a combination twice as powerful as either alone.

To man, therefore, there is nothing more useful than man. Nothing, I repeat, more excellent for the preservation of their being can be wished for by men than that all should so agree in all points that the minds and bodies of all should form, as it were, one single mind and one single body; that all should, with one consent, endeavour as far as they can to preserve their being; and that all should, with one consent, seek what is useful to them all. From which it follows that men who are governed by reason—that is, who seek what is useful to them under the guidance of reason—desire nothing for themselves which they do not also desire for the rest of mankind, and are consequently just, faithful and honourable in their conduct.

Such are the dictates of reason, which I meant to indicate thus briefly before beginning to prove them in more detail. I have taken this course in order, if possible, to gain the attention of those who believe that the principle that every man is bound to seek what is useful to himself is the foundation of impiety rather than of piety and virtue.

Having briefly shown that the contrary is the case, I go on to prove it by the same method I have used so far.

Propositions 19 to 39

Proposition 19

Every man, by the laws of his nature, necessarily desires or shrinks from what he judges to be good or bad.

Proof. The knowledge of good and evil is (Proposition 8 of this Part) the emotion of pleasure or pain insofar as we are conscious of it; therefore every man necessarily desires what he thinks good and shrinks from what he thinks bad. Now this appetite is nothing but a man's nature, or essence (compare Part 3, Proposition 9, Note; Definition 1 of the Emotions). Therefore every man, by the laws of his nature alone, desires the one and shrinks from the other, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 20

The more a man endeavours, and is able, to seek what is useful to him—that is, to preserve his own being—the more he is endowed with virtue; and conversely, insofar as a man neglects to seek what is useful to him, that is, to preserve his own being, he is lacking in power.

Proof. Virtue is human power, which is defined solely by a man's essence (Definition 8 of this Part)—that is, solely by the endeavour a man makes to persist in his own being. So the more a man endeavours, and is able, to preserve his own being, the more he is endowed with virtue; and consequently (Part 3, Proposition 4; Part 3, Proposition 6), insofar as a man neglects to preserve his own being, he is lacking in power. Q.E.D.

Note. No one, then, neglects to seek his own good, or to preserve his own being, unless he is overcome by causes external and foreign to his nature. No one, I say, refuses food or kills himself from the necessity of his own nature; it is always under compulsion from external causes, and it may happen in many ways. A man kills himself under compulsion from another who twists his right hand, in which he happens to be holding a sword, and forces him to turn the blade against his own heart. Or he may be compelled, like Seneca, by a tyrant's command, to open his own veins—that is, to escape a greater evil by incurring a lesser. Or, last, hidden external causes may so disorder his imagination, and so affect his body, that the body takes on a nature contrary to its former one, a nature whose idea cannot exist in the mind (Part 3, Proposition 10). But that a man should endeavour, from the necessity of his own nature, to cease to exist is as impossible as that something should be made out of nothing, as anyone will see after a little reflection.

Proposition 21

No one can desire to be blessed, to act rightly and to live rightly without at the same time desiring to be, to act and to live—that is, actually to exist.

Proof. The proof of this proposition, or rather the proposition itself, is self-evident, and is also plain from the definition of desire. For the desire to live, to act and so on, blessedly or rightly, is (Definition 1 of the Emotions) the essence of man—that is (Part 3, Proposition 7), the endeavour everyone makes to preserve his own being. Therefore no one can desire, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 22

No virtue can be conceived as prior to this endeavour to preserve one's own being.

Proof. The endeavour for self-preservation is the essence of a thing (Part 3, Proposition 7); so if any virtue could be conceived as prior to it, the essence of a thing would have to be conceived as prior to itself, which is plainly absurd. Therefore no virtue, and so on. Q.E.D.

Corollary. The endeavour for self-preservation is the first and only foundation of virtue. For nothing can be conceived prior to this principle, and without it no virtue can be conceived at all.

Proposition 23

A man, insofar as he is determined to a particular action because he has inadequate ideas, cannot absolutely be said to act from virtue; he can only be so described insofar as he is determined to the action because he understands.

Proof. Insofar as a man is determined to an action through having inadequate ideas, he is passive (Part 3, Proposition 1); that is (Part 3, Definition 1; Part 3, Definition 3), he does something which cannot be perceived through his essence alone—that is (by Definition 8 of this Part), which does not follow from his virtue. But insofar as he is determined to an action because he understands, he is active; that is, he does something which is perceived through his essence alone, and which follows adequately from his virtue. Q.E.D.

Proposition 24

To act absolutely from virtue is in us the same as to act, to live, or to preserve one's being (these three mean the same thing) under the guidance of reason, on the basis of seeking what is useful to oneself.

Proof. To act absolutely from virtue is nothing but to act according to the laws of one's own nature. But we act only insofar as we understand (Part 3, Proposition 3). Therefore to act from virtue is in us nothing but to act, to live, or to preserve one's being under the guidance of reason, and that on the basis of seeking what is useful to us (Proposition 22 of this Part, Corollary). Q.E.D.

Proposition 25

No one endeavours to preserve his own being for the sake of anything else.

Proof. The endeavour by which each thing endeavours to persist in its being is defined solely by the essence of the thing itself (Part 3, Proposition 7); and from that alone, and not from the essence of anything else, it necessarily follows (Part 3, Proposition 6) that everyone endeavours to preserve his being. The proposition is also plain from Proposition 22 of this Part, Corollary; for if a man endeavoured to preserve his being for the sake of something else, that other thing would obviously be the foundation of virtue, which by the corollary just cited is absurd. Therefore no one, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 26

Whatever we endeavour under the guidance of reason is nothing but to understand; nor does the mind, insofar as it uses reason, judge anything useful to itself except what conduces to understanding.

Proof. The endeavour for self-preservation is nothing but the essence of the thing in question (Part 3, Proposition 7), which, insofar as it exists as it is, is conceived to have force for continuing in existence (Part 3, Proposition 6) and for doing whatever necessarily follows from its given nature (see Part 3, Proposition 9, Note). But the essence of reason is nothing but our mind insofar as it understands clearly and distinctly (see Part 2, Proposition 40, Note 2). Therefore (Part 2, Proposition 40) whatever we endeavour under the guidance of reason is nothing but to understand. Again, since this endeavour of the mind, by which the mind, insofar as it reasons, endeavours to preserve its own being, is nothing but understanding, this endeavour at understanding is (Proposition 22 of this Part, Corollary) the first and only foundation of virtue; nor shall we endeavour to understand things for the sake of some further object (Proposition 25 of this Part). And on the other hand, the mind, insofar as it reasons, will not be able to conceive anything as good for itself except what conduces to understanding.

Proposition 27

We know nothing to be certainly good or evil except what really conduces to understanding, or what can hinder us from understanding.

Proof. The mind, insofar as it reasons, desires nothing beyond understanding, and judges nothing useful to itself except what conduces to understanding (by Proposition 26 of this Part). But the mind (Part 2, Proposition 41; Part 2, Proposition 43, Note) can have certainty about nothing except insofar as it has adequate ideas—that is (Part 2, Proposition 40, Note), insofar as it reasons. Therefore we know nothing to be good or evil except what really conduces, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 28

The mind's highest good is the knowledge of God, and the mind's highest virtue is to know God.

Proof. The mind is not capable of understanding anything higher than God—that is (Part 1, Definition 6), than a Being absolutely infinite, without which (Part 1, Proposition 15) nothing can either be or be conceived. Therefore (Proposition 26 of this Part; Proposition 27 of this Part) the mind's highest usefulness, or (Definition 1 of this Part) good, is the knowledge of God. Again, the mind is active only insofar as it understands, and only to that extent can it absolutely be said to act from virtue. The mind's absolute virtue is therefore to understand; and since, as we have shown, the highest thing the mind can understand is God, the highest virtue of the mind is to understand, or to know, God. Q.E.D.

Proposition 29

No individual thing whose nature is entirely different from our own can help or check our power of acting, and absolutely nothing can do us good or harm unless it has something in common with our nature.

Proof. The power of every individual thing, and consequently the power of a man, by which he exists and acts, can only be determined by another individual thing (Part 1, Proposition 28) whose nature (Part 2, Proposition 6) must be understood through the same attribute through which human nature is conceived. So our power of acting, however it is conceived, can be determined, and consequently helped or hindered, by the power of another individual thing which has something in common with us, but not by the power of anything whose nature is entirely different from our own. And since we call good or evil what is the cause of pleasure or pain (Proposition 8 of this Part)—that is (Part 3, Proposition 11, Note), what increases or diminishes, helps or hinders our power of acting—therefore what is entirely different from our nature can be neither good nor bad for us. Q.E.D.

Proposition 30

A thing cannot be bad for us through what it has in common with our nature; it is bad for us insofar as it is contrary to our nature.

Proof. We call a thing bad when it is the cause of pain (Proposition 8 of this Part)—that is (by the Definition, which see in Part 3, Proposition 11, Note), when it diminishes or checks our power of acting. So if anything were bad for us through what it has in common with our nature, it would be able to diminish or check the very thing it has in common with our nature, which (Part 3, Proposition 4) is absurd. Therefore nothing can be bad for us through what it has in common with us; but on the contrary, insofar as it is bad for us—that is, as we have just shown, insofar as it can diminish or check our power of acting—it is contrary to our nature. Q.E.D.

Proposition 31

Insofar as a thing agrees with our nature, it is necessarily good.

Proof. Insofar as a thing agrees with our nature, it cannot be bad for it; so it will necessarily be either good or indifferent. If we suppose it neither good nor bad, then nothing will follow from its nature (Definition 1 of this Part) which tends to preserve our nature—that is, by hypothesis, which tends to preserve the thing itself; and this (Part 3, Proposition 6) is absurd. Therefore, insofar as a thing agrees with our nature, it is necessarily good. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows that the more a thing agrees with our nature, the more useful, or better, it is for us; and conversely, the more useful a thing is for us, the more it agrees with our nature. For insofar as it does not agree with our nature, it will necessarily be either different from it or contrary to it. If different, it can be neither good nor bad (Proposition 29 of this Part); if contrary, it will be contrary to what agrees with our nature—that is, contrary to what is good—in short, bad. Nothing, therefore, can be good except insofar as it agrees with our nature; and so a thing is useful in proportion as it agrees with our nature, and the other way round. Q.E.D.

Proposition 32

Insofar as men are subject to passion, they cannot in that respect be said to agree in nature.

Proof. Things said to agree in nature are understood to agree in power (Part 3, Proposition 7), not in lack of power, or negation, and consequently not in passion (Part 3, Proposition 3, Note). So men, insofar as they are subject to their passions, cannot be said to agree in nature. Q.E.D.

Note. This is also self-evident. If we say that white and black agree only in that neither is red, we are absolutely affirming that they agree in no respect at all. And if we say that a man and a stone agree only in that both are finite, lacking in power, not existing by the necessity of their own nature, and indefinitely surpassed by the power of external causes, we should certainly be affirming that a man and a stone are alike in no respect whatever. So things which agree only in a negation, or in what neither of them possesses, really agree in nothing.

Proposition 33

Men can differ in nature insofar as they are assailed by emotions which are passions, or passive states; and to that extent one and the same man is variable and inconstant.

Proof. The nature, or essence, of the emotions cannot be explained through our own essence, or nature, alone (Part 3, Definition 1; Part 3, Definition 2), but must be defined by the power—that is (Part 3, Proposition 7), by the nature—of external causes compared with our own. So it follows that there are as many kinds of each emotion as there are external objects by which we are affected (Part 3, Proposition 56); that men may be affected differently by one and the same object (Part 3, Proposition 51), and to that extent differ in nature; and last, that one and the same man may be affected differently towards the same object, and may therefore be variable and inconstant. Q.E.D.

Proposition 34

Insofar as men are assailed by emotions which are passions, they can be contrary to one another.

Proof. A man—Peter, say—can be the cause of Paul's feeling pain, because he has something like what Paul hates (Part 3, Proposition 16), or because he alone possesses something Paul also loves (Part 3, Proposition 32, Note), or for other causes (Part 3, Proposition 55, Note). It may therefore happen that Paul hates Peter (Definition 7 of the Emotions), and consequently it may easily happen that Peter hates Paul in return, and that each endeavours to injure the other (Part 3, Proposition 39)—that is (Proposition 30 of this Part), that they are contrary to one another. But the emotion of pain is always a passion, or passive state (Part 3, Proposition 59); so men, insofar as they are assailed by emotions which are passions, can be contrary to one another. Q.E.D.

Note. I said that Paul may hate Peter because he conceives that Peter possesses something Paul also loves; and from this it seems at first sight to follow that these two men, by both loving the same thing and so by the agreement of their natures, stand in one another's way—in which case Proposition 30 of this Part and Proposition 31 of this Part would be untrue. But if we give the matter unbiased attention we shall see that the discrepancy vanishes. For the two men are not in one another's way in virtue of the agreement of their natures—that is, by both loving the same thing—but in virtue of the one differing from the other. Insofar as each loves the same thing, the love of each is fed by it (Part 3, Proposition 31)—that is (Definition 6 of the Emotions), the pleasure of each is fed by it. So it is far from being the case that they are at odds because they both love the same thing and so far agree in nature. The cause of their opposition lies, as I said, solely in the fact that they are supposed to differ; for we suppose that Peter has the idea of the loved object as already in his possession, while Paul has the idea of it as lost. Hence the one man is affected with pleasure and the other with pain, and so they are at odds with one another. And we can easily show in the same way that all the other causes of hatred depend solely on differences between men's natures, and not on their agreement.

Proposition 35

Only insofar as men live under the guidance of reason do they always necessarily agree in nature.

Proof. Insofar as men are assailed by emotions which are passions, they can differ in nature (Proposition 33 of this Part) and be at odds with one another. But men are said to be active only insofar as they act under the guidance of reason (Part 3, Proposition 3); so whatever follows from human nature insofar as it is defined by reason must (Part 3, Definition 2) be understood through human nature alone as its proximate cause. And since every man by the laws of his nature desires what he judges good and endeavours to remove what he judges bad (Proposition 19 of this Part); and since what we judge good or bad in accordance with reason necessarily is good or bad (Part 2, Proposition 41); it follows that men, insofar as they live under the guidance of reason, necessarily do only such things as are necessarily good for human nature, and consequently for each individual man (Proposition 31 of this Part, Corollary)—in other words, such things as agree with each man's nature. Therefore men, insofar as they live under the guidance of reason, necessarily always live in harmony with one another. Q.E.D.

Corollary 1. There is no individual thing in nature more useful to a man than a man who lives under the guidance of reason. For the thing most useful to a man is what most agrees with his nature (Proposition 31 of this Part, Corollary), and that is obviously man. But a man acts absolutely according to the laws of his own nature when he lives under the guidance of reason (Part 3, Definition 2), and only to that extent is he always necessarily in agreement with the nature of another man (by Proposition 35 of this Part). Therefore among individual things nothing is more useful to a man than a man who lives under the guidance of reason. Q.E.D.

Corollary 2. The more each man seeks what is useful to him, the more useful men are to one another. For the more a man seeks what is useful to him and endeavours to preserve himself, the more he is endowed with virtue (Proposition 20 of this Part)—or, what comes to the same thing (Definition 8 of this Part), the more he is endowed with power to act according to the laws of his own nature, that is, to live under the guidance of reason. But men most agree in nature when they live under the guidance of reason (by Proposition 35 of this Part); therefore (by Corollary 1 above) men will be most useful to one another when each most seeks what is useful to him. Q.E.D.

Note. What we have just shown is confirmed by experience so plainly that it is in nearly everyone's mouth: "Man is to man a God." Yet it rarely happens that men live under the guidance of reason; things are so ordered among them that they are generally envious and troublesome to one another. Nevertheless they can hardly lead a solitary life, so that the definition of man as a social animal has met with general assent; and in fact men do get far more convenience than harm out of social life. So let satirists laugh their fill at human affairs, let theologians rail, and let misanthropes praise the life of untaught rusticity to the skies, heaping contempt on men and praise on beasts—when all is said, they will find that men can provide for their wants far more easily by mutual help, and that only by joining forces can they escape the dangers that beset them on every side. Not to mention how much more excellent, and more worth knowing, it is to study the actions of men than the actions of beasts. But I shall treat of this at greater length elsewhere.

Proposition 36

The highest good of those who follow virtue is common to all, and all can equally rejoice in it.

Proof. To act from virtue is to act under the guidance of reason (Proposition 24 of this Part), and whatever we endeavour to do under the guidance of reason is to understand (Proposition 26 of this Part). Therefore (Proposition 28 of this Part) the highest good of those who follow virtue is to know God—that is (Part 2, Proposition 47, Note), a good common to all, which all men can possess equally insofar as they are of the same nature. Q.E.D.

Note. Someone may ask what would follow if the highest good of those who follow virtue were not common to all. Would it not then follow, as above (Proposition 34 of this Part), that men living under the guidance of reason—that is (Proposition 35 of this Part), men insofar as they agree in nature—would be at odds with one another? To this I answer that it follows not accidentally but from the very nature of reason that man's highest good is common to all, since it is deduced from the very essence of man insofar as that is defined by reason, and since a man could neither be nor be conceived without the power of taking pleasure in this highest good. For it belongs to the essence of the human mind (Part 2, Proposition 47) to have an adequate knowledge of God's eternal and infinite essence.

Proposition 37

The good which every man who follows virtue desires for himself he will also desire for other men, and the more so the greater his knowledge of God.

Proof. Men, insofar as they live under the guidance of reason, are most useful to their fellow men (Proposition 35 of this Part, Corollary 1); therefore (Proposition 19 of this Part), under the guidance of reason we shall necessarily endeavour to bring it about that men live under the guidance of reason. But the good which every man, insofar as he is guided by reason—in other words, insofar as he follows virtue—desires for himself is to understand (Proposition 26 of this Part). So the good which each follower of virtue seeks for himself he will desire for others too. Again, desire, insofar as it is referred to the mind, is the very essence of the mind (Definition 1 of the Emotions); and the essence of the mind consists in knowledge (Part 2, Proposition 11), which involves the knowledge of God (Part 2, Proposition 47) and without which (Part 1, Proposition 15) it can neither be nor be conceived. Therefore, in proportion as the mind's essence involves a greater knowledge of God, so much the greater will be the follower of virtue's desire that other men should possess what he seeks as good for himself. Q.E.D.

Another Proof. The good which a man desires for himself and loves he will love the more constantly if he sees that others love it too (Part 3, Proposition 31); so he will endeavour to make others love it as well. And since the good in question is common to all, and all can therefore rejoice in it, he will endeavour, for the same reason, to bring it about that all should rejoice in it; and he will do so the more (Part 3, Proposition 37) in proportion as his own enjoyment of that good is greater.

Note 1. A man who, guided by emotion alone, endeavours to make others love what he loves and to make the rest of the world live according to his own fancy, acts by mere impulse and is therefore hateful—especially to those who take delight in something different, and who accordingly study, by the same impulse, to make men live as pleases them. And since the highest good men seek under the guidance of emotion is often such that only one person can possess it, those who love it are not consistent in their intentions: while they delight in singing its praises, they fear to be believed. But a man who endeavours to lead others by reason acts not by impulse but courteously and kindly, and his intention is always consistent. Again, whatever we desire and do of which we are the cause insofar as we have the idea of God, or know God, I set down to religion. The desire to do good which is engendered by a life according to reason I call piety. And the desire by which a man living according to reason is bound to join others to himself in friendship I call honour; by honourable I mean what is praised by men living according to reason, and by base what is contrary to the gaining of friendship. I have also shown in addition what the foundations of a state are; and the difference between true virtue and infirmity may readily be gathered from what I have said—namely, that true virtue is nothing but living under the guidance of reason, while infirmity is nothing but a man's allowing himself to be led by things outside himself, and to be determined by them to act as the general disposition of things requires rather than as his own nature, considered in itself alone, requires.

Footnote: Honestas

These are the matters I undertook to prove in Proposition 18 of this Part, from which it is plain that the law against slaughtering animals is founded on vain superstition and womanish pity rather than on sound reason. The rational search for what is useful to us further teaches us that we must join ourselves with our fellow men, but not with beasts, or with things whose nature is different from our own; we have the same right over them as they have over us. Indeed, since everyone's right is defined by his virtue, or power, men have far greater rights over beasts than beasts have over men. Still, I do not deny that beasts feel; what I deny is that we may not consider our own advantage and use them as we please, treating them in whatever way best suits us, since their nature is not like ours and their emotions are naturally different from human emotions (Part 3, Proposition 57, Note). It remains for me to explain what I mean by just and unjust, sin and merit. On these points see the following note.

Note 2. In the Appendix to Part One I undertook to explain praise and blame, merit and sin, justice and injustice.

Of praise and blame I have spoken in Part 3, Proposition 29, Note; the time has now come to treat of the rest. But first I must say a few words about man in the state of nature and in society.

Every man exists by sovereign natural right, and consequently by sovereign natural right performs the actions that follow from the necessity of his own nature. So by sovereign natural right every man judges what is good and what is bad, looks after his own advantage as his own disposition directs (Proposition 19 of this Part; Proposition 20 of this Part), avenges the wrongs done to him (Part 3, Proposition 40, Corollary 2), and endeavours to preserve what he loves and destroy what he hates (Part 3, Proposition 28). Now if men lived under the guidance of reason, everyone would keep this right of his without any injury to his neighbour (Proposition 35 of this Part, Corollary 1). But since they are subject to their emotions, which far surpass human power, or virtue (Proposition 6 of this Part), they are often drawn in different directions and are at odds with one another (Proposition 33 of this Part; Proposition 34 of this Part), while standing in need of mutual help (Proposition 35 of this Part, Note). So in order that men may live together in harmony and help one another, it is necessary that they give up their natural right and, for the sake of security, refrain from all actions that can injure their fellow men. How this end can be reached—so that men, who are necessarily subject to their emotions (Proposition 4 of this Part, Corollary), inconstant and various as they are, should be able to make one another secure and to feel mutual trust—is evident from Proposition 7 of this Part and Part 3, Proposition 39. It is shown there that an emotion can only be restrained by an emotion stronger than and contrary to itself, and that men refrain from inflicting injury through fear of incurring a greater injury themselves.

On this law a society can be established, so long as it keeps in its own hands the right, possessed by everyone, of avenging injury and of pronouncing on good and evil, and provided it also has the power to lay down a general rule of conduct and to pass laws sanctioned not by reason, which is powerless to restrain emotion, but by threats (Proposition 17 of this Part, Note). Such a society, established with laws and with the power of preserving itself, is called a state, and those who live under its protection are called citizens. We may readily see that in the state of nature there is nothing which by universal consent is pronounced good or bad; for in the state of nature everyone thinks solely of his own advantage and decides, according to his own disposition and with reference only to his own advantage, what is good and what bad, being bound by no law to anyone but himself.

In the state of nature, then, sin is inconceivable. It can only exist in a state where good and evil are pronounced on by common consent, and where everyone is bound to obey the authority of the state. Sin, therefore, is nothing but disobedience, and is punished by the right of the state alone. Obedience, on the other hand, is set down as merit, since a man is thought worthy of merit if he takes delight in the advantages a state provides.

Again, in the state of nature no one is by common consent master of anything, nor is there anything in nature which can be said to belong to one man rather than another: all things are common to all. So in the state of nature we can conceive no wish to render every man his own, or to deprive a man of what belongs to him; in other words, there is nothing in the state of nature answering to justice and injustice. Such ideas are only possible in a social state, when it is decreed by common consent what belongs to one man and what to another.

From all this it is evident that justice and injustice, sin and merit, are extrinsic notions, and not attributes which display the nature of the mind. But I have said enough.

Proposition 38

Whatever disposes the human body so as to make it capable of being affected in more ways, or of affecting external bodies in more ways, is useful to man, and the more so the more it makes the body capable of being affected, or of affecting other bodies, in more ways; and conversely, whatever makes the body less capable in this respect is harmful.

Proof. Whatever increases the capabilities of the body increases the mind's capacity for perception too (Part 2, Proposition 14); so whatever disposes the body in this way, and makes it capable, is necessarily good, or useful (Proposition 26 of this Part; Proposition 27 of this Part), and is so in proportion to the extent to which it can make the body capable. And conversely (Part 2, Proposition 14; Proposition 26 of this Part; Proposition 27 of this Part), it is harmful if it makes the body less capable in this respect. Q.E.D.

Proposition 39

Whatever preserves the proportion of motion and rest which the parts of the human body have to one another is good; and whatever brings about a change in that proportion is bad.

Proof. The human body needs many other bodies for its preservation (Part 2, Postulate 4). But what constitutes the specific character (forma) of a human body is that its parts communicate their several motions to one another in a certain fixed proportion (the Definition before Lemma 4, which follows Part 2, Proposition 13). Therefore whatever preserves the proportion between motion and rest which the parts of the human body have to one another preserves the specific character of the human body, and consequently makes the human body capable of being affected in many ways and of affecting external bodies in many ways; and so it is good (by Proposition 38 of this Part). And whatever brings about a change in that proportion makes the human body take on another specific character—in other words (see the Preface to this Part, towards the end, though the point is indeed self-evident), destroys it, so that it becomes wholly incapable of being affected in more ways; and so it is bad. Q.E.D.

Note. How far such causes can injure or help the mind will be explained in Part Five. But I would remark here that I consider a body to undergo death when the proportion of motion and rest which held among its several parts is changed. For I do not venture to deny that a human body, while keeping the circulation of the blood and the other properties in which the life of a body is thought to consist, may nonetheless be changed into another nature entirely different from its own. There is no reason compelling me to maintain that a body does not die unless it becomes a corpse; on the contrary, experience seems to point the other way. It sometimes happens that a man undergoes such changes that I should hardly call him the same man. I have heard tell of a Spanish poet who was seized with an illness, and though he recovered from it remained so forgetful of his past life that he would not believe the plays and tragedies he had written were his own; indeed he might have been taken for a grown-up child if he had forgotten his native language as well. And if this instance seems incredible, what shall we say of infants? A man of ripe age thinks their nature so unlike his own that he can only be persuaded he was ever an infant himself by the analogy of other men. However, I prefer to leave such questions undiscussed, in case I give the superstitious grounds for raising new ones.

Propositions 40 to 65

Proposition 40

Whatever conduces to men's social life, or makes them live together in harmony, is useful; and whatever brings discord into a state is bad.

Proof. For whatever makes men live together in harmony also makes them live according to reason (Proposition 35 of this Part), and is therefore (Proposition 26 of this Part; Proposition 27 of this Part) good; and for the same reason whatever brings about discord is bad. Q.E.D.

Proposition 41

Pleasure in itself is not bad but good; pain, on the contrary, is in itself bad.

Proof. Pleasure (Part 3, Proposition 11, Note) is an emotion by which the body's power of acting is increased or helped; pain is an emotion by which the body's power of acting is diminished or checked. Therefore (Proposition 38 of this Part) pleasure in itself is good, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 42

Merriment cannot be excessive, but is always good; melancholy, on the contrary, is always bad.

Proof. Merriment (see Part 3, Proposition 11, Note) is a pleasure which, insofar as it is referred to the body, consists in all the parts of the body being affected equally; that is (Part 3, Proposition 11), the body's power of acting is increased or helped in such a way that its several parts keep their former proportion of motion and rest. So merriment is always good (Proposition 39 of this Part) and cannot be excessive. But melancholy (see Part 3, Proposition 11, Note) is a pain which, insofar as it is referred to the body, consists in the absolute decrease or checking of the body's power of acting; so (Proposition 38 of this Part) it is always bad. Q.E.D.

Proposition 43

Stimulation may be excessive and bad; and suffering may be good, insofar as stimulation, or pleasure, is bad.

Proof. Localised pleasure, or stimulation (titillatio), is a pleasure which, insofar as it is referred to the body, consists in one or some of its parts being affected more than the rest (see Part 3, Proposition 11, Note). The force of this emotion may be enough to overcome the body's other actions (Proposition 6 of this Part) and may stay obstinately fixed in it, so making it incapable of being affected in a variety of other ways; and so (Proposition 38 of this Part) it may be bad. Again, suffering, which is a pain, cannot as such be good (Proposition 41 of this Part). But since its force and increase are defined by the power of an external cause compared with our own (Proposition 5 of this Part), we can conceive infinite degrees and kinds of strength in this emotion (Proposition 3 of this Part); we can therefore conceive it as able to restrain stimulation and to prevent it becoming excessive and hindering the body's capabilities. To that extent, then, it will be good. Q.E.D.

Proposition 44

Love and desire may be excessive.

Proof. Love is pleasure accompanied by the idea of an external cause (Definition 6 of the Emotions); so stimulation accompanied by the idea of an external cause is love (Part 3, Proposition 11, Note), and love may therefore be excessive. Again, the strength of a desire varies with the emotion it arises from (Part 3, Proposition 37). Now an emotion may overcome all a man's other actions (Proposition 6 of this Part); so the desire arising from that emotion may likewise overcome all his other desires and become excessive, as we showed in the last proposition about stimulation.

Note. Merriment, which I have said is good, can be conceived more easily than it can be observed. For the emotions which assail us daily are generally referred to some part of the body which is affected more than the rest; and so the emotions are generally excessive, and fix the mind on one object so firmly that it cannot think of others. And although men are as a rule subject to many emotions, and very few are found who are always assailed by one and the same, there are cases where one and the same emotion stays obstinately fixed. We sometimes see men so absorbed in one object that, although it is not present, they think they have it before them; and when this happens to a man who is not asleep, we say he is delirious or mad. Nor are those thought any less mad who are inflamed with love, and dream night and day of nothing but their mistress, or some woman—they are made objects of ridicule. But when a miser thinks of nothing but gain, or money, or an ambitious man of nothing but glory, they are not reckoned mad, because they are generally harmful and are thought worthy of hatred. In fact, avarice, ambition, lust and the rest are kinds of madness, though they are not counted among the diseases.

Proposition 45

Hatred can never be good.

Proof. When we hate a man we endeavour to destroy him (Part 3, Proposition 39)—that is (Proposition 37 of this Part), we endeavour to do something bad. Therefore, and so on. Q.E.D.

Note. Here, and in what follows, by hatred I mean only hatred towards men.

Corollary 1. Envy, derision, contempt, anger, revenge and the other emotions belonging to hatred, or arising from it, are bad; this is evident from Part 3, Proposition 39 and Proposition 37 of this Part.

Corollary 2. Whatever we desire out of hatred is base, and in a state unjust. This too is evident from Part 3, Proposition 39, and from the definitions of baseness and injustice in Proposition 37 of this Part, Note.

Note. Between derision (which I have in Corollary 1 stated to be bad) and laughter I recognise a great difference. Laughter, like jesting, is merely pleasure, and therefore, so long as it is not excessive, is in itself good (Proposition 41 of this Part). Nothing but a grim and gloomy superstition forbids a man to enjoy himself. For why is it more proper to relieve one's hunger and thirst than to drive away one's melancholy? My reasoning, and my conviction, is this. No deity, and no one else except the envious, takes pleasure in my weakness and my discomfort, or counts as virtue in me the tears, the sobs and the fear which are signs of a weak spirit. On the contrary, the greater the pleasure we are affected with, the greater the perfection we pass to—in other words, the more we necessarily share in the divine nature. So it is the part of a wise man to make use of what comes his way and to enjoy it as much as he can, though not to the point of surfeit, for that would not be enjoyment. It is the part of a wise man, I say, to refresh and restore himself with moderate and pleasant food and drink, with perfumes, with the soft beauty of growing plants, with dress, with music, with games, with the theatre, and with everything else of the kind that a man can use without injury to his neighbour. For the human body is made up of a great many parts of different natures, which continually need fresh and varied nourishment, so that the whole body may be equally capable of everything that follows from the necessity of its own nature, and consequently so that the mind may be equally capable of understanding many things at once. This way of life, then, agrees best with our principles and with common practice; so if any other plan is in question, this one is the best and is in every way to be commended. There is no need for me to set the matter out more clearly or in more detail.

Proposition 46

He who lives under the guidance of reason endeavours, as far as he can, to repay other men's hatred, anger and contempt towards him with love, or kindness.

Proof. All emotions of hatred are bad (Proposition 45 of this Part, Corollary 1); so a man who lives under the guidance of reason will endeavour as far as he can to avoid being assailed by such emotions (Proposition 19 of this Part), and consequently will endeavour to prevent others being assailed by them too (Proposition 37 of this Part). But hatred is increased by being returned and can be quenched by love (Part 3, Proposition 43), so that hatred may pass into love (Part 3, Proposition 44). Therefore he who lives under the guidance of reason will endeavour to repay hatred with love, that is, with kindness. Q.E.D.

Note. He who chooses to avenge wrongs with hatred is certainly wretched. But he who strives to conquer hatred with love fights his battle in joy and confidence; he withstands many as easily as one, and has very little need of fortune's help. Those he defeats yield gladly, not because their strength failed but because it grew. All this follows so plainly from the mere definitions of love and of understanding that I need not prove it in detail.

Proposition 47

The emotions of hope and fear cannot be good in themselves.

Proof. The emotions of hope and fear cannot exist without pain; for fear is pain (Definition 13 of the Emotions), and hope (Definition 12 of the Emotions, Explanation; Definition 13 of the Emotions, Explanation) cannot exist without fear. Therefore (Proposition 41 of this Part) these emotions cannot be good in themselves, but only insofar as they can restrain excessive pleasure (Proposition 43 of this Part). Q.E.D.

Note. We may add that these emotions show a defect of knowledge and a lack of power in the mind; and for the same reason confidence, despair, joy and disappointment are signs of a want of mental power. For although confidence and joy are pleasurable emotions, they nevertheless imply a preceding pain, namely hope and fear. So the more we endeavour to be guided by reason, the less we depend on hope; we endeavour to free ourselves from fear and, as far as we can, to master fortune, directing our actions by the sure counsels of wisdom.

Proposition 48

The emotions of over-esteem and disparagement are always bad.

Proof. These emotions (see Definition 21 of the Emotions; Definition 22 of the Emotions) are contrary to reason, and are therefore (Proposition 26 of this Part; Proposition 27 of this Part) bad. Q.E.D.

Proposition 49

Over-esteem is apt to make its object proud.

Proof. If we see that someone rates us too highly out of love, we are apt to become elated (Part 3, Proposition 41), or to be affected with pleasure (Definition 30 of the Emotions); the good we hear of ourselves we readily believe (Part 3, Proposition 25); and so out of love we rate ourselves too highly—in other words, we are apt to become proud. Q.E.D.

Proposition 50

Pity, in a man who lives under the guidance of reason, is in itself bad and useless.

Proof. Pity (Definition 18 of the Emotions) is a pain, and therefore (Proposition 41 of this Part) in itself bad. The good effect which follows from it—our endeavour to free the object of our pity from misery—is an action we desire to do solely at the dictate of reason (Proposition 37 of this Part); and only at the dictate of reason can we perform any action we know for certain to be good (Proposition 27 of this Part). So in a man who lives under the guidance of reason, pity is in itself useless and bad. Q.E.D.

Note. A man who rightly grasps that all things follow from the necessity of the divine nature, and come to pass according to the eternal laws and rules of nature, will find nothing worth hating, deriding or despising, nor will he pity anyone; but so far as human virtue reaches, he will endeavour to do well, as the saying is, and to rejoice. We may add that a man who is easily touched with compassion, and moved by another's sorrow or tears, often does something he afterwards regrets—partly because we can never be sure that an action caused by an emotion is good, and partly because we are easily deceived by false tears. I am speaking here expressly of a man living under the guidance of reason. He who is moved to help others neither by reason nor by compassion is rightly called inhuman; for (Part 3, Proposition 27) he seems unlike a man.

Proposition 51

Approval is not contrary to reason, but can agree with it and arise from it.

Proof. Approval is love towards someone who has done good to another (Definition 19 of the Emotions); so it may be referred to the mind insofar as the mind is active (Part 3, Proposition 59)—that is (Part 3, Proposition 3), insofar as it understands. So it agrees with reason, and so on. Q.E.D.

Another Proof. He who lives under the guidance of reason desires for others the good he seeks for himself (Proposition 37 of this Part); so when he sees someone doing good to his fellow, his own endeavour to do good is helped—in other words, he will feel pleasure (Part 3, Proposition 11, Note) accompanied by the idea of the benefactor. Therefore he approves of him. Q.E.D.

Note. Indignation, as we defined it (Definition 20 of the Emotions), is necessarily bad (Proposition 45 of this Part). We may remark, however, that when the sovereign power punishes a citizen who has injured another, for the sake of keeping the peace, it should not be said to be indignant with the criminal; for it is not driven by hatred to ruin him, but led by a sense of duty to punish him.

Proposition 52

Self-approval may arise from reason, and the self-approval that arises from reason is the highest there can be.

Proof. Self-approval is pleasure arising from a man's contemplation of himself and of his own power of acting (Definition 25 of the Emotions). But a man's true power of acting, his virtue, is reason herself (Part 3, Proposition 3), which he contemplates clearly and distinctly (Part 2, Proposition 40; Part 2, Proposition 43); so self-approval arises from reason. Again, when a man contemplates himself, he perceives clearly and distinctly, that is adequately, only what follows from his power of acting (Part 3, Definition 2)—that is (Part 3, Proposition 3), from his power of understanding. So the highest self-approval there can be arises in that contemplation alone. Q.E.D.

Note. Self-approval is in reality the highest thing we can hope for. For (as we showed in Proposition 25 of this Part) no one endeavours to preserve his being for the sake of any further object; and since this approval is fed and strengthened more and more by praise (Part 3, Proposition 53, Corollary), and on the contrary (Part 3, Proposition 55, Corollary) is disturbed more and more by blame, fame becomes the most powerful of all incitements to action, and life under disgrace becomes almost unendurable.

Proposition 53

Humility is not a virtue, and does not arise from reason.

Proof. Humility is pain arising from a man's contemplation of his own weakness (Definition 26 of the Emotions). But insofar as a man knows himself by true reason, he is supposed to understand his own essence, that is, his power (Part 3, Proposition 7). So if in contemplating himself a man perceives some weakness in himself, that is not in virtue of his understanding himself, but (Part 3, Proposition 55) in virtue of his power of acting being checked. And if we suppose that a man perceives his own weakness in virtue of understanding something stronger than himself, by whose knowledge he measures his own power of acting, this is the same as saying that we conceive him to understand himself distinctly (Proposition 26 of this Part), because his power of acting is helped. So humility, the pain arising from a man's contemplation of his own weakness, does not arise from contemplation or from reason, and is not a virtue but a passion. Q.E.D.

Footnote: Land reads: "Quod ipsius agendi potentia juvatur"—which I have translated above. He suggests as alternative readings to 'quod', 'quo' (= whereby) and 'quodque' (= and that).

Proposition 54

Repentance is not a virtue, and does not arise from reason; a man who repents of an action is doubly wretched, or weak.

Proof. The first part of this proposition is proved like the one before it. The second part is proved from the mere definition of the emotion in question (Definition 27 of the Emotions); for the man lets himself be overcome, first by an evil desire and then by pain.

Note. Since men seldom live under the guidance of reason, these two emotions, humility and repentance, and hope and fear as well, bring more good than harm; so if we must sin, we had better sin in that direction. For if men who are subject to emotion were all equally proud, they would shrink from nothing and fear nothing, and how then could they be joined and bound together? The crowd plays the tyrant when it is not afraid; so we need not wonder that the prophets, who looked to the good not of a few but of all, so strenuously commended humility, repentance and reverence. Indeed those who are subject to these emotions may be led much more easily than others to live under the guidance of reason—that is, to become free and to enjoy the life of the blessed.

Proposition 55

Extreme pride, or extreme dejection, indicates extreme ignorance of oneself.

Proof. This is evident from Definition 28 of the Emotions and Definition 29 of the Emotions.

Proposition 56

Extreme pride, or extreme dejection, indicates extreme weakness of spirit.

Proof. The first foundation of virtue is self-preservation (Proposition 22 of this Part, Corollary) under the guidance of reason (Proposition 24 of this Part). So a man who is ignorant of himself is ignorant of the foundation of all the virtues, and consequently of all the virtues themselves. Again, to act from virtue is simply to act under the guidance of reason (Proposition 24 of this Part), and a man who acts under the guidance of reason must necessarily know that he does so (Part 2, Proposition 43). Therefore a man who is in extreme ignorance of himself, and consequently of all the virtues, acts least of all from virtue—in other words (Definition 8 of this Part), is most weak of spirit. So extreme pride, or extreme dejection, indicates extreme weakness of spirit. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows very clearly that the proud and the dejected are especially prey to the emotions.

Note. Yet dejection can be corrected more easily than pride; for pride is a pleasurable emotion and dejection a painful one, and the pleasurable is the stronger of the two (Proposition 18 of this Part).

Proposition 57

The proud man delights in the company of flatterers and hangers-on, but hates the company of the high-minded.

Proof. Pride is pleasure arising from a man's over-estimation of himself (Definition 28 of the Emotions; Definition 6 of the Emotions); and the proud man will endeavour to feed this estimation by every means in his power (Part 3, Proposition 13, Note). So he will delight in the company of flatterers and hangers-on, whose character is too well known to need definition here, and will avoid the company of the high-minded, who value him at his deserts. Q.E.D.

Note. It would take too long to list here all the evil results of pride, since the proud are prey to every emotion, though to none less than to love and pity. I cannot, however, pass over in silence the fact that a man may be called proud for underestimating other people; and in this sense pride may be defined as pleasure arising from the false opinion by which a man thinks himself superior to his fellows. And dejection, the quality opposite to this kind of pride, may be defined as pain arising from the false opinion by which a man thinks himself inferior to his fellows. This being so, we can easily see that a proud man is necessarily envious (Part 3, Proposition 41, Note), and takes pleasure only in the company of those who humour his weak mind to the top of its bent, and make him mad instead of merely foolish.

Though dejection is the emotion contrary to pride, the dejected man is very near akin to the proud one. For since his pain arises from comparing his own weakness with other men's power, or virtue, it will be removed—in other words, he will feel pleasure—if his imagination is occupied in contemplating other men's faults. Hence the proverb, "The unhappy are comforted by finding fellow-sufferers." And conversely, he will be the more pained the more he thinks himself inferior to others; so none are so prone to envy as the dejected. They are especially sharp in watching men's actions, with a view to finding fault rather than to correcting it, and they reserve their praise for dejection itself, and glory in it—though all the while with a dejected air. These effects follow as necessarily from that emotion as it follows from the nature of a triangle that its three angles are equal to two right angles. I have already said that I call these and similar emotions bad solely in respect of what is useful to man. The laws of nature have regard to nature's general order, of which man is only a part. I mention this in passing, in case anyone should think I have meant to set out men's faults and irrational deeds rather than the nature and properties of things. For, as I said in the Preface to Part Three, I regard human emotions and their properties as on the same footing with all other natural things. Human emotions certainly show the power and the ingenuity of nature, if not of human nature, quite as fully as the other things we admire and delight to contemplate. But I go on to note the qualities in the emotions which bring men advantage or do them harm.

Proposition 58

Honour (gloria) is not contrary to reason, but may arise from it.

Proof. This is evident from Definition 30 of the Emotions, and also from the definition of an honourable man (Proposition 37 of this Part, Note 1).

Note. Empty honour, as it is called, is self-approval fed only by the good opinion of the crowd; and when that good opinion ceases, so does the self-approval, which is the highest object of every man's love (Proposition 52 of this Part, Note). So a man whose honour is rooted in popular approval must strive and scheme and act anxiously, day after day, to keep his reputation; for the crowd is fickle and inconstant, and a reputation not kept up quickly withers away. Everyone wants to catch the applause of the crowd for himself, and readily suppresses the fame of others; and since the thing they are fighting over is reckoned the greatest of all goods, each combatant is seized with a fierce desire to put down his rivals by every possible means, until the one who comes out victorious at last is prouder of having harmed others than of having done himself good. This kind of honour, then, is really empty, being nothing at all.

What is to be noted about shame may easily be gathered from what was said about compassion and repentance. I will only add that shame, like compassion, though not a virtue, is nevertheless good insofar as it shows that the man who feels it really has a desire to live honourably—in the same way that pain is good, as showing that the injured part has not mortified. So although a man who feels shame is sorrowful, he is more perfect than a man who is shameless and has no desire to live honourably.

These are the points I undertook to make about the emotions of pleasure and pain. As for the desires, they are good or bad according as they spring from good or bad emotions. But all of them, insofar as they are engendered in us by emotions in which the mind is passive, are blind (Proposition 44 of this Part, Note), and would be useless if men could easily be brought to live under the guidance of reason alone, as I shall now briefly show.

Proposition 59

To every action to which we are determined by an emotion in which the mind is passive, we can be determined by reason without any emotion.

Proof. To act rationally is nothing but (Part 3, Proposition 3; Part 3, Definition 2) to perform the actions which follow from the necessity of our own nature considered in itself alone. But pain is bad insofar as it diminishes or checks the power of acting (Proposition 41 of this Part); so pain cannot determine us to any action we could not perform under the guidance of reason. Again, pleasure is bad only insofar as it hinders a man's capacity for action (Proposition 41 of this Part; Proposition 43 of this Part); so to that extent it could not determine us to any action we could not perform under the guidance of reason. And last, pleasure insofar as it is good agrees with reason, since it consists in a man's capacity for action being increased or helped; nor is the mind passive in it, except insofar as a man's power of acting is not increased far enough to give him an adequate conception of himself and his actions (Part 3, Proposition 3, Note).

So if a man affected with pleasure is brought to such a state of perfection that he gains an adequate conception of himself and his own actions, he will be equally capable—indeed more capable—of the actions to which he is determined by emotions in which the mind is passive. But all emotions belong to pleasure, to pain or to desire (Definition 4 of the Emotions, Explanation), and desire (Definition 1 of the Emotions) is nothing but the attempt to act. Therefore, to every action, and so on. Q.E.D.

Another Proof. An action is called bad insofar as it arises from someone's being affected by hatred or some other bad emotion. But no action considered in itself alone is either good or bad, as we pointed out in the Preface to Part 4, one and the same action being sometimes good and sometimes bad. So to the action which is sometimes bad, or arises from some bad emotion, we may also be led by reason (Proposition 19 of this Part). Q.E.D.

Note. An example will make this clearer. The act of striking, considered physically, insofar as we look only at the fact that a man raises his arm, clenches his fist and moves the whole arm violently downwards, is a virtue, or excellence, conceived as proper to the structure of the human body. If, then, a man moved by anger or hatred is led to clench his fist and move his arm, this happens (as we showed in Part 2) because one and the same action can be associated with various mental images of things; so we may be determined to one and the same action by confused ideas, or by clear and distinct ones. From which it is evident that every desire springing from an emotion in which the mind is passive would become useless if men could be guided by reason. Let us now see why we call a desire arising from an emotion in which the mind is passive blind.

Proposition 60

A desire arising from a pleasure or a pain which belongs not to the whole body but only to one or some of its parts is of no use in respect of a man as a whole.

Proof. Suppose, for instance, that A, a part of the body, is so strengthened by some external cause that it prevails over the remaining parts (Proposition 6 of this Part). This part will not endeavour to do away with its own powers in order that the other parts of the body may perform their office; for that it would need a force, or power, of doing away with its own powers, which (Part 3, Proposition 6) is absurd. That part, and consequently the mind too, will endeavour to preserve its condition. So a desire arising from a pleasure of that kind is of no use in respect of a man as a whole. And if we suppose instead that the part A is checked so that the remaining parts prevail, it may be proved in the same way that a desire arising from pain is of no use in respect of a man as a whole. Q.E.D.
