Note. Since pleasure is generally (Proposition 44 of this Part, Note) referred to one part of the body, we generally desire to preserve our being without taking our health as a whole into account. To which we may add that the desires which have the strongest hold on us (Proposition 9 of this Part) take account of the present and not of the future.

Proposition 61

A desire which springs from reason cannot be excessive.

Proof. Desire (Definition 1 of the Emotions), considered absolutely, is the actual essence of a man insofar as it is conceived as in some way determined to a particular action by some given modification of itself. So a desire arising from reason—that is (Part 3, Proposition 3), engendered in us insofar as we are active—is the actual essence, or nature, of a man insofar as it is conceived as determined to those actions which are adequately conceived through a man's essence alone (Part 3, Definition 2). If such a desire could be excessive, human nature considered in itself alone would be able to exceed itself, or to do more than it can, which is a plain contradiction. Therefore such a desire cannot be excessive. Q.E.D.

Proposition 62

Insofar as the mind conceives a thing under the dictates of reason, it is affected equally whether the idea is of a thing future, past or present.

Proof. Whatever the mind conceives under the guidance of reason, it conceives under the form of eternity, or of necessity (Part 2, Proposition 44, Corollary 2), and is therefore affected with the same certainty (Part 2, Proposition 43, Note). So whether the thing is present, past or future, the mind conceives it under the same necessity and is affected with the same certainty; and whether the idea is of something present, past or future, it will in every case be equally true (Part 2, Proposition 41)—that is, it will always have the same properties of an adequate idea (Part 2, Definition 4). Therefore, insofar as the mind conceives things under the dictates of reason, it is affected in the same way whether the idea is of a thing future, past or present. Q.E.D.

Note. If we could have adequate knowledge of the duration of things, and could determine by reason how long they will exist, we should contemplate things future with the same emotion as things present; the mind would desire the good it conceived as future just as though it were present, and would consequently neglect a lesser present good for the sake of a greater future one, and would in no way desire what is good in the present but a source of evil in the future, as we shall shortly show. But we can have only a very inadequate knowledge of the duration of things (Part 2, Proposition 31), and we can determine how long they will exist (Part 2, Proposition 44, Note) only by imagination, which is not so powerfully affected by the future as by the present. So such true knowledge of good and evil as we have is merely abstract, or general, and the judgment we pass on the order of things and the connection of causes, with a view to determining what is good or bad for us in the present, is imaginary rather than real. It is therefore no wonder if the desire arising from such knowledge of good and evil, insofar as it looks into the future, is more readily checked than the desire for things which are agreeable at the present time (compare Proposition 16 of this Part).

Proposition 63

He who is led by fear, and does good in order to escape evil, is not led by reason.

Proof. All the emotions belonging to the mind as active—in other words, to reason—are emotions of pleasure and of desire (Part 3, Proposition 59). Therefore he who is led by fear, and does good in order to escape evil, is not led by reason.

Note. Superstitious people, who know better how to rail at vice than how to teach virtue, and who strive not to guide men by reason but so to restrain them that they would rather escape evil than love virtue, have no other aim than to make others as wretched as themselves. It is therefore no wonder that they are generally troublesome and odious to their fellow men.

Corollary. Under a desire which springs from reason we seek good directly and shun evil indirectly.

Proof. A desire which springs from reason can only spring from a pleasurable emotion in which the mind is not passive (Part 3, Proposition 59)—in other words, from a pleasure which cannot be excessive (Proposition 61 of this Part)—and not from pain. So this desire springs from the knowledge of good, not of evil (Proposition 8 of this Part); and hence under the guidance of reason we seek good directly and shun evil only by implication. Q.E.D.

Note. This corollary may be illustrated by the example of a sick man and a healthy one. The sick man eats what he naturally shrinks from, through fear of death; the healthy man takes pleasure in his food and so gets more enjoyment out of life than he would if he were in fear of death and directly desiring to avoid it. So too a judge who condemns a criminal to death, not out of hatred or anger but out of love of the public good, is guided solely by reason.

Proposition 64

The knowledge of evil is an inadequate knowledge.

Proof. The knowledge of evil (Proposition 8 of this Part) is pain, insofar as we are conscious of it. Now pain is the transition to a lesser perfection (Definition 3 of the Emotions) and therefore cannot be understood through a man's nature (Part 3, Proposition 6; Part 3, Proposition 7); so it is a passive state (Part 3, Definition 2) which (Part 3, Proposition 3) depends on inadequate ideas. Consequently the knowledge of it (Part 2, Proposition 29)—that is, the knowledge of evil—is inadequate. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows that if the human mind had only adequate ideas, it would form no conception of evil.

Proposition 65

Under the guidance of reason we should pursue the greater of two goods and the lesser of two evils.

Proof. A good which prevents our enjoying a greater good is in reality an evil; for we apply the terms good and bad to things insofar as we compare them with one another (see the Preface to this Part). So an evil is in reality a lesser good; and hence under the guidance of reason we seek, or pursue, only the greater good and the lesser evil. Q.E.D.

Corollary. Under the guidance of reason we may pursue the lesser evil as though it were the greater good, and may shun the lesser good which would be the cause of the greater evil. For the evil here called the lesser is really good, and the lesser good is really evil; so we may seek the one and shun the other. Q.E.D.

Propositions 66 to 73

Proposition 66

Under the guidance of reason we may seek a greater good in the future in preference to a lesser good in the present, and may seek a lesser evil in the present in preference to a greater evil in the future.

Footnote: "Maltim praesens minus prae majori futuro." (Van Vloten). Bruder reads: "Malum praesens minus, quod causa est faturi alicujus mali." The last word of the latter is an obvious misprint, and is corrected by the Dutch translator into "majoris boni." (Pollock, p. 268, note.)

Proof. If the mind could have adequate knowledge of things future, it would be affected towards what is future in the same way as towards what is present (Proposition 62 of this Part). So looking merely to reason, as in this proposition we are supposed to do, it makes no difference whether the greater good or evil is supposed present or future. Hence (Proposition 65 of this Part) we may seek a greater good in the future in preference to a lesser good in the present, and so on. Q.E.D.

Corollary. Under the guidance of reason we may seek a lesser evil in the present because it is the cause of a greater good in the future, and may shun a lesser good in the present because it is the cause of a greater evil in the future. This corollary stands to the foregoing proposition as the Corollary to Proposition 65 of this Part stands to Proposition 65 of this Part itself.

Note. If these statements are compared with what we have shown about the strength of the emotions, in this Part up to Proposition 18 of this Part, we shall readily see the difference between a man led solely by emotion, or opinion, and a man led by reason. The first, whether he will or no, performs actions of which he is entirely ignorant; the second is his own master, and performs only such actions as he knows to be of first importance in life, and therefore desires above all. So I call the first a slave and the second a free man, and it will be well to make a few observations about the free man's character and manner of life.

Proposition 67

A free man thinks of death least of all things, and his wisdom is a meditation not of death but of life.

Proof. A free man is one who lives under the guidance of reason, who is not led by fear (Proposition 63 of this Part) but directly desires what is good (Proposition 63 of this Part, Corollary)—in other words (Proposition 24 of this Part), who strives to act, to live and to preserve his being on the basis of seeking his own true advantage. Such a man thinks of nothing less than of death; his wisdom is a meditation of life. Q.E.D.

Proposition 68

If men were born free, they would form no conception of good and evil so long as they remained free.

Proof. I call a man free who is led by reason alone. So a man born free, who remains free, has only adequate ideas; and therefore (Proposition 64 of this Part, Corollary) he has no conception of evil, and consequently, good and evil being correlative, none of good either. Q.E.D.

Note. It is evident from Proposition 4 of this Part that the hypothesis of this proposition is false, and inconceivable, except insofar as we look solely to the nature of man, or rather to God—not insofar as God is infinite, but only insofar as he is the cause of man's existence.

This, and other things we have already proved, seem to have been signified by Moses in the story of the first man. For in that narrative no power of God is conceived except the power by which he created man, that is, the power by which he provided solely for man's advantage. It is said that God forbade man, being free, to eat of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, and that as soon as man ate of it he would fear death rather than desire to live. Further, it is written that when man had found a wife who agreed entirely with his nature, he knew that there could be nothing in nature more useful to him; but that after he came to believe the beasts were like himself, he at once began to imitate their emotions (Part 3, Proposition 27) and to lose his freedom. This freedom was afterwards recovered by the patriarchs, led by the spirit of Christ—that is, by the idea of God, on which alone it depends that a man can be free and can desire for others the good he desires for himself, as we showed above (Proposition 37 of this Part).

Proposition 69

The virtue of a free man is seen to be as great in avoiding dangers as in overcoming them.

Proof. An emotion can only be checked or removed by an emotion contrary to it and stronger in restraining emotion (Proposition 7 of this Part). But blind daring and fear are emotions which can be conceived as equally great (Proposition 5 of this Part; Proposition 3 of this Part); so no less virtue, or firmness, is needed to check daring than to check fear (Part 3, Proposition 59, Note). In other words (Definition 40 of the Emotions; Definition 41 of the Emotions), the free man shows as much virtue when he avoids dangers as when he strives to overcome them. Q.E.D.

Corollary. The free man is as brave in a timely retreat as in combat; that is, a free man shows equal courage, or presence of mind, whether he chooses to give battle or to withdraw.

Note. What courage (animositas) is, and what I mean by it, I explained in Part 3, Proposition 59, Note. By danger I mean anything which can give rise to any evil, such as pain, hatred, discord and the like.

Proposition 70

The free man who lives among the ignorant strives as far as he can to avoid accepting favours from them.

Proof. Everyone judges what is good according to his own disposition (Part 3, Proposition 39, Note); so an ignorant man who has done another a kindness puts his own value on it, and will feel pain if he sees the receiver valuing it less highly (Part 3, Proposition 42). But the free man desires only to join other men to himself in friendship (Proposition 37 of this Part)—not repaying their kindnesses with others reckoned to be of equal value, but guiding himself and others by the free decision of reason, and doing only what he knows to be of first importance. Therefore the free man, so as not to become hateful to the ignorant, or to follow their desires rather than reason, will endeavour as far as he can to avoid accepting their favours.

Note. I say as far as he can. For though men are ignorant, they are still men, and in case of need could give us human help, the most excellent thing there is. So it is often necessary to accept favours from them, and consequently to repay such favours in kind; and we must therefore be careful in declining them, in case we seem to despise those who offer them, or to be unwilling out of meanness to return them, and so give offence by the very act of trying to avoid giving it. In declining favours, then, we must look to what is useful and what is courteous.

Proposition 71

Only free men are thoroughly grateful to one another.

Proof. Only free men are thoroughly useful to one another, and joined among themselves by the closest bond of friendship (Proposition 35 of this Part, Corollary 1); only such men endeavour, with a mutual eagerness of love, to do one another good (Proposition 37 of this Part); and therefore only they are thoroughly grateful to one another. Q.E.D.

Note. The goodwill which men led by blind desire have for one another is generally a bargain, or a bait, rather than pure goodwill. Moreover, ingratitude is not an emotion; yet it is base, since it generally shows that a man is affected by excessive hatred, anger, pride or avarice. A man who through mere folly does not know how to return kindnesses is not ungrateful; and much less so is a man who is not won over by a courtesan's gifts to serve her lust, or by a thief's to conceal his thefts, or by anything of the sort. On the contrary, such a man shows a constant mind, since no gifts can corrupt him to his own or the general harm.

Proposition 72

The free man never acts deceitfully, but always in good faith.

Proof. Suppose it is asked what a man should do in a case where he could free himself from the danger of immediate death by breaking his word: would not his plan of self-preservation persuade him outright to deceive? The answer is that if reason persuaded him to act so, it would persuade all men to act likewise; and in that case reason would persuade men not to agree in good faith to unite their forces or to hold laws in common—that is, not to have any general laws at all, which is absurd.

Proposition 73

A man guided by reason is more free in a state, where he lives under a common system of law, than in solitude, where he obeys no one.

Proof. A man guided by reason does not obey out of fear (Proposition 63 of this Part); but insofar as he endeavours to preserve his being according to the dictates of reason—that is (Proposition 66 of this Part, Note), insofar as he endeavours to live in freedom—he desires to order his life for the common good (Proposition 37 of this Part), and consequently, as we showed in Proposition 37 of this Part, Note 2, to live according to the laws of his country. Therefore the free man, in order to enjoy greater freedom, desires to hold the common rights of citizenship. Q.E.D.

Note. These and similar observations about man's true freedom may be referred to strength of character—that is, to courage and nobility of mind (Part 3, Proposition 59, Note). I do not think it worth while to prove separately all the properties of strength, still less to show that a man who is strong hates no one, is angry with no one, envies no one, is indignant with no one, despises no one, and least of all things is proud. These propositions, and all that concern the true way of life and religion, are easily proved from Proposition 37 of this Part and Proposition 46 of this Part—namely, that hatred should be overcome with love, and that every man should desire for others the good he seeks for himself. And we may repeat what we noted in the note to Proposition 50 of this Part, and elsewhere: that the strong man has always first in his thoughts that all things follow from the necessity of the divine nature, so that whatever he judges harmful and evil, and whatever accordingly seems to him impious, horrible, unjust and base, takes on that appearance from his own disordered, fragmentary and confused view of the universe. So before all things he strives to conceive things as they really are, and to remove the hindrances to true knowledge—hatred, anger, envy, derision, pride and the like, which I have mentioned above. And so he endeavours, as we said before, as far as he can, to do good and to go on his way rejoicing. How far human virtue can reach such a condition, and what its powers may be, I shall prove in the following Part.

Appendix

What I have said in this Part about the right way of life has not been arranged so as to be taken in at a glance, but has been set out piecemeal, as I thought each proposition could most readily be deduced from what came before it. I propose, therefore, to rearrange my remarks and bring them under leading heads.

1. All our endeavours, or desires, follow from the necessity of our nature in such a way that they can be understood either through it alone, as their proximate cause, or in virtue of our being a part of nature, which cannot be adequately conceived through itself without other individuals.

2. The desires which follow from our nature in such a way that they can be understood through it alone are those referred to the mind insofar as the mind is conceived as consisting of adequate ideas. The remaining desires are referred to the mind only insofar as it conceives things inadequately, and their force and increase are generally defined not by human power but by the power of things outside us. So the first are rightly called actions and the second passions; for the first always indicate our power, while the second show our weakness and our fragmentary knowledge.

3. Our actions—that is, the desires defined by a man's power, or reason—are always good; the rest may be either good or bad.

4. In life, then, it is before all things useful to perfect the understanding, or reason, as far as we can, and in this alone a man's highest happiness, or blessedness, consists. Indeed blessedness is nothing but the contentment of spirit which arises from the intuitive knowledge of God. And to perfect the understanding is nothing but to understand God, God's attributes, and the actions which follow from the necessity of his nature. So the ultimate aim of a man led by reason, his highest desire, by which he seeks to govern all the rest, is the one that brings him to an adequate conception of himself and of everything within the reach of his understanding.

5. Therefore without understanding there is no rational life; and things are good only insofar as they help a man to enjoy the life of the mind, which is defined by understanding. Conversely, whatever hinders a man from perfecting his reason and enjoying the rational life is alone called evil.

6. Since everything of which a man is the efficient cause is necessarily good, no evil can befall him except through external causes—namely, in virtue of his being a part of the whole of nature, whose laws human nature is compelled to obey and to conform to in almost infinite ways.

7. It is impossible that a man should not be a part of nature, or should not follow her general order. But if he finds himself among individuals whose nature agrees with his own, his power of acting will be helped and fed by them; whereas if he finds himself among those who agree with his nature very little, he will hardly be able to accommodate himself to them without undergoing a great change himself.

8. Whatever in nature we judge to be evil, or capable of injuring our capacity for existing and enjoying the rational life, we may endeavour to remove in whatever way seems safest to us; and conversely, whatever we judge good, or useful for preserving our being and enabling us to enjoy the rational life, we may take for our own use and employ as we think best. Everyone without exception may, by the sovereign right of nature, do whatever he thinks will further his own interest.

9. Nothing can agree more with the nature of a given thing than other individuals of the same species; therefore (compare Proposition 7 of this Part) there is nothing more useful to a man, for preserving his being and enjoying the rational life, than a fellow man who is led by reason. And since we know of nothing among individual things more excellent than a man led by reason, no one can better display the power of his skill and character than by so training men that they come at last to live under the rule of their own reason.

10. Insofar as men are moved by envy, or by any kind of hatred towards one another, they are at odds, and are to be feared in proportion as they are more powerful than their fellows.

11. Yet minds are conquered not by force but by love and high-mindedness.

12. It is before all things useful to men to join their ways of life, to bind themselves together with whatever bonds they think best fitted to gather them into one, and generally to do whatever serves to strengthen friendship.

13. But this needs skill and watchfulness. For men are various—since those who live under the guidance of reason are few—and are generally envious, and readier to take revenge than to feel compassion. So no small strength of character is needed to take every man as he is and to restrain oneself from imitating the emotions of others. But those who carp at mankind, who are more skilled in railing at vice than in instilling virtue, and who break men's characters rather than strengthen them, are harmful both to themselves and to others. So many, out of too great an impatience of spirit, or out of a misguided religious zeal, have preferred to live among beasts rather than among men—as boys and young men who cannot peaceably endure their parents' scolding will enlist as soldiers and choose the hardships of war and the despotism of discipline over the comforts of home and a father's advice, and will let any burden be laid on them so long as they can spite their parents.

14. So although men are generally governed in everything by their own appetites, their living together brings far more advantages than drawbacks. It is better, then, to bear patiently the wrongs they may do us, and to strive to promote whatever serves to bring about harmony and friendship.

15. The things which beget harmony are those which have to do with justice, fairness and honourable living. For men resent not only what is unjust or unfair but also what is thought disgraceful, and any slighting of the accepted customs of their society. And for winning love, those qualities are especially necessary which have to do with religion and piety (compare Proposition 37 of this Part, Note 1; Proposition 2 of this Part; Proposition 46 of this Part, Note; Proposition 73 of this Part, Note).

16. Harmony is also often the result of fear, but such harmony is insecure. Besides, fear arises from weakness of spirit and has nothing to do with the exercise of reason; and the same is true of compassion, though compassion seems to bear a certain likeness to piety.

17. Men are also won over by generosity, especially those who have not the means of buying what they need to live. But to give help to every poor man is far beyond the power and the advantage of any private person, whose wealth is wholly inadequate to meet such a call; and an individual man's resources of character are too limited for him to make all men his friends. So providing for the poor is a duty which falls on the state as a whole, and which looks only to the general advantage.

18. In accepting favours, and in returning gratitude, our duty must be wholly different (compare Proposition 70 of this Part, Note; Proposition 71 of this Part, Note).

19. Again, mercenary love—the lust of generation arising from bodily beauty—and in general every kind of love which has any cause but freedom of soul, readily passes into hatred; unless, what is worse, it is a kind of madness, in which case it promotes discord rather than harmony (compare Part 3, Proposition 31, Corollary).

20. As for marriage, it is certain that it agrees with reason if the desire for physical union is engendered not solely by bodily beauty but also by the desire to have children and to bring them up wisely, and if the love of both the man and the woman is caused not by bodily beauty alone but also by freedom of soul.

21. Furthermore, flattery begets harmony—but only by way of the vile fault of servility, or treachery. None are more readily caught by flattery than the proud, who wish to be first and are not.

22. In self-abasement there is a false show of piety and religion; and although self-abasement is the opposite of pride, a man who abases himself is very near akin to the proud (Proposition 57 of this Part, Note).

23. Shame too brings about harmony, but only in matters that cannot be hidden. And since shame is a kind of pain, it has nothing to do with the exercise of reason.

24. The remaining emotions of pain towards men are directly opposed to justice, fairness, honour, piety and religion; and although indignation seems to bear a certain likeness to fairness, life is lawless where every man may pass judgment on another's deeds and take it on himself to vindicate his own rights or another's.

25. Correctness of conduct (modestia)—the desire to please men which is determined by reason—belongs to piety (Proposition 37 of this Part, Note 1). But if it springs from emotion, it is ambition, the desire by which men, under a false cloak of piety, generally stir up discord and sedition. For a man who desires to help his fellows, in word or in deed, so that they may together enjoy the highest good, will before all things strive to win them over with love; not to draw them into admiration, so that a system may be called after his name, nor to give any cause for envy. And in conversation he will avoid talking of men's faults and will be careful to speak sparingly of human weakness, while dwelling at length on human virtue, or power, and on the way it may be perfected. So men will be stirred, not by fear or aversion, but only by the emotion of joy, to endeavour as far as they can to live under the guidance of reason.

26. Besides men, we know of no particular thing in nature whose mind we can take pleasure in, and which we can join to ourselves in friendship or any kind of fellowship. So a regard for our own advantage does not require us to preserve whatever else there is in nature besides man, but to preserve or destroy it according to its various uses, and to adapt it to our own use as best we can.

27. The advantage we get from things outside us—besides the experience and knowledge we acquire from observing them and from recombining their elements in different forms—is chiefly the preservation of the body. From this point of view, the most useful things are those which can feed and nourish the body so that all its parts may properly perform their functions; for the more the body is capable of being affected in a variety of ways, and of affecting external bodies in many ways, the more the mind is capable of thinking (Proposition 38 of this Part; Proposition 39 of this Part). But there seem to be very few things of this kind in nature, so for the proper nourishment of the body we must use many foods of different natures. For the human body is composed of a great many parts of different natures, which continually need varied nourishment, so that the whole body may be equally capable of everything that can follow from its own nature, and consequently so that the mind too may be equally capable of forming many perceptions.

28. Now for providing such nourishment the strength of each individual would hardly be enough if men did not help one another. But money has given us a token for everything; and so it is the idea of money that chiefly engrosses the mind of the multitude, which can hardly conceive any kind of pleasure that is not accompanied by the idea of money as its cause.

29. This is the fault only of those who seek money not out of poverty or to supply their necessary wants, but because they have learnt the arts of gain, by which they bring themselves to great splendour. They do feed their bodies, as custom requires, but sparingly, believing that they lose as much of their wealth as they spend on preserving their bodies. But those who know the true use of money, and who set the measure of wealth solely by their actual needs, live content with little.

30. Since, then, those things are good which help the various parts of the body to perform their functions, and since pleasure consists in an increase of, or help to, a man's power insofar as he is composed of mind and body, it follows that everything which brings pleasure is good. But since things do not act with the object of giving us pleasure, and their power of acting is not adjusted to suit our advantage, and since pleasure is generally referred to one part of the body more than to the rest, most emotions of pleasure—unless reason and watchfulness are at hand—and consequently the desires arising from them, may become excessive. We may add that emotion leads us to attend chiefly to what is agreeable in the present, and that we cannot estimate what is future with emotions equally vivid (Proposition 44 of this Part, Note; Proposition 60 of this Part, Note).

31. Superstition, on the other hand, seems to count as good everything that brings pain and as bad everything that brings pleasure. But as we said above (Proposition 45 of this Part, Note), none but the envious take delight in my weakness and my trouble. For the greater the pleasure we are affected with, the greater the perfection we pass to, and consequently the more we share in the divine nature; and no pleasure can ever be evil which is governed by a true regard for our own advantage. But conversely, a man who is led by fear and does good only to avoid evil is not guided by reason.

32. But human power is extremely limited and is infinitely surpassed by the power of external causes, so we have no absolute power of shaping the things outside us to our use. Nevertheless we shall bear with an equal mind everything that happens to us contrary to what our own advantage requires, so long as we are conscious that we have done our duty, and that the power we have was not enough to protect us completely, and so long as we remember that we are a part of the whole of nature and follow her order. If we understand this clearly and distinctly, that part of our nature which is defined by understanding—in other words, the better part of ourselves—will assuredly rest content in what befalls us, and will endeavour to persist in that contentment. For insofar as we are intelligent beings, we cannot desire anything but what is necessary, nor rest absolutely content in anything but what is true. So insofar as we understand these things rightly, the endeavour of the better part of ourselves is in harmony with the order of nature as a whole.

Part Five: Of the Power of the Understanding, or of Human Freedom

Preface, Axioms, Propositions 1 to 20

Preface

At last I come to the remaining part of my Ethics, which concerns the way that leads to freedom. Here I shall treat of the power of reason, showing how far reason can control the emotions, and what mental freedom, or blessedness, is; and we shall then be able to see how much more powerful the wise man is than the ignorant one. It is no part of my plan to point out the method and the means by which the understanding may be perfected, nor to show how the body may be tended so as to be capable of performing its functions properly. The second belongs to medicine, the first to logic. Here, I repeat, I shall treat only of the power of the mind, or of reason, and shall chiefly show the extent and nature of its dominion over the emotions, for their control and moderation. That we do not have absolute dominion over them I have already shown.

The Stoics, however, thought the emotions depended absolutely on our will and that we could govern them absolutely; yet the protest of experience, not their own principles, forced them to confess that no small practice and effort is needed to control and moderate them. Someone tried to illustrate this, if I remember rightly, by the example of two dogs, one a house-dog and the other a hunting-dog: by long training the house-dog could be brought to hunt, and the hunting-dog to stop chasing hares.

Descartes inclines strongly to this opinion. He held that the soul, or mind, is specially united to a particular part of the brain, the pineal gland, by whose help the mind can feel all the movements set going in the body, and external objects too, and which the mind can move in various ways by a simple act of will. He asserted that this gland is so suspended in the middle of the brain that it can be moved by the slightest motion of the animal spirits; that it is suspended there in as many different ways as the animal spirits can strike against it; and that as many different marks are impressed on it as there are external objects driving the animal spirits towards it. From which it follows that if the will of the soul suspends the gland in a position in which it has already been suspended once before by the animal spirits driven one way or another, the gland in its turn reacts on those spirits, driving and determining them to the state they were in when the gland was in that position before. He further asserted that every act of the will is united by nature to a particular motion of the gland. Whenever anyone wishes to look at a distant object, for instance, the act of will makes the pupil of the eye dilate; whereas if the person had merely thought of dilating the pupil, the wish to dilate it would not have brought the result about, since the motion of the gland which drives the animal spirits towards the optic nerve in a way that dilates or contracts the pupil is joined by nature not to the wish to dilate or contract the pupil but to the wish to look at distant or very near objects. And last, he held that although every motion of that gland seems to have been united by nature to one particular thought out of all our thoughts from the very beginning of our life, it can nevertheless become associated with other thoughts through habit; and this he tries to prove in the Passions de l'âme, I.50. From which he concludes that there is no soul so weak that it cannot, under proper direction, acquire absolute power over its passions. For passions, as he defines them, are "perceptions, or feelings, or disturbances of the soul, which are referred to the soul as species, and which"—mark the expression—"are produced, preserved, and strengthened through some movement of the spirits" (Passions de l'âme, I.27). And since we can join any motion of the gland, and consequently of the spirits, to any act of will, the determination of the will depends entirely on our own powers. So if we determine our will with sure and firm decisions in the direction we wish our actions to take, and associate the motions of the passions we wish to acquire with those decisions, we shall acquire absolute dominion over our passions.

Such is the doctrine of this illustrious philosopher, so far as I gather it from his own words; and if it were less ingenious I could hardly believe it came from so great a man. Indeed I am lost in wonder that a philosopher who had firmly asserted that he would draw no conclusions which do not follow from self-evident premisses, and would affirm nothing he did not clearly and distinctly perceive, and who had so often taken the schoolmen to task for trying to explain obscurities by occult qualities, could hold a hypothesis beside which occult qualities are commonplace. What does he understand, I ask, by the union of the mind and the body? What clear and distinct conception has he of thought in the most intimate union with a particle of extended matter? I should really like him to explain this union through its proximate cause. But he had so distinct a conception of mind as distinct from body that he could assign no particular cause of the union between them, or of the mind itself, and was obliged to fall back on the cause of the whole universe, that is, on God.

Further, I should much like to know what degree of motion the mind can impart to this pineal gland, and with what force it can hold it suspended. For I do not know whether the gland can be moved more slowly or more quickly by the mind than by the animal spirits, or whether the motions of the passions which we have closely united with firm decisions cannot be disjoined from them again by physical causes—in which case it would follow that although the mind firmly intended to face a given danger, and had united the motions of boldness to that decision, yet at the sight of the danger the gland might be suspended in a way which would leave the mind unable to think of anything but running away. In truth, as there is no common standard between will and motion, so there is no comparison possible between the powers of the mind and the power, or strength, of the body; and consequently the strength of the one cannot in any way be measured by the strength of the other. We may add that no gland is to be found in the middle of the brain so placed that it can be set in motion so easily and in so many ways, and that not all the nerves reach as far as the cavities of the brain. And last, I pass over everything he says about the will and its freedom, since I have abundantly proved that his premisses are false.

Therefore, since the power of the mind, as I have shown above, is defined by the understanding alone, we shall determine the remedies against the emotions solely by the knowledge of the mind—remedies which I believe everyone has experienced, though without observing them accurately or seeing them distinctly—and from the same foundation we shall deduce everything that concerns the mind's blessedness.

Axioms

1. If two contrary actions are started in the same subject, a change must necessarily take place in both, or in one of the two, and must continue until they cease to be contrary.

2. The power of an effect is defined by the power of its cause, insofar as its essence is explained, or defined, by the essence of its cause.

(Part 3, Proposition 7)

Proposition 1

Just as thoughts and the ideas of things are arranged and associated in the mind, so are the modifications of the body, or the images of things, arranged and associated in exactly the same way in the body.

Proof. The order and connection of ideas is the same (Part 2, Proposition 7) as the order and connection of things, and conversely the order and connection of things is the same (Part 2, Proposition 6, Corollary; Part 2, Proposition 7) as the order and connection of ideas. So just as the order and connection of ideas in the mind takes place according to the order and association of the modifications of the body (Part 2, Proposition 18), so conversely (Part 3, Proposition 2) the order and connection of the modifications of the body takes place according to the way in which thoughts and the ideas of things are arranged and associated in the mind. Q.E.D.

Proposition 2

If we detach a disturbance of the spirit, or emotion, from the thought of an external cause and join it to other thoughts, then the love or hatred towards that external cause, and the waverings of spirit that arise from those emotions, will be destroyed.

Proof. What constitutes the form of love or hatred is pleasure or pain accompanied by the idea of an external cause (Definition 6 of the Emotions; Definition 7 of the Emotions). So when this cause is removed, the form of love or hatred is removed with it, and these emotions, and those arising from them, are destroyed. Q.E.D.

Proposition 3

An emotion which is a passion ceases to be a passion as soon as we form a clear and distinct idea of it.

Proof. An emotion which is a passion is a confused idea (by the general Definition of the Emotions). So if we form a clear and distinct idea of a given emotion, that idea will be distinguished from the emotion only in thought, insofar as the emotion is referred to the mind alone (Part 2, Proposition 21, Note). Therefore (Part 3, Proposition 3) the emotion will cease to be a passion. Q.E.D.

Corollary. An emotion therefore comes more under our control, and the mind is less passive towards it, the better we know it.

Proposition 4

There is no modification of the body of which we cannot form some clear and distinct conception.

Proof. Properties common to all things can only be conceived adequately (Part 2, Proposition 38); therefore (Part 2, Proposition 12, and Lemma 2, which follows Part 2, Proposition 13) there is no modification of the body of which we cannot form some clear and distinct conception. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows that there is no emotion of which we cannot form some clear and distinct conception. For an emotion is the idea of a modification of the body (by the general Definition of the Emotions), and must therefore (by Proposition 4 of this Part) involve some clear and distinct conception.

Note. Since there is nothing from which some effect does not follow (Part 1, Proposition 36), and since we understand clearly and distinctly whatever follows from an idea which is adequate in us (Part 2, Proposition 40), it follows that everyone has the power of understanding himself and his emotions clearly and distinctly, if not absolutely then at least in part, and consequently of bringing it about that he is less subject to them. To reach this result, then, we must chiefly direct our efforts to acquiring, as far as we can, a clear and distinct knowledge of every emotion, so that the mind may through the emotion be determined to think of the things it clearly and distinctly perceives and rests fully content in; and so that the emotion itself may be separated from the thought of an external cause and associated with true thoughts. In this way not only will love, hatred and the rest be destroyed (Proposition 2 of this Part), but the appetites, or desires, which usually arise from such emotions will become incapable of being excessive (Part 4, Proposition 61). For it must be especially noted that the appetite through which a man is said to be active and the appetite through which he is said to be passive are one and the same. We have shown, for instance, that human nature is so constituted that everyone desires his fellow men to live as he does (Part 3, Proposition 31, Note); in a man who is not guided by reason this appetite is a passion called ambition, and does not differ much from pride, whereas in a man who lives by the dictates of reason it is an activity, or virtue, called piety (Part 4, Proposition 37, Note 1). In the same way all appetites, or desires, are passions only insofar as they spring from inadequate ideas, and the same results are credited to virtue when they are roused or generated by adequate ideas. For all the desires by which we are determined to a given action may arise from adequate ideas as much as from inadequate ones (Part 4, Proposition 59). And than this remedy for the emotions—to return to the point I started from—which consists in a true knowledge of them, no more excellent remedy within our power can be devised. For the mind has no power but that of thinking and of forming adequate ideas, as we showed above (Part 3, Proposition 3).

Proposition 5

An emotion towards a thing which we conceive simply, and not as necessary, or contingent, or possible, is, other things being equal, the greatest of all.

Proof. An emotion towards a thing we conceive to be free is greater than one towards a thing we conceive to be necessary (Part 3, Proposition 49), and consequently greater still than one towards what we conceive as possible or contingent (Part 4, Proposition 11). But to conceive a thing as free can be nothing but to conceive it simply, while we are ignorant of the causes by which it has been determined to act (Part 2, Proposition 35, Note). Therefore an emotion towards a thing we conceive simply is, other things being equal, greater than one we feel towards what is necessary, possible or contingent, and consequently is the greatest of all. Q.E.D.

Proposition 6

The mind has greater power over the emotions, and is less subject to them, insofar as it understands all things as necessary.

Proof. The mind understands all things to be necessary (Part 1, Proposition 29) and to be determined to exist and act by an infinite chain of causes. Therefore (by Proposition 5 of this Part) it so far brings it about that it is less subject to the emotions arising from those things, and (Part 3, Proposition 48) feels less emotion towards the things themselves. Q.E.D.

Note. The more this knowledge, that things are necessary, is applied to particular things, which we conceive more distinctly and vividly, the greater the mind's power over the emotions—as experience also bears witness. For we see that the pain arising from the loss of some good is eased as soon as the man who has lost it sees that it could not by any means have been kept. And we see too that no one pities an infant because it cannot speak, or walk, or reason, or because it passes so many years, as it were, unconscious; whereas if most people were born full-grown, and only one here and there an infant, everyone would pity the infants, because infancy would then be regarded not as a natural and necessary state but as a fault, or failing, in nature. And we may note several other instances of the same kind.

Proposition 7

Emotions which are roused by, or spring from, reason are, if we take account of time, stronger than those which have to do with particular objects we regard as absent.

Proof. We do not regard a thing as absent by reason of the emotion with which we conceive it, but by reason of the body being affected by another emotion which excludes that thing's existence (Part 2, Proposition 17). So the emotion referred to a thing we regard as absent is not of a kind to overcome the rest of a man's activity and power (Part 4, Proposition 6), but on the contrary is of a kind to be in some degree controlled by the emotions which exclude the existence of its external cause (Part 4, Proposition 9). But an emotion springing from reason is necessarily referred to the common properties of things (see Part 2, Proposition 40, Note 2), which we always regard as present—for there can be nothing to exclude their present existence—and which we always conceive in the same way (Part 2, Proposition 38). So an emotion of this kind always stays the same; and consequently (Axiom 1 of this Part) emotions contrary to it, which are not kept going by their external causes, will have to adapt themselves to it more and more until they are no longer contrary to it. To that extent the emotion springing from reason is the more powerful. Q.E.D.

Proposition 8

An emotion is stronger in proportion to the number of simultaneous causes which rouse it.

Proof. Many simultaneous causes are more powerful than a few (Part 3, Proposition 7); therefore (Part 4, Proposition 5), the more simultaneous causes rouse an emotion, the stronger it becomes. Q.E.D.

Note. This proposition is also evident from Axiom 2 of this Part.

Proposition 9

An emotion which has many and various causes, which the mind regards as simultaneous with the emotion itself, is less harmful; we are less subject to it, and less affected towards each of its causes, than we should be by a different and equally powerful emotion having fewer causes, or a single one.

Proof. An emotion is bad, or harmful, only insofar as it hinders the mind from thinking (Part 4, Proposition 26; Part 4, Proposition 27). So an emotion which determines the mind to contemplate several things at once is less harmful than an equally powerful emotion which so engrosses the mind in contemplating a few objects, or one, that it cannot think of anything else. That was our first point. Again, since the mind's essence—in other words, its power (Part 3, Proposition 7)—consists solely in thought (Part 2, Proposition 11), the mind is less passive towards an emotion which makes it think of several things at once than towards an equally strong emotion which keeps it engrossed in contemplating a few objects, or one. That was our second point. And last, this emotion (Part 3, Proposition 48), insofar as it has several causes, is less powerful in respect of each of them. Q.E.D.

Proposition 10

So long as we are not assailed by emotions contrary to our nature, we have the power of arranging and associating the modifications of our body according to the order of the intellect.

Proof. The emotions contrary to our nature—that is (Part 4, Proposition 30), the bad ones—are bad insofar as they hinder the mind from understanding (Part 4, Proposition 27). So long, therefore, as we are not assailed by emotions contrary to our nature, the mind's power of endeavouring to understand things (Part 4, Proposition 26) is not hindered, and so it is able to form clear and distinct ideas and to deduce them one from another (Part 2, Proposition 40, Note 2; Part 2, Proposition 47, Note). Consequently we have in such cases the power of arranging and associating the modifications of the body according to the order of the intellect. Q.E.D.

Note. By this power of rightly arranging and associating the modifications of the body we can keep ourselves from being easily affected by bad emotions. For (Proposition 7 of this Part) a greater force is needed to control the emotions when they are arranged and associated according to the order of the intellect than when they are uncertain and unsettled. The best we can do, then, so long as we do not have a perfect knowledge of our emotions, is to frame a rule of right conduct, or fixed practical precepts, to commit it to memory, and to apply it at once to the particular circumstances that meet us in life, so that our imagination may be thoroughly filled with it and it may always be ready to hand. For instance, we laid down among the rules of life (Part 4, Proposition 46, Note) that hatred should be overcome with love, or high-mindedness, and not repaid with hatred in return. Now for this precept of reason to be always ready to hand when we need it, we should often think over the wrongs men commonly commit, and consider how and by what means they may best be warded off by high-mindedness; and so we shall associate the idea of a wrong with the idea of this precept, which will then always be at hand when a wrong is done to us (Part 2, Proposition 18). And if we also keep in readiness the idea of our true advantage, and of the good which follows from mutual friendship and common fellowship, and if we remember that complete contentment is the result of the right way of life (Part 4, Proposition 52), and that men, no less than everything else, act by the necessity of their nature—then, I say, the wrong, and the hatred which commonly arises from it, will take up a very small part of our imagination and will easily be overcome. Or if the anger springing from a grievous wrong is not easily overcome, it will nevertheless be overcome, though not without a struggle, far sooner than if we had not reflected on the matter beforehand—as is evident from Proposition 6 of this Part, Proposition 7 of this Part and Proposition 8 of this Part. In the same way we should reflect on courage as a means of overcoming fear: the ordinary dangers of life should often be brought to mind and imagined, together with the means by which presence of mind and strength of character can avoid and overcome them.

But we must note that in arranging our thoughts and ideas we should always keep in view what is good in every individual thing (Part 4, Proposition 63, Corollary; Part 3, Proposition 59), so that we may always be determined to act by an emotion of pleasure. If a man sees, for instance, that he pursues honour too keenly, let him think of its right use, of the end for which it should be pursued, and of the means of attaining it. Let him not think of its misuse, and its emptiness, and the fickleness of mankind, and the like—which no man thinks of except from a sickness of disposition. It is with thoughts like these that the most ambitious torment themselves most, when they despair of gaining the distinctions they hanker after, and in venting their anger would like to appear wise. So it is certain that those who cry out loudest against the misuse of honour and the vanity of the world are the very men who covet it most greedily. This is not peculiar to the ambitious but is common to all who are ill used by fortune and weak in spirit. A poor man who is miserly will talk endlessly about the misuse of wealth and the vices of the rich, and by doing so merely torments himself and shows the world that he cannot bear his own poverty, nor other people's riches either. And so too those who have been ill received by a woman they love think of nothing but the inconstancy and treachery and other stock faults of women—all of which they forget the moment their sweetheart takes them into favour again. So a man who wishes to govern his emotions and appetites by the love of freedom alone will strive as far as he can to gain a knowledge of the virtues and their causes, and to fill his spirit with the joy that arises from truly knowing them. He will not wish to dwell on men's faults, or to carp at his fellows, or to revel in a false show of freedom. And whoever diligently observes and practises these precepts—which are not difficult—will in a short time be able, for the most part, to direct his actions by the commands of reason.

Footnote: Continuo. Rendered "constantly" by Mr. Pollock on the ground that the classical meaning of the word does not suit the context. I venture to think, however, that a tolerable sense may be obtained without doing violence to Spinoza's scholarship.

Proposition 11

The more objects a mental image is referred to, the more frequent it is, or the more often vivid, and the more it occupies the mind.

Proof. The more objects a mental image, or an emotion, is referred to, the more causes there are by which it can be roused and fed, all of which (by hypothesis) the mind contemplates at once along with the emotion. So the emotion is the more frequent, or is more often in full vigour, and (Proposition 8 of this Part) occupies the mind the more. Q.E.D.

Proposition 12

The mental images of things are more easily associated with images referred to things we clearly and distinctly understand than with others.

Proof. The things we clearly and distinctly understand are either the common properties of things or deductions from them (see Part 2, Proposition 40, Note 2), and are consequently (by Proposition 11 of this Part) more often roused in us. So it may more readily happen that we contemplate other things along with these than along with something else, and consequently (Part 2, Proposition 18) that the images of those things are more often associated with the images of these than with the images of something else. Q.E.D.

Proposition 13

A mental image is more often vivid the more other images it is associated with.

Proof. The more other images an image is associated with, the more causes there are (Part 2, Proposition 18) by which it can be roused. Q.E.D.

Proposition 14

The mind can bring it about that all the modifications of the body, or images of things, are referred to the idea of God.

Proof. There is no modification of the body of which the mind cannot form some clear and distinct conception (Proposition 4 of this Part); so it can bring it about that they are all referred to the idea of God (Part 1, Proposition 15). Q.E.D.

Proposition 15

He who clearly and distinctly understands himself and his emotions loves God, and the more so the more he understands himself and his emotions.

Proof. He who clearly and distinctly understands himself and his emotions feels pleasure (Part 3, Proposition 53), and this pleasure is (by Proposition 14 of this Part) accompanied by the idea of God. Therefore (Definition 6 of the Emotions) such a man loves God, and for the same reason loves him the more the more he understands himself and his emotions. Q.E.D.

Proposition 16

This love towards God must hold the chief place in the mind.

Proof. For this love is associated with all the modifications of the body (Proposition 14 of this Part) and is fed by them all (Proposition 15 of this Part); therefore (Proposition 11 of this Part) it must hold the chief place in the mind. Q.E.D.

Proposition 17

God is without passions, and is not affected by any emotion of pleasure or pain.

Proof. All ideas, insofar as they are referred to God, are true (Part 2, Proposition 32)—that is (Part 2, Definition 4), adequate; and therefore (by the general Definition of the Emotions) God is without passions. Again, God cannot pass to a greater or a lesser perfection (Part 1, Proposition 20, Corollary 2); therefore (by Definition 2 of the Emotions; Definition 3 of the Emotions) he is not affected by any emotion of pleasure or pain.

Corollary. Strictly speaking, God neither loves nor hates anyone. For God (by Proposition 17 of this Part) is not affected by any emotion of pleasure or pain, and consequently (Definition 6 of the Emotions; Definition 7 of the Emotions) neither loves nor hates anyone.

Proposition 18

No one can hate God.

Proof. The idea of God which is in us is adequate and perfect (Part 2, Proposition 46; Part 2, Proposition 47); so insofar as we contemplate God we are active (Part 3, Proposition 3), and consequently (Part 3, Proposition 59) there can be no pain accompanied by the idea of God—in other words (Definition 7 of the Emotions), no one can hate God. Q.E.D.

Corollary. Love towards God cannot be turned into hatred.

Note. It may be objected that since we understand God as the cause of all things, we are for that very reason regarding God as the cause of pain. But I answer that insofar as we understand the causes of pain, pain to that extent (Proposition 3 of this Part) ceases to be a passion—that is, it ceases to be pain (Part 3, Proposition 59). So insofar as we understand God to be the cause of pain, we to that extent feel pleasure.

Proposition 19

He who loves God cannot endeavour that God should love him in return.

Proof. If a man endeavoured that, he would be desiring (Proposition 17 of this Part, Corollary) that God, whom he loves, should not be God, and consequently would be desiring to feel pain (Part 3, Proposition 19), which is absurd (Part 3, Proposition 28). Therefore he who loves God, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 20

This love towards God cannot be spoiled by the emotion of envy or of jealousy; on the contrary, it is fed the more the more men we conceive to be joined to God by the same bond of love.

Proof. This love towards God is the highest good we can seek under the guidance of reason (Part 4, Proposition 28); it is common to all men (Part 4, Proposition 36); and we desire that all should rejoice in it (Part 4, Proposition 37). Therefore (Definition 23 of the Emotions) it cannot be spoiled by the emotion of envy, nor by the emotion of jealousy (Proposition 18 of this Part; Part 3, Proposition 35, Note); but on the contrary must be fed the more, the more men we conceive to rejoice in it. Q.E.D.

Note. We can show in the same way that there is no emotion directly contrary to this love by which it could be destroyed. So we may conclude that this love towards God is the most constant of all the emotions, and that insofar as it is referred to the body it cannot be destroyed unless the body is destroyed too. What its nature is insofar as it is referred to the mind alone, we shall inquire presently.

I have now gone through all the remedies against the emotions—all that the mind, considered in itself alone, can do against them. From which it appears that the mind's power over the emotions consists:

1. In the actual knowledge of the emotions (Proposition 4 of this Part, Note).

2. In its separating the emotions from the thought of an external cause, which we conceive confusedly (Proposition 2 of this Part; Proposition 4 of this Part, Note).

3. In the fact that, in respect of time, the emotions referred to things we distinctly understand surpass those referred to what we conceive confusedly and in a fragmentary way (Proposition 7 of this Part).

4. In the number of causes by which the modifications are fed that have to do with the common properties of things, or with God (Proposition 9 of this Part; Proposition 11 of this Part).

Footnote: Affectiones. Camerer reads affectus—emotions.

5. And last, in the order in which the mind can arrange and associate its own emotions one with another (Proposition 10 of this Part, Note; Proposition 12 of this Part; Proposition 13 of this Part; Proposition 14 of this Part).

But so that this power of the mind over the emotions may be better understood, it should be specially noted that we call emotions strong when we compare one man's emotion with another's and see that one man is more troubled than another by the same emotion, or when we compare the various emotions of the same man and find that he is more affected, or stirred, by one than by another. For the strength of every emotion is defined by a comparison of our own power with the power of an external cause. Now the power of the mind is defined by knowledge alone, and its weakness, or passivity, by the privation of knowledge alone. It follows, then, that the most passive mind is the one whose greatest part is made up of inadequate ideas, so that it may be characterised more readily by its passive states than by its activities; while the most active mind is the one whose greatest part is made up of adequate ideas, so that although it may contain as many inadequate ideas as the first, it can still be more easily characterised by the ideas that belong to human virtue than by those that tell of human weakness. Again, it must be noted that unhealthiness of spirit, and misfortunes, can generally be traced to an excessive love for something subject to many changes, which we can never become masters of. For no one is anxious or worried about anything unless he loves it, and wrongs, suspicions and enmities arise only in regard to things no one can really be master of.

We may thus readily conceive the power that clear and distinct knowledge has over the emotions, and especially that third kind of knowledge (Part 2, Proposition 47, Note) which is founded on the actual knowledge of God. If it does not absolutely destroy them insofar as they are passions (Proposition 3 of this Part; Proposition 4 of this Part, Note), it at any rate makes them occupy a very small part of the mind (Proposition 14 of this Part). And further, it begets a love towards a thing unchangeable and eternal (Proposition 15 of this Part), which we may really come into possession of (Part 2, Proposition 45), and which cannot be defiled with the faults inherent in ordinary love, but may grow from strength to strength, and may take up the greater part of the mind and penetrate it deeply.

And now I have finished with everything that concerns this present life. For, as I said at the beginning of this note, I have briefly described all the remedies against the emotions; and anyone may readily have seen this for himself, if he has attended to what has been advanced in this note, and to the definitions of the mind and its emotions, and lastly, to Part 3, Proposition 1 and Part 3, Proposition 3. It is now time, therefore, to pass on to what concerns the duration of the mind, without relation to the body.

Propositions 21 to 42

Proposition 21

The mind can imagine nothing, and can remember nothing past, except while the body endures.

Proof. The mind does not express the actual existence of its body, nor does it imagine the modifications of the body as actual, except while the body endures (Part 2, Proposition 8, Corollary); and consequently (Part 2, Proposition 26) it does not imagine any body as actually existing except while its own body endures. So it cannot imagine anything (Part 2, Proposition 17, Note), or remember things past, except while the body endures (see Part 2, Proposition 18, Note). Q.E.D.

Proposition 22

Nevertheless there is necessarily in God an idea which expresses the essence of this or that human body under the form of eternity.

Proof. God is the cause not only of the existence of this or that human body but of its essence too (Part 1, Proposition 25). That essence must therefore be conceived through the very essence of God (Part 1, Axiom 4), and be so conceived by a certain eternal necessity (Part 1, Proposition 16); and this conception must necessarily exist in God (Part 2, Proposition 3). Q.E.D.

Proposition 23

The human mind cannot be absolutely destroyed with the body; something of it remains which is eternal.

Proof. There is necessarily in God a concept, or idea, which expresses the essence of the human body (Proposition 22 of this Part), and which is therefore necessarily something belonging to the essence of the human mind (Part 2, Proposition 13). But we have not assigned to the human mind any duration definable by time, except insofar as it expresses the actual existence of the body, which is explained through duration and can be defined by time—that is (Part 2, Proposition 8, Corollary), we assign it duration only while the body endures. Yet there is nevertheless something which is conceived by a certain eternal necessity through the very essence of God (Proposition 22 of this Part); and this something, which belongs to the essence of the mind, will necessarily be eternal. Q.E.D.

Note. This idea, which expresses the essence of the body under the form of eternity, is, as we have said, a certain mode of thinking which belongs to the essence of the mind and is necessarily eternal. Yet it is not possible for us to remember that we existed before our body, since our body can bear no trace of such an existence, and since eternity cannot be defined in terms of time or have any relation to time. But, notwithstanding, we feel and know that we are eternal. For the mind feels the things it conceives by understanding no less than the things it remembers; the eyes of the mind, by which it sees and observes things, are proofs. So although we do not remember that we existed before the body, we feel that our mind, insofar as it involves the essence of the body under the form of eternity, is eternal, and that its existence therefore cannot be defined in terms of time or explained through duration. Our mind can be said to endure, and its existence can be defined by a fixed time, only insofar as it involves the actual existence of the body; and only to that extent has it the power of determining the existence of things by time and conceiving them under the head of duration.

Proposition 24

The more we understand particular things, the more we understand God.

Proof. This is evident from Part 1, Proposition 25, Corollary.

Proposition 25

The highest endeavour of the mind, and its highest virtue, is to understand things by the third kind of knowledge.

Proof. The third kind of knowledge proceeds from an adequate idea of certain attributes of God to an adequate knowledge of the essence of things (see Part 2, Proposition 40, Note 2); and the more we understand things in this way, the better we understand God (by Proposition 24 of this Part). Therefore (Part 4, Proposition 28) the highest virtue of the mind—that is (Part 4, Definition 8), its power, or nature, or (Part 3, Proposition 7) its highest endeavour—is to understand things by the third kind of knowledge. Q.E.D.

Proposition 26

The more capable the mind is of understanding things by the third kind of knowledge, the more it desires to understand things by that kind.

Proof. This is evident. For insofar as we conceive the mind to be capable of conceiving things by this kind of knowledge, we conceive it to that extent as determined so to conceive them; and consequently (Definition 1 of the Emotions) the mind desires to do so in proportion as it is more capable of it. Q.E.D.

Proposition 27

From this third kind of knowledge arises the highest contentment of mind there can be.

Proof. The highest virtue of the mind is to know God (Part 4, Proposition 28), or to understand things by the third kind of knowledge (Proposition 25 of this Part); and this virtue is the greater the more the mind knows things by that kind of knowledge (Proposition 24 of this Part). So a man who knows things by this kind of knowledge passes to the summit of human perfection, and is therefore (Definition 2 of the Emotions) affected with the highest pleasure, accompanied by the idea of himself and of his own virtue. Thus (Definition 25 of the Emotions) from this kind of knowledge arises the highest contentment there can be. Q.E.D.

Proposition 28

The endeavour, or desire, to know things by the third kind of knowledge cannot arise from the first kind, but from the second.

Proof. This proposition is self-evident. For whatever we understand clearly and distinctly, we understand either through itself or through something conceived through itself. That is, ideas which are clear and distinct in us, or which are referred to the third kind of knowledge (Part 2, Proposition 40, Note 2), cannot follow from ideas that are fragmentary and confused and belong to the first kind, but must follow from adequate ideas, that is, from ideas of the second and third kinds. Therefore (Definition 1 of the Emotions) the desire to know things by the third kind of knowledge cannot arise from the first kind, but from the second. Q.E.D.

Proposition 29

Whatever the mind understands under the form of eternity, it understands not by conceiving the present actual existence of the body, but by conceiving the essence of the body under the form of eternity.

Proof. Insofar as the mind conceives the present existence of its body, it conceives to that extent a duration which can be determined by time, and only to that extent has it the power of conceiving things in relation to time (Proposition 21 of this Part; Part 2, Proposition 26). But eternity cannot be explained in terms of duration (Part 1, Definition 8, Explanation). So to that extent the mind has not the power of conceiving things under the form of eternity. It does have that power, however, because it is of the nature of reason to conceive things under the form of eternity (Part 2, Proposition 44, Corollary 2), and because it is of the nature of the mind to conceive the essence of the body under the form of eternity (Proposition 23 of this Part); and besides these two there is nothing belonging to the essence of the mind (Part 2, Proposition 13). Therefore this power of conceiving things under the form of eternity belongs to the mind only in virtue of its conceiving the essence of the body under the form of eternity. Q.E.D.

Note. We conceive things as actual in two ways: either as existing in relation to a given time and place, or as contained in God and following from the necessity of the divine nature. Whatever we conceive in this second way as true, or real, we conceive under the form of eternity, and its ideas involve the eternal and infinite essence of God, as we showed in Part 2, Proposition 45, Note, which see.

Proposition 30

Our mind, insofar as it knows itself and the body under the form of eternity, necessarily has to that extent a knowledge of God, and knows that it is in God and is conceived through God.

Proof. Eternity is the very essence of God, insofar as that essence involves necessary existence (Part 1, Definition 8). So to conceive things under the form of eternity is to conceive them insofar as they are conceived through the essence of God as real beings, or insofar as they involve existence through the essence of God. Therefore our mind, insofar as it conceives itself and the body under the form of eternity, necessarily has to that extent a knowledge of God, and knows, and so on. Q.E.D.

Proposition 31

The third kind of knowledge depends on the mind as its formal cause, insofar as the mind is itself eternal.

Proof. The mind conceives nothing under the form of eternity except insofar as it conceives its own body under the form of eternity (Proposition 29 of this Part)—that is, except insofar as it is itself eternal (Proposition 21 of this Part; Proposition 23 of this Part). Therefore (by Proposition 30 of this Part), insofar as it is eternal, it has the knowledge of God, which is necessarily adequate (Part 2, Proposition 46); and so the mind, insofar as it is eternal, is capable of knowing everything that can follow from this given knowledge of God (Part 2, Proposition 40)—in other words, of knowing things by the third kind of knowledge (see Part 2, Proposition 40, Note 2), of which accordingly the mind (Part 3, Definition 1), insofar as it is eternal, is the adequate, or formal, cause. Q.E.D.

Note. The more powerful a man is in this kind of knowledge, then, the more fully conscious he is of himself and of God—in other words, the more perfect and blessed he is, as will appear more clearly in what follows. But we must note here that although we are already certain that the mind is eternal insofar as it conceives things under the form of eternity, yet, so that what we wish to show may be more easily explained and better understood, we shall consider the mind as though it had just begun to exist and to understand things under the form of eternity, as indeed we have done so far. We may do this without any danger of error, so long as we are careful to draw no conclusion unless our premisses are plain.

Proposition 32

Whatever we understand by the third kind of knowledge we take delight in, and our delight is accompanied by the idea of God as its cause.

Proof. From this kind of knowledge arises the highest contentment of mind there can be, that is (Definition 25 of the Emotions), pleasure; and this contentment is accompanied by the idea of the mind itself (Proposition 27 of this Part), and consequently (Proposition 30 of this Part) by the idea of God as its cause too. Q.E.D.

Corollary. From the third kind of knowledge there necessarily arises the intellectual love of God. From this kind of knowledge arises pleasure accompanied by the idea of God as its cause—that is (Definition 6 of the Emotions), the love of God; not insofar as we imagine him as present (Proposition 29 of this Part), but insofar as we understand him to be eternal. This is what I call the intellectual love of God.

Proposition 33

The intellectual love of God which arises from the third kind of knowledge is eternal.

Proof. The third kind of knowledge is eternal (Proposition 31 of this Part; Part 1, Axiom 3); therefore (by Part 1, Axiom 3) the love which arises from it is also necessarily eternal. Q.E.D.

Note. Although this love towards God has (by Proposition 33 of this Part) no beginning, it nevertheless has all the perfections of love, just as if it had arisen as we supposed in Proposition 32 of this Part, Corollary. There is no difference here, except that the mind possesses as eternal the very perfections we imagined accruing to it, and that they are accompanied by the idea of God as their eternal cause. If pleasure consists in the transition to a greater perfection, then blessedness must certainly consist in the mind's being endowed with perfection itself.

Proposition 34

The mind is subject to the emotions which belong to the passions only while the body endures.

Proof. Imagining is the idea by which the mind contemplates a thing as present (Part 2, Proposition 17, Note), and this idea indicates the present disposition of the human body rather than the nature of the external thing (Part 2, Proposition 16, Corollary 2). So an emotion (see the general Definition of the Emotions) is an act of imagining, insofar as it indicates the present disposition of the body; and therefore (Proposition 21 of this Part) the mind is subject to the emotions belonging to the passions only while the body endures. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows that no love is eternal except intellectual love.

Note. If we look at men's common opinion, we shall see that they are indeed conscious of the eternity of their mind, but that they confuse eternity with duration, and ascribe it to the imagination, or the memory, which they believe remains after death.

Proposition 35

God loves himself with an infinite intellectual love.

Proof. God is absolutely infinite (Part 1, Definition 6); that is (Part 2, Definition 6), the nature of God rejoices in infinite perfection, and such rejoicing is (Part 2, Proposition 3) accompanied by the idea of himself—that is (Part 1, Proposition 11; Part 1, Definition 1), by the idea of his own cause. And this is what we have (in Proposition 32 of this Part, Corollary) described as intellectual love.

Proposition 36

The mind's intellectual love towards God is that very love of God by which God loves himself—not insofar as he is infinite, but insofar as he can be explained through the essence of the human mind regarded under the form of eternity. In other words, the mind's intellectual love towards God is part of the infinite love with which God loves himself.

Proof. This love of the mind must be referred to the mind's activities (Proposition 32 of this Part, Corollary; Part 3, Proposition 3); it is indeed itself an activity by which the mind regards itself, accompanied by the idea of God as cause (Proposition 32 of this Part, Corollary)—that is (Part 1, Proposition 25, Corollary; Part 2, Proposition 11, Corollary), an activity by which God, insofar as he can be explained through the human mind, regards himself accompanied by the idea of himself. Therefore (by Proposition 35 of this Part) this love of the mind is part of the infinite love with which God loves himself. Q.E.D.

Corollary. It follows that God, insofar as he loves himself, loves men; and consequently that God's love towards men and the mind's intellectual love towards God are one and the same.

Note. From this we clearly understand what our salvation, or blessedness, or freedom consists in: namely, in a constant and eternal love towards God, or in God's love towards men. This love, or blessedness, is called Glory in the Bible, and not undeservedly. For whether it is referred to God or to the mind, it may rightly be called contentment of spirit, which (Definition 25 of the Emotions; Definition 30 of the Emotions) is not really distinct from glory. Insofar as it is referred to God, it is (Proposition 35 of this Part) pleasure, if we may still use that word, accompanied by the idea of itself; and insofar as it is referred to the mind, it is the same (Proposition 27 of this Part).
